A N C I E N T H I S T O RY

## OF MEXICO

## BOOK I

Description of the country of Anahuac, or a brief account of the land, the climate, the mountains, the rivers, the lakes, the minerals, the plants, the animals,

and the men of the kingdom of Mexico.

he name of Anahuac, which was at first given to the valley

T         of Mexico alone, because its principal cities had been

founded on the islets and on the shores of two lakes, later took on a wider meaning and was used to denote almost the whole of that great tract of land which is now known by the name of New Spain. (a)

§ I.

This vast country was then divided into the kingdoms of Mexico, Division of the counAcolhuacan, Tlacopan, and Michoacán, into the republics of Tlaxcala, try of Anahuac. Cholula, and Huexotzinco, and into several other particular states.

D2                          The

(a) Anahuac means near the water, and from it the name Anahuatlaca, or Nahuatlaca, seems to be derived, by which the civilized nations that occupied the shores of the Mexican lake were known.

[p. 28]

The kingdom of Michoacán, the most western of all, bordered on Bk. I.

the east and south on the dominions of the Mexicans, on the north

on the country of the Chichimecs and of other, more barbarous na‐

tions, and on the west on Lake Chapala and some independent states.

The capital, Tzintzuntzan, called by the Mexicans Huitzitzilla, was sit‐

uated on the eastern shore of the beautiful Lake Pátzcuaro. Besides

these two cities there were others of great note, such as those of

Tiripetío, Zacapu, and Tarecuato: all this country was pleasant, rich, and

well peopled.

The kingdom of Tlacopan, situated between those of Mexico and

Michoacán, was of so small an extent that, outside the capital of the

same name, it comprised nothing else than a few cities of the

Tepanec nation and the villages of the Mazahuas situated in the west‐

ern mountains of the Mexican valley. The court of Tlacopan was on

the western shore of Lake Texcoco, four miles west of that of Mexico.

(b)

The kingdom of Acolhuacan, the most ancient, and at one time

the most extensive, was afterward reduced to narrower limits by the

conquests of the Mexicans. It bordered on the east on the republic of

Tlaxcala, on the south on the province of Chalco, belonging to the

kingdom of Mexico, on the north on the country of the Huastecs,

and on the west it ended at Lake Texcoco; and it was likewise

hemmed in by several states of Mexico. Its length from south to

north was a little more than two hundred miles, and its greatest

breadth did not exceed sixty; but in so small a district there were very

large cities and very numerous peoples. The court of Texcoco, situated

on the eastern shore of the lake of the same name, fifteen miles east

of that of Mexico, was justly celebrated no less for its antiquity and

grandeur

than

(b) The Spaniards, altering the Mexican names or adapting them to their own lan‐

guage, say Tacuba, Oculma, Otumba, Guaxuta, Tepeaca, Guatemala, Churubusco, etc., in‐

stead of Tlacopan, Acolman, Otompan, Huexotla, Tepejacac, Quauhtemallan, and

Huitzilopochco; and this example we shall imitate as far as is fitting, in order to spare

readers the difficulty of pronouncing them. [p. 29] than for the civilization and refinement of its inhabitants. The three cities of Huexotla, Coatlichan, and Atenco were so near to it that they Bk. I. could be considered as so many suburbs. That of Otompan was of great note, as were also those of Acolman and of Tepepulco.

The celebrated republic of Tlaxcallan, or Tlascalla, bordered on the west on the kingdom of Acolhuacan, on the south on the republics of Cholula and of Huexotzinco, and on the state of Tepeyacac, which belonged to the crown of Mexico, on the north on the state of Zacatlán, and on the east on other states likewise subject to the same crown. Its length did not reach fifty miles, nor its breadth more than thirty. Tlaxcallan, the capital, from which the republic took its name, was situated on the slope of the great mountain Matlalcueye, to the northwest, and about seventy miles east of the Mexican court.

The kingdom of Mexico, though the most recent, nevertheless had a much greater extent than all the other aforesaid kingdoms and republics taken together. It extended to the southwest and south as far as the Pacific Ocean, to the southeast as far as the neighborhood of Cuauhtemallan, to the east, excepting the districts of the three republics and a small part of the kingdom of Acolhuacan, as far as the Gulf of Mexico, and to the north as far as the country of the Huastecs; to the northwest it bordered on the barbarous Chichimecs, and to the west it was confined by the dominions of Tlacopan and of Michoacán. The whole Mexican kingdom was comprised between 14° and 21° of north latitude, and between 271° and 283° of longitude, reckoned from the meridian of the island of Hierro. (c)

The noblest portion of this land, as much with regard to its advantageous situation as to its population, was the

valley

(c) Solís and other authors, Spanish as well as French and English, give a much greater extent to the kingdom of Mexico, and Dr. Robertson says that “the territories belonging to the chiefs of Tezeuco and Tacuba, were hardly inferior in extent to those of the Mexican monarch”; but how far these authors are from the truth we shall show in our Dissertations. [p. 30] valley of Mexico itself, crowned by beautiful and verdant mountains, Bk. I.

whose circumference, measured along the lower part of the moun‐

tains, is more than 120 miles. A good part of the valley is occupied by

two lakes, the one upper, of fresh water, and the other lower, of

brackish water, which communicate with each other by a good chan‐

nel. Into the lower lake, because it lay in the lowest part of the valley,

all the waters flowing down from the mountains used to run together;

hence, whenever through the extraordinary abundance of the rains

the water rose above the bed of the lake, it easily flooded the city of

Mexico, founded in that same lake, as was seen to happen no less un‐

der the dominion of the Mexican monarchs than under that of the

Spaniards. These two lakes, whose circumference was not less than

ninety miles, represented in some manner the figure of a camel,

whose head and neck were the fresh-water lake, or that of Chalco,

the body the brackish lake, called that of Texcoco, and the legs and

feet the streams and torrents that ran down from the mountains to

the lake. Between the two lakes lies the small peninsula of

Iztapalapan, which separates them. Besides the three courts of

Mexico, Acolhuacan, and Tlacopan, there were in this delightful val‐

ley forty other considerable cities and innumerable villages and ham‐

lets. The largest cities after the courts were those of Xochimilco,

Chalco, Iztapalapan, and Cuauhtitlan, which today scarcely preserve a

twentieth part of what they then were. (d)

Mexico, the most renowned of all the cities of the New World, and

capital of the empire (the description of which we shall give in anoth‐

er place), was built, after the fashion of Venice, on several islets of

Lake Texcoco, in 19° and almost 26′

(d) The other notable cities of the Mexican valley were those of Mixquic,

Cuitláhuac, Azcapotzalco, Tenayuca, Otompan, Colhuacan, Mexicaltzinco, Huitzilopochco,

Coyoacán, Atenco, Coatlichan, Huexotla, Chiautla, Acolman, Teotihuacan, Itztapaloccan,

Tepetlaoztoc, Tepepulco, Tizayuca, Citlaltepec, Coyotepec, Zumpango, Tultitlán, Xaltocan,

Tetepanco, Ecatepec, Tequixquiac, Huipochtlan, Tepotzotlán, Tehuilloyoccan, Huehuetoca,

Atlacuihuayan, etc. See our Dissertation VI. [p. 31] of north latitude, and in 276° 34′ of longitude, between the two

Bk. I. courts of Texcoco and Tlacopan, fifteen miles to the west of the former, and four to the east of the other. Of its provinces some were inland and others maritime.

The principal inland provinces were: to the north, that of the § 2.

Provinces of the Otomis; to the west and southwest, those of the Matlatzincas and the kingdom of Mexico. Cuitlatecs; to the south, those of the Tlahuicas and the Cohuixcas; to the southeast, after the states of Itzocan, Yauhtepec, Cuauhquechollan, Atlixco, Tehuacán, and others, the great provinces of the Mixtecs, the Zapotecs, and finally those of the Chiapanecs. To the east were the provinces of Tepeyacac, of the Popolocas, and of the Totonacs. The maritime provinces of the Gulf of Mexico were those of Coatzacoalco and of Cuetlachtlan, which the Spaniards call Cotasta. The provinces of the Pacific Ocean were those of Colima, Zacatula, Tototepec, Tehuantepec, and Soconusco.

The province of the Otomis began in the northern part of the Mexican valley and continued northward through those mountains as far as ninety miles from the capital. Above all the inhabited places, of which there were very many, there rose the ancient and celebrated city of Tollan (today Tula), and that of Jilotepec, which after the conquest made by the Spaniards was the metropolis of the Otomi nation. Beyond the places of this nation toward the north and northwest there were no other inhabited places as far as New Mexico. All this great tract of land, which is of more than a thousand miles, was occupied by barbarous nations, which had neither a fixed domicile nor obedience to any sovereign.

The province of the Matlatzincas comprised, besides the valley of Toluca, all the space that lies from there to Tlaximaloyan (today Taximaroa), the frontier of the kingdom of Michoacán. The fertile valley of Toluca is more than forty miles long from southeast to northwest, and as much as thirty wide where it broadens most. Toluca, which was the principal city

of [p. 32] of the Matlatzincas, from which the valley took its name, was, as it Bk. I. still is, situated at the foot of a lofty mountain perpetually crowned

with snow, thirty miles from Mexico. All the other places of the valley

were inhabited partly by the Matlatzincas and partly by the Otomis.

In the surrounding mountains there were the states of Xalatlauhco, of

Tzompahuacan, and of Malinalco; at no great distance to the east of the

valley, that of Ocuilan, and to the west those of Tozantla and of

Zoltepec.

The Cuitlatecs inhabited a country that stretched more than two

hundred miles from northwest to southeast, from the kingdom of

Michoacán as far as the Pacific Ocean. Their capital was the great

and populous city of Mexcaltepec on the coast, of which scarcely the

ruins remain.

The capital of the Tlahuicas was the pleasant and strong city of

Quauhnahuac, called by the Spaniards Cuernabaca, about forty miles

south of Mexico. Their province, which began at the southern moun‐

tains of the Mexican valley, extended almost sixty miles to the south.

The great province of the Cohuixcas bordered on the north on the

Matlatzincas and the Tlahuicas, on the west on the Cuitlatecs, on the

east on the Yopis and the Mixtecs, and to the south it extended as far

as the Pacific Ocean, in that part where at present are the port and

city of Acapulco. This province was divided into many particular

states, such as those of Zumpango, Chilapa, Tlapa, and Teoixtla (today

Tixtla), a land for the most part excessively hot and unwholesome.

Tlachco, a place celebrated for its silver mines, either belonged to the

aforesaid province or bordered on it.

The Mixtecapan, or province of the Mixtecs, extended from

Acatlán, a place a hundred and twenty miles from the court toward

the southeast, as far as the Pacific Ocean, and contained several cities

and villages, well peopled and of considerable commerce.

To the east of the Mixtecs were the Zapotecs, so called from their

capital Teotzapotlan. In their district was the valley of Huaxyacac,

called by the Spaniards Oaxaca or Guaxaca.

The

[p. 33]

The city of Huaxyacac was afterward erected into a bishopric, and the valley into a marquisate, in favor of the conqueror Don Hernán Bk. I. Cortés. (e)

To the north of the Mixtecs lay the province of Mazatlan, and to the north and east of the Zapotecs that of Chinantla, with their capitals of the same names, whence their inhabitants were called Mazatecs and Chinantecs. The provinces of the Chiapanecs, the Zoques, and the Quelenes were the last of the Mexican empire toward the southeast. The principal cities of the Chiapanecs were Teochiapan (called by the Spaniards Chiapa de Indios), Tochtla, Chamula, and Zinacantán; of the Zoques, Tecpantla; and of the Quelenes, Teopixca.

On the slope and in the surroundings of the famous mountain Popocatépetl, which is thirty-three miles to the southeast of the court, there were the large states of Amaquemecan, Tepoztlán, Yauhtepec, Oaxtepec, Chietla, Itzocan, Acapetlayoccan, Cuauhquechollan, Atlixco, Cholula, and Huexotzinco. These last two, which were the most considerable, having, with the help of their neighbors the Tlaxcalans, shaken off the yoke of the Mexicans, reestablished their aristocratic government. The cities of Cholula and of Huexotzinco were among the greatest, and the most populous, of all that land. The Cholulans had the small hamlet of Cuitlaxcoapan on the very spot where the Spaniards afterward founded the city of Puebla, which is the second of New Spain. (f)

To the east of Cholula was the notable state of

History of Mexico, Vol. I.                         E                  Tepeyacac

(e) Some believe that anciently there was nothing else in the place of Huaxyacac than a mere garrison of the Mexicans, and that that city was founded by the Spaniards; but besides that we know from the register of tributes that Huaxyacac was one of the tributary cities of the crown of Mexico, we know also that the Mexicans were not wont to establish a garrison except in the most populous places of the conquered provinces. The Spaniards were said to found some city whenever they gave a Spanish name to some place of the Indians and established Spanish magistrates there; nor was it otherwise with the founding of Antequera in Huaxyacac, and that of Segura de la Frontera in Tepeyacac.

(f) The Spaniards say Tustla, Mecameca, Izucar, Atrisco, and Quechula instead of Tochtlan, Amaquemecan, Itzocan, Atlixco, and Quecholac.

[p. 34]

Tepeyacac, and farther on that of the Popolocas, whose principal Bk. I.

cities were Tecamachalco and Quecholac. To the south of the Popolocas

lay the state of Tehuacán, bordering on the country of the Mixtecs, to

the east the maritime province of Cuetlachtlan, and to the north that

of the Totonacs. This great province, which on that side was the last

of the empire, extended for fully a hundred and fifty miles, beginning

at the frontier of Zacatlán (a state likewise belonging to the crown of

Mexico, and about eighty miles distant from this court) and ending at

the Gulf of Mexico. Besides the capital, Mizquihuacan, fifteen miles

east of Zacatlán, there was the beautiful city of Cempoala on the coast

of the Gulf, which was the first city of the empire that the Spaniards

entered, and from which, as we shall see, their good fortune began.

These were the principal inland provinces of the Mexican empire,

passing over for the moment several other lesser states in order to

make the description less tedious.

Among the maritime provinces of the Pacific Ocean the most

northerly was that of Colima, whose capital of the same name was

situated in 19° of latitude and 272° of longitude. Continuing along

the same coast toward the southeast there was the province of

Zacatula, with a capital called by the same name. Then came the

coast of the Cuitlatecs, and next that of the Cohuixcas, in whose dis‐

trict lay Acapulco, today a port famous for its commerce with the

Philippine Islands, in 16° 40′ of latitude and 276° of longitude.

Bordering on the coast of the Cohuixcas was that of the Yopis, and

next to this that of the Mixtecs, known in our times by the name of

Jicayán. Then followed the great province of Tehuantepec, and finally

that of Soconusco. The city of Tehuantepec, from which the state

took its name, was situated on a pretty islet formed by a river two

miles from the sea. The province of Soconusco, which was the last

and the most southerly of the empire, bordered on the east and

southeast on the country of Xochitepec, which did not belong to the

crown of Mexico, and on the west on that of

Tehuantepec [p. 35] of Tehuantepec, and toward the south ended at the sea. Its capital, also called Soconusco, was situated between two rivers in 14° of lati‐ Bk. I. tude and 283° of longitude. On the Gulf of Mexico there were, besides the coast of the Totonacs, the provinces of Cuetlachtlan and of Coatzacoalco. The latter bordered on the east on the vast country of Onohualco, under which name the Mexicans included the states of Tabasco and of the peninsula of Yucatán, which were not subject to their dominion. Besides the capital, likewise called Coatzacoalco, founded on the bank of a great river, there were other well-populated places, among which special mention is due to Painala, as having been the birthplace of the famous Malintzin, one of the most effective instruments of the conquest of Mexico. The province of Cuetlachtlan, which had a capital likewise so called, comprised all that coast which lies between the Alvarado River, where the province of Coatzacoalco ends, and the Antigua (*), where that of the Totonacs began. In that part of the coast which the Mexicans called Chalchicuecan there now stand the city and port of Veracruz, the most renowned in all New Spain.

The whole country of Anahuac was, generally speaking, well populated. In the History and in the dissertations we shall have occasion to mention several particular cities and to give some idea of the multitude of their inhabitants. Almost all the inhabited places survive still under the same ancient names, though in part altered; but all the ancient cities, except Mexico, Orizaba, and one or two others, are seen to be so diminished that they scarcely have the fourth part of the number of buildings and inhabitants that they once had; there are many that have only the tenth part, and some that do not preserve even the twentieth part. Now, speaking of the Indians in general, and comparing what is reported of their multitude

E2                          by

(*) We give this river the Spanish name by which it is now known, because we do not know what the Mexicans called it. [p. 36] by the first Spanish historians and the native writers with what we

Bk. I.

have seen with our own eyes, we can affirm that of ten parts of the

ancient inhabitants scarcely one survives at present: a lamentable ef‐

fect of the calamities they have suffered.

The land is for the most part rugged and mountainous, covered

§ 3. Rivers, lakes, and with thick forests, and watered by great rivers, though they are not

fountains.

comparable to those of South America. Of these, some flow to the

Gulf of Mexico and others to the Pacific Ocean. Among the former,

those of Papaloapan, of Coatzacoalco, and of Chiapan are the greatest.

The river of Papaloapan, which the Spaniards call Alvarado, after the

name of the first Spanish captain who sailed upon it, has its principal

source in the mountains of the Zapotecs, and after making a circuit

through the province of Mazatlan, and receiving other lesser rivers

and streams, discharges itself by three navigable mouths into the

Gulf, at a distance of 30 miles from Veracruz. The river Coatzacoalco,

which is likewise navigable, descends from the mountains of the

Mixes, and, crossing the province from which it takes its name, emp‐

ties into the sea near the country of Onohualco. The river of Chiapan

begins its course in the mountains called Cuchumatanes, which sepa‐

rate the diocese of Chiapas from that of Guatemala, crosses the prov‐

ince called after it, and then that of Onohualco, whence it goes to the

sea. The Spaniards call it Tabasco, as they also called that tract of land

which joins the peninsula of Yucatán to the Mexican continent. They

also named it the River of Grijalva, out of regard for the commander

of the first Spanish fleet, who discovered it.

Among the rivers that run to the Pacific Ocean, the most

renowned is the Tololotlan, called by the Spaniards the river of

Guadalajara, or the great river. It takes its rise in the mountains of the

valley of Toluca, crosses the kingdom of Michoacán and Lake

Chapala, then goes on to water the country of Tonalá, where the city

of Guadalajara, capital of New Galicia, now stands, and after running

a course of more than

600

[p. 37]

600 miles, it empties into the sea at a latitude of 22 degrees. The river of Tehuantepec rises in the mountains of the Mixes, and after a Bk. I. short course discharges into the sea at a latitude of fifteen degrees and a half. The river of the Yopis waters the country of that nation, and has its mouth fifteen miles east of the port of Acapulco, forming on that side the dividing line between the dioceses of Mexico and Puebla.

There were also, and there still are, several lakes, which served no less to embellish the country than to facilitate the commerce of those peoples. The lakes of Nicaragua, of Chapala, and of Pátzcuaro, which were the most considerable, did not belong to the Mexican empire. Among the others, those most important to our history are the two of the Mexican valley, of which we have already made mention. That of Chalco extended fully twelve miles from east to west as far as the city of Xochimilco, and then, taking a direction toward the north for as many miles, was joined by means of a canal to the lake of Texcoco; but its breadth did not exceed six miles. The lake of Texcoco measured fifteen miles, and even seventeen, from east to west, and somewhat more from south to north; but its extent is now smaller, because the Spaniards have diverted many of the waters that flowed into it. All the water that runs into it is originally fresh, and becomes brackish only on account of the nitrous bed of the lake in which it is received. (g) Besides these two great lakes there were in the same valley of Mexico, and to the north of this court, two other smaller ones, to which the two cities

of

(g) M. de Bomare says in his Dictionary of Natural History that the salt of the Mexican lake may come from the waters of the North Sea filtered through the earth; and to confirm his opinion he cites Le Journal des Sçavans of the year 1676, but this is truly a gross error, since that lake is 180 miles distant from the sea, besides which the bed of the lake is so elevated that it stands at least a mile in perpendicular height above the surface of the sea. The anonymous author of the work entitled Observations curieuses sur le lac de Mexique (the very one of which the journalists of Paris give the extract) is very far from adopting the error of M. de Bomare. [p. 38] of Zumpango and of Xaltocan gave their names. The lake of Tochtlan Bk. I.

in the province of Coatzacoalco is very beautiful, and its shores are

most delightful.

As for springs, there are so many in that land, and so diverse in

quality, that they would deserve a separate history, especially if those

of the kingdom of Michoacán had to be recorded. There are number‐

less sources of mineral waters, nitrous, sulphurous, vitriolic, and alu‐

minous: some of which burst forth boiling, and so hot that in a few

moments any fruit of the earth or any animal flesh is cooked in them.

There are also petrifying waters, such as those of Tehuacán, a city

about 120 miles distant from Mexico toward the southeast, those of

the spring of Pucuaro in the estates of the Count of Miravalles in the

kingdom of Michoacán, and that of a river in the province of the

Quelenes. With the water of Pucuaro are made little whitish stones,

smooth and not unpleasant to the taste, whose scrapings, taken in

broth or in atolli (*), are among the most powerful diaphoretics, and

are used with marvelous effect against various kinds of fevers. (h) The

citizens of Mexico in the time of their kings used the water of the

great spring of Chapultepec, which was conducted to the city by a

good aqueduct, of which we shall speak elsewhere. On the occasion

of mentioning the waters of that kingdom we could describe, if the

condition of our History permitted, the stupendous falls or cascades

of several rivers, (i) and the bridges over other rivers formed by na‐

ture herself, especially the Puente de Dios. So they call in that country

a vast mass of earth thrown across the deep river Atoyac near the vil‐

lage of Molcaxac, about a hundred miles from Mexico toward the

southeast, over which there comfortably pass

the

(*) Atolli was what the Mexicans called a certain gruel of maiz, or maize, of which

we shall speak in another place.

(h) The little stones of Pucuaro have become known only a short time ago. I was

an eyewitness of their marvelous effects in the epidemic of 1762. The dose pre‐

scribed for those who sweat easily is a dram of scrapings.

(i) Among the cascades the most famous is that made by the great river of

Guadalajara at a place called Tempizque, fifteen miles south of that city. [p. 39] the carts and carriages. It may be believed that it was a fragment of

Bk. I. the neighboring mountain, torn away by some ancient earthquake.

The climate of the countries of Anahuac varies according to their

§ 4. situation. The maritime countries are hot, and for the most part hu‐ Climate of Anahuac. mid and unwholesome. Their heat, which makes one sweat even in January, is caused by the extreme depression of the coasts relative to the inland regions, or by the hills of sand heaped up on the beaches, as happens at Veracruz, my native city. The humidity comes no less from the sea than from the waters that descend in abundance from the mountains that overlook the coasts. In the hot lands there is never any frost, and very many inhabitants of such regions have no other idea of snow than what they acquire by reading books or from the accounts of strangers. Lands that are too elevated, or too near the very high mountains that are eternally covered with snow there, are cold; and I have been on a mountain not more than twenty-five miles distant from the capital, where there is frost and ice even in the dog days. All the other inland countries, where the greatest population of that land was, enjoy a climate so mild and so gentle that they feel neither the rigor of winter nor the heats of summer. It is true that in many of those countries there is frequent frost in the three months of December, January, and February, and sometimes it even snows; but the slight inconvenience that such cold causes lasts no longer than until sunrise: there is no need of any other fire than that of the sun's rays to warm oneself in winter, nor of any other refreshment in time of heat than that of shade. The same garment that covers men in the dog days defends them in January, and the animals sleep the whole year under the open sky.

This mildness and pleasantness of the climate beneath the torrid zone is the effect of several natural causes entirely unknown to the ancients, who believed it uninhabitable, and not well understood by some moderns, by whom it is held to be little favorable

to [p. 40] to living beings. The clearness of the atmosphere, the lesser obliquity

Bk. I.

of the solar rays, and the longer stay of this planet above the horizon

in winter, in comparison with other regions farther from the equator,

combine to lessen the cold and to avoid all that horror with which

Nature is seen disfigured under other zones. One enjoys even at that

season the beauty of the sky and the innocent delights of the coun‐

tryside, whereas under the cold zones, and for the most part even un‐

der the temperate, clouds rob one of the prospect of the sky, and

snow buries the fair productions of the earth. Nor are lesser causes at

work to temper the heat of summer. The copious rains that frequent‐

ly water the earth after midday from April or May to September and

October, the high mountains always laden with snow, scattered here

and there throughout the land of Anahuac, the cool winds that then

blow, and the shorter stay of the Sun above the horizon, in compari‐

son with the regions of the temperate zone, transform the summer of

those happy countries into a cheerful and cool spring.

But the pleasantness of the climate is offset by the thunderstorms,

which are frequent in summer, especially in the neighborhood of

Matlalcueye, or the mountain of Tlaxcala, and by the earthquakes

that are sometimes felt, though with more fright than damage. Both

effects are caused by the sulphur and the other combustible materials

deposited in great abundance in the bowels of the earth. As for hail‐

storms, they are neither more frequent nor greater there than in

Europe.

§ 5.    The fire kindled in the bowels of the earth by the aforesaid sulMountains, stones,

and minerals. phurous and bituminous materials has made in some mountains

vents, or volcanoes, from which flames, ashes, and smoke have at

times been seen to come forth. There are five mountains in the dis‐

trict of the Mexican empire in which this fearful phenomenon has

been observed at different times. The Poyauhtecatl, called by the

Spaniards the Volcano of Orizaba, began to throw out smoke in

1545, and continued for twenty years; but since then there has not

been seen in it in more than two centuries

the [p. 41] the slightest sign of fire. This celebrated mountain, which is of coni‐

Bk. I. cal shape, and is without doubt the highest in all the land of Anahuac, is by reason of its height the first land seen by navigators sailing toward that quarter, at a distance of 150 miles. (k) Its summit is always covered with snow, and its slope is adorned with great cedars, pines, and other trees of precious wood; whence its prospect is beautiful from every side. It is more than ninety miles from the capital toward the east.

The Popocatépetl and the Iztaccíhuatl, near each other and thirty-three miles distant from Mexico toward the southeast, are also of astonishing height. The Popocatépetl, to which they give there by antonomasia the name of Volcan, has a mouth or vent of more than half a mile, through which in the time of the Mexican kings it often threw out flames, and in the last century many times hurled a great quantity of ashes over the neighboring places; but in this century hardly any smoke has been observed. The Iztaccíhuatl, known to the Spaniards by the name of Sierra nevada, has also at times thrown out smoke and ashes. Both mountains always have their summits crowned with snow, which is so abundant that from what tumbles down on the neighboring slopes the cities of Mexico, Puebla, and Cholula are supplied, and other neighboring places, even as far as forty miles distant from those mountains, in which an incredible quantity is consumed all the year in ices. (l) The mountains of Colima and of Tochtlan, very far from the capital, and even farther from each other,

History of Mexico, Vol. I.                           F                         have

(k) The Poyauhtecatl is higher than the Teide, or Peak of Tenerife, according to Father Taillandier, a Jesuit, who observed both. See the Lettres édifiantes, etc. Of the Popocatépetl Thomas Gage says that it is as high as the highest mountains of the Alps. He might have said something more still, if he had also taken into account the elevation of the ground from which this celebrated mountain rises.

(l) The excise on ice, or frozen snow, that is consumed in the capital amounted in 1746 to as much as 15,522 Mexican scudi; some years later it rose to more than 20 thousand, and at present we may believe that it is much more. [p. 42] have at times thrown out fire in our own times. (m) Bk. I.     Besides these mountains there are still others, which, though not

burning, are nonetheless very renowned for their height, such as the

Matlalcueye, or mountain of Tlaxcala, the Nappatecuhtli, called by the

Spaniards, from its shape, Cofre, or trunk, the Tentzon near the village

of Molcaxac, that of Toluca, and others which, as not important to

my purpose, I willingly pass over. Everyone knows by now that the

celebrated chain of the Andes, or Alps of South America, continues

across the Isthmus of Panama and through all New Spain until it is

lost in the unknown countries of the north. The most considerable

part of this chain is known in that kingdom by the name of Sierra

Madre, especially in Sinaloa and Tarahumara, provinces twelve hun‐

dred miles distant from the capital.

The mountains of Anahuac abound in mines of every sort of met‐

al, and of an infinite variety of other fossils. The Mexicans had gold in

the countries of the Cohuixcas, the Mixtecs, the Zapotecs, and in

several others. They collected for

the

(m) A few years ago there was published in Italy an account of the mountain of

Tochtlan, otherwise Tuxtla, full of curious but all too gross lies. In it were to be seen

described rivers of fire, frightened elephants, etc. Among the burning mountains we

do not mention either the Jorullo, or the Momotombo of Nicaragua, or that of

Guatemala; because none of these three was included in the Mexican dominions.

That of Guatemala ruined that great and beautiful city with earthquakes on July 29,

1773. As for the Jorullo, situated in the valley of Urecho in the kingdom of

Michoacán, before the year 1760 there was nothing there but a small hill, on which

stood a large sugar estate. But on September 29, 1760 it burst forth with furious

earthquakes, which utterly ruined both the estate and the neighboring village of La

Huacana, and from that time on it has not ceased to throw out fire and burning

stones, from which three lofty mountains have been formed, whose circumference

was already about six miles, according to the report that in 1766 was made to me by

the Chevalier Don Juan Manuel de Bustamante, governor of that province, and an

eyewitness. In the eruption the ashes were hurled as far as the city of Querétaro, ful‐

ly a hundred and fifty miles distant from Jorullo. A thing truly incredible, but none‐

theless notorious and public in that city, where a gentleman showed me the ashes

that he had collected on a sheet of paper. In the city of Valladolid, sixty miles distant,

ashes rained in such abundance that the courtyards of the houses had to be swept

two or three times a day. [p. 43] the most part this precious metal in grains among the sands of the

Bk. I. rivers, and the aforesaid peoples paid a certain quantity of it to the crown of Mexico. Silver was dug from the mines of Tlachco (celebrated even at that time), of Zumpango, and others; but it was not so highly prized by them as by other nations. After the conquest so many silver mines have been discovered in that country, especially in the provinces that lie to the northwest of the capital, that it is quite impossible to number them. Of copper they had two kinds, one hard, which they used in place of iron for making axes, hatchets, hoes, and other instruments of war and of agriculture, and the other common and pliant, for making basins, pots, and other vessels. This metal abounded more than elsewhere in the provinces of Zacatula and of the Cohuixcas, as it does today in the kingdom of Michoacán. They dug tin from the mines of Tlachco, and lead from those of Ixmiquilpan, a place in the country of the Otomis. Of tin they made money, as we shall say in its place, and of lead we know that it was sold in the markets; but we are wholly ignorant of the use they made of it. They had also iron mines in Tlaxcala, in Tlachco, and in other places; but either they did not discover the mines, or else they did not know how to take advantage of the metal. They had likewise in Chilapa mines of quicksilver, and in many places mines of sulphur, alum, vitriol, cinnabar, ochre, and of a white earth very much like white lead. As for the quicksilver and the vitriol, we do not know what use they made of them; of the other minerals they made use for their paintings and dyes. Of amber and of asphaltum, or bitumen of Judea, there was, and there still is, great abundance on the coasts of both seas, and several places of the empire paid tribute in both of them to the king of Mexico. The amber, which they set in gold, they used only for ornament and pleasure; of the asphaltum they made use in certain incensings, as we shall see elsewhere.

Among the precious stones there were, and there still are, diamonds,

F2                 though [p. 44] though few, emeralds, amethysts, cat's-eyes, turquoises, cornelians, Bk. I.

and certain green stones resembling emeralds and not much inferior

to them, and of all these stones the provinces of the Mixtecs, of the

Zapotecs, and of the Cohuixcas, in whose mountains the mines of

such gems are found, paid tribute to the king. Of their abundance, of

the esteem in which the Mexicans held them, and of the manner in

which they worked them, we shall speak more opportunely in anoth‐

er place. The mountains that lie on the coast of the Gulf of Mexico

between the port of Veracruz and the river Coatzacoalco, as also

those of Chinantla and of the province of the Mixtecs, supplied them

with rock crystal, and the cities of Tochtepec, of Cuetlachtlan, of

Cosamaloapan, and others were obliged to contribute annually a cer‐

tain quantity for the luxury of the court.

No less do those mountains abound in various kinds of stones val‐

ued for use in architecture, sculpture, and other arts. There are quar‐

ries of jasper and of marble of various colors in the mountains of

Calpulalpan east of Mexico, in those that separate the two valleys of

Mexico and of Toluca, now called Monte de las Cruces, and in those of

the Zapotecs; of alabaster at Tecalco (at present Tecali), a place near

the province of Tepeyacac, and in the country of the Mixtecs; of

Tetzontli in the same valley of Mexico, and in many other places of the

kingdom. The stone Tetzontli is for the most part of a dark red color,

quite hard, porous, and light; it binds lime and sand to itself very

tightly, and for that reason is sought after more than any other stone

for the buildings of the capital, whose soil is marshy and not very

firm. There are also whole mountains of lodestone, and among others

a very large one between Teoixtla and Chilapa in the country of the

Cohuixcas. Of the Quetzalitztli, commonly known by the name of

nephritic stone, the Mexicans made various curious figures, some of

which are preserved in several museums of Europe. The Chimaltizatl,

which is a species of selenite, or ass's mirror, is a diaphanous whitish

stone,

and [p. 45] and easily divisible into thin plates, which by means of calcination

Bk. I. yields a fine plaster, and the ancient Mexicans used it for the white of their paintings. There is also an infinite quantity of gypsum and of talc; but as to this stone we do not know the use they made of it. The Mezcuitlatl, that is, dung of the moon, is of the class of those stones which, because of their resistance to the action of fire, are called by the chemists Lapides refractarii. It is transparent, and of a reddish gold color. But no other stone was so commonly used among the Mexicans as the Itztli, of which there is abundance in many places of Mexico. The Itztli is semi-diaphanous, of vitreous substance, and for the most part black; but there is also found white and blue. Of this stone they made mirrors, knives, lancets, razors, and even swords, as we shall say where we treat of their military affairs, and after the introduction of the Gospel highly prized altar stones were made of it. (n)

Since the mineral kingdom of Mexico is so abundant and rich, the § 6. vegetable kingdom is even more plentiful and varied. The celebrated Plants          valued for

their flowers. Dr. Hernández, that is, the Pliny of New Spain, describes in his Natural History as many as twelve hundred plants proper to that land; but his description, being limited to medicinal plants, scarcely comprises a part, though a large one, of what provident Nature has produced there for the benefit of mortals. Of medicinal plants we shall say a word when we treat of the medicine of the Mexicans. With regard to the other classes of vegetables, there are some valued for their flowers, others for their fruits, others for their leaves, others for their root, others for their stem or their wood, and others finally for their gum, resin, oil, or juice. (o) Among the many flowers,

that

(n) The Itztli is known in South America under the name of Gallinazo stone. The celebrated M. de Caylus, in a manuscript dissertation of his, seen and cited by M. de Bomare, proves that the Obsidian stone, of which the ancients made the murrhine vases so highly esteemed by them, was entirely like the Gallinazo stone.

(o) We adopt this division of plants, though imperfect, because it seemed to us the most convenient and the most fitting to the purpose of our History. [p. 46] that adorned the meadows and decked the gardens of the Mexicans, Bk. I. there are some worthy of mention either for the singular beauty of

their colors, or for their most delicate fragrance, or even for their ex‐

traordinary form.

The Floripundio, which by its size deserves the first place, is a

white flower, beautiful, very fragrant, and monopetalous, that is, of a

single leaf, but so large that it measures eight inches and even more in

length, and three or four in diameter at the upper part. Many hang

together from the branches in the form of bells, but not quite round,

since their leaf, or corolla (*), makes five or six angles in it at propor‐

tionate distances from one another. These flowers grow on a pretty

little shrub, whose branches form a round crown in the manner of a

dome. Its trunk is tender, its leaves large, angular, and of a dull green.

The flowers are followed by round fruits, as big as oranges, which

have almonds inside.

The Jolloxochitl, or Flower of the Heart, is also large, and no less

valued for its beauty than for its scent, which is so strong that a single

flower suffices to fill a whole house with the sweetest fragrance. It has

many glutinous leaves, white on the outside and reddish or yellowish

within, and so arranged that when the flower is open and its leaves

spread it has the shape of a star, but when closed it somewhat resem‐

bles a heart, and for that reason it was given such a name. The tree

that bears it is quite large, and its leaves long and rough. (p)

The Coatzontecoxochitl, or Flower of the Viper's Head, is of incom‐

parable beauty. (q) It is composed of five petals,

or

(*) The colored leaves of which the flower is composed are called petals by Fabio

Colonna, and corolla by Linnaeus, to distinguish them from the true leaves.

(p) There is another Jolloxochitl, very fragrant, but quite different in form.

(q) A flower of remarkable form, which scarcely anyone could express in words or imitate

with the brush as it deserves, greatly sought after by the princes of the Indians as a miracle of

nature, and held in high esteem. Hernández, Hist. Nat. N. Hispaniae, bk. 8, ch. 8. The

Lincei Academicians of Rome, who commented on [p. 47] or leaves, purple at the innermost part, whitish in the middle, and red in the rest, but charmingly spotted with yellow and whitish dots. The Bk. I. plant that bears it has leaves resembling those of the iris, or flag, but longer and broader, and small, slender stalks. This flower was one of the most esteemed by the Mexicans.

The Oceloxochitl, or Tiger Flower, is large, composed of three pointed leaves, and red, but toward the middle variegated with white and yellow, and in some manner representing the spots of that beast, whence it took its name. The plant has leaves likewise resembling those of the flag, and a bulbous root.

The Cacaloxochitl, or Raven's Flower, is small, but very fragrant, and painted white, red, and yellow. The tree that bears these flowers is seen to be covered with them everywhere, forming at the ends of the branches natural bunches most pleasing both to the sight and to the smell. There is nothing more common than these flowers in the hot lands: the Indians use them to adorn their altars, and the Spaniards make delicious preserves of them. (r)

The Izquixochitl is a small white flower, resembling in shape the dog rose, or wild rose, and in taste the cultivated rose, but far superior to it in fragrance. It grows on large trees.

The Cempoalxochitl, or Cempasuchil as the Spaniards say, is the flower transplanted into Europe that the French call Œillet d'Inde, or Indian pink. It is very common in Mexico, where they also call it Flower of the Dead, and there are several different species of it, differing in size, in shape, and in the number of leaves of which they are composed.

The flower that the Mexicans call Xiloxochitl, and the Mixtecs

Tiata

and published this History of Hernández in 1651, and saw the portrait of this flower in its colors made in Mexico, formed such an idea of its beauty that they adopted it as the emblem of their most learned Academy, calling it Lynx Flower.

(r) It may be believed that the tree of the Cacaloxochitl is the very one that M. de Bomare describes under the name of Frangipanier.

[p. 48]

Tiata, is composed entirely of fine stamens, equal and straight but pli‐

Bk. I.

ant, and about six finger-breadths long, springing from a round calyx

quite like that of the acorn, but different in size, in color, and in sub‐

stance. Of these lovely flowers some are all red and others all white,

and the tree that bears them is also most beautiful.

The Macpalxochitl, or Hand Flower, resembles the tulip; but its pis‐

til represents the shape of a bird's foot, or rather that of a monkey,

with six fingers ending in as many nails. The Spanish common folk of

that kingdom give to the tree that bears these so curious flowers the

name of Arbol de manitas.

Besides these and other innumerable flowers peculiar to that coun‐

try, in the cultivation of which the Mexicans delighted, the land of

Mexico was enriched with all those that were transplanted there from

Asia and from Europe, such as lilies, jasmines, pinks of various

species, and many others, which now in the gardens of Mexico vie

with the American flowers.

As for fruits, the land of Anahuac is indebted in part to the Canary

§ 7. Plants prized for Islands, and in part to Spain, for melons, apples, peaches, quinces,

their fruit.

apricots, pears, pomegranates, figs, purple morello cherries, walnuts,

almonds, olives, chestnuts, and grapes, though these last were not al‐

together lacking in that land. (f)

With regard to coconuts, the plantain or banana (Musa), citrons,

oranges, and lemons, I had persuaded myself on

the

(f) The places called Parras and Parral in the diocese of Nueva Vizcaya got those

names from the abundance of vines found there, from which many vineyards were

made, which today yield good wine. In the Mixteca there are two species of wild

vines, native to that land: the one, resembling the common vine both in its shoots

and in the shape of its leaves, bears red grapes, large, and covered with a hard skin,

but of a sweet and pleasing taste, which would doubtless be improved if they were

brought under cultivation. The grape of the other vine is hard, large, and of a very

harsh taste; but quite good preserves are made of it. [p. 49] the testimony of Oviedo, of Hernández, and of Bernal Díaz, that the coconuts were owed to the Philippine Islands, and the others to the Bk. I. Canaries; (t) but knowing that many hold a different opinion, I do not wish to engage in a dispute which, besides being of no importance to me, would divert me from the course of the History. It is certain that these plants, and all the others brought there from elsewhere, have thrived happily in that country, and have multiplied as much as in their own. All the maritime lands abound in coconut palms. Of oranges there are seven very different species, and of lemons at least four. There are as many, and very different ones, of (u) Musa, or Platano, as the Spaniards call it. The largest, which is the Zapalote, is from fifteen to twenty inches in length, and up to three in diameter. It is hard, and little esteemed, nor is it eaten otherwise than roasted or cooked. The Platano largo, that is, long, is eight inches at most in length

History of Mexico, Vol. I.                                   G                              and

(t) Oviedo in his Natural History testifies that the first to carry the Musa from the Canary Islands to Hispaniola in 1516 was Fr. Tomás de Berlanga, a Dominican, and that from there it was transplanted to the continent of America. Hernández, in bk. 3, ch. 40 of his Natural History, speaks thus of the coconut: It grows everywhere among the Eastern Indians, and now also among the Western Indians. Bernal Díaz, in his True History of the Conquest, ch. 17, says that he sowed in the land of Coatzacoalco seven or eight orange seeds: And these, he adds, were the first oranges that were planted in New Spain. As for the Musa, it may be believed that of the four species there, only one is foreign, namely the one they call Guineo.

(u) The Musa was not entirely unknown to the ancients. Pliny, citing the account that the soldiers of Alexander the Great gave of all that they saw in India, makes this description of it: Another tree is greater, and more excellent in its fruit and sweetness, on which the wise men of the Indians live. Its leaf imitates the wings of birds, three cubits in length, two in breadth. It puts forth its fruit from the rind, wonderful for the sweetness of its juice, so that one satisfies four. The name of the tree is pala, of the fruit ariena. Hist. Nat., bk. XII, ch. VI. Besides these characteristics proper to the Musa, it must be added that the name Palan, given to the Musa in those remote times, is preserved to this day in Malabar, as Garcia de Orta, a learned Portuguese physician who lived there many years, attests. It might be suspected that from the name Palan the name Platano has been derived, which so ill suits it. The name Bananas, which the French give it, is what it has in Guinea, and that of Musa, which the Italians give it, is taken from the Arabic language. By some it is called Fruit of Paradise, and there was no lack of those who persuaded themselves that this was precisely the fruit that made our first Fathers transgress. [p. 50] and one and a half in diameter. Its skin is at first green, then yellow, Bk. I.

and at greater ripeness black, or blackish. The fruit is tasty and

wholesome, whether eaten cooked or raw. The Guineo is smaller than

the other, but thicker, softer, more delicious, and less wholesome.

Those fibers with which the pulp is seen to be covered are flatulent.

This species of Musa is cultivated in the public garden of Bologna,

and we have tasted it; but we found it so poorly ripened and unpleas‐

ant, on account of the climate, that it might be taken for another

species altogether different from that one. The Dominico is the small‐

est, but also the most delicate. The plant, too, is smaller than the oth‐

ers. There are in that land whole forests, and very large ones, of Musa

no less than of oranges and lemons, and in Michoacán a considerable

trade is carried on in dried plantain, which is much better than grapes

and figs.

Now the fruits indubitably native to that land are the Ananas,

which, because at first sight it resembles the pine cone, was called

Piña by the Spaniards; the Mamei, the Chirimoya (v), the Anona, the

Cabeza, that is, head, de Negro, the black Zapote, the Chicozapote, the

white Zapote, the yellow Zapote, the Zapote of St. Dominic, the

Ahuacate, the Guayaba, the Capulino, the Guava, or Cuaxinicuil, the

Pitahaya, the Papaya, the Guanabana, the encarcelada nut, or impris‐

oned nut, plums, pine nuts, dates, the Chajote, the Tilapo, the Obo, or

Hobo, the Nance, the Cacahuate, and several others, the knowledge of

which does not matter to Italian readers. These fruits are for the

most part described in the works of Oviedo, Acosta, Hernández,

Laet, Nieremberg,

Marcgrave

(v) Several European writers on the things of America confuse the Chirimoya with

the Anona and with the Guanabana: but these are three different species of fruit,

though the first two resemble each other somewhat. One must also take care not to

confuse the Ananas with the Anona, which are more different from each other than

the watermelon and the melon. M. de Bomare, on the contrary, makes two fruits of

the Chirimoya and the Cherimolia, whereas cherimolia is nothing other than a cor‐

ruption of the first and legitimate name of that fruit. The Ate also, which some set

down as a fruit altogether different from the Chirimoya, is only a variety of its

species.

[p. 51]

Marcgrave, Piso, Barrère, Sloane, Ximénez, Ulloa, and many other naturalists: so that I shall speak only of some of those least known in Bk. I. Europe.

All the fruits comprised by the Mexicans under the generic name of Tzapotl are round, or approach roundness, and all have a hard stone. (x) The black Zapote has a thin rind, green, smooth, and tender, and the pulp black, soft, and sweetly flavored, which at first sight resembles that of cassia. (y) Inside the pulp it has flat blackish stones, no longer than a finger's breadth. It is perfectly spherical, and its diameter is from one and a half to four or five inches. The tree is of medium size, and dense, and its leaves small. The pulp of this fruit, iced and seasoned with sugar and cinnamon, is of a delicate taste.

The white Zapote, which for its narcotic virtue was called by the Mexicans Cochitzapotl, is somewhat like the black one in size, in shape, and in the color of the rind, though in the rind of the white the green is lighter; but in other respects it differs greatly, for the pulp of this one is white and delightful. Its stone, which is believed to be poisonous, is large, round, hard, and white. The tree is dense, and larger than the black one, and the leaves are larger still. Moreover, the black is proper to a hot climate, and the white, on the contrary, to a cold or temperate one.

The Chicozapote (in Mexican Chictzapotl) is of spherical shape, or approaches it, and measures an inch and a half or two inches in diameter. Its rind is gray, its pulp reddish white, and its stones black, hard, and pointed. From

G2                            this

(x) The fruits comprised by the Mexicans under the name of Tzapotl are the Mamei Tetzontzapotl, the Chirimoya Matzapotl, the Anona Quauhtzapotl, the black Zapote Tliltzapotl, etc.

(y) Gemelli says that the black Zapote has also the flavor of cassia; but this is very far from the truth, as all those who have eaten it know. He says further that this fruit, when it is unripe, is poison to fish; but it is surprising that such an effect should have been known only to the foreigner Gemelli, who did not stay ten months in Mexico. [p. 52] this fruit, when still green, a gluey milk is drawn, which readily conBk. I. denses, called by the Mexicans Chictli and by the Spaniards Chicle,

which boys and women are accustomed to chew, and in Colima they

use it to make small statues and curious figurines. (z) The

Chicozapote, well ripened, is one of the most delicious fruits, and in

the opinion of many, even Europeans, superior to all the fruits of

Europe. The tree is of medium size, its wood good for working, and

its leaves round, and similar in color and consistency to those of the

orange tree. It grows without cultivation in the hot lands, and in the

Mixteca, in the Huasteca, and in Michoacán there are woods of such

trees twelve and fifteen miles long. (A)

The Capollino, or Capulin, as the Spaniards call it, is the cherry of

Mexico. The tree is hardly distinguishable from the cherry tree of

Europe, and the fruit is similar in size, in color, and in the stone; but

not so in flavor.

The Nance is a small round fruit, yellow, aromatic, and savory, with

very small seeds, which grows on trees proper to the hot lands.

The Chayote is a round fruit, and similar in the husk with which it

is covered to the chestnut, but four or five times larger, and of a deep‐

er green color. Its flesh is greenish white, and in the middle it has a

large stone, white and similar to the flesh in substance. It is eaten

cooked together with the stone. This fruit grows on a twining and

perennial plant, whose root is also good to eat.

The imprisoned nut is so called by the common people because

its

(z) Gemelli persuaded himself that the Chicle was a composition made on pur‐

pose; but he was mistaken, since it is nothing other than the simple milk of the un‐

ripe fruit condensed in the air. The aforesaid author makes mention of these fruits in

vol. 6, bk. 2, ch. 10.

(A) Among the gross lies of Thomas Gage is that of saying that in the garden of

San Jacinto (the hospice of the Dominicans of the Mission of the Philippine Islands,

in a suburb of Mexico, where he lodged for several months) there were

Chicozapotes. This fruit can never thrive in the valley of Mexico, nor in any other

country exposed to frost. [p. 53] its almond is closely shut up inside a very hard shell. It is smaller than

Bk. I. the common walnut, and in shape it resembles the nutmeg; its shell is smooth, and its almond is sparser and of inferior taste to the common one. (B) The common walnut, brought there from Europe, has likewise multiplied greatly, and has become as common as in Europe itself.

The plant of the Tlalcacahuatl, or Cacahuate, as the Spaniards call it, is without doubt one of the rarest that are found there. It is an herb, but very bushy, and well furnished with roots. Its leaves are somewhat like those of purslane, but less thick. Its little flowers are white, and from them comes no fruit. Its fruit grows not on the branches or on the stem, as in other plants, but attached to the hairlike fibers of the roots, inside a white sheath, grayish, long, roundish, ridged, and rough, just as it is represented in our third figure among those of the flowers and fruits. Each sheath holds two, three, or four cacahuates, which have the shape of pine nuts, but are much larger and thicker, and each is composed, like so many other seeds, of two lobes, and has its germinating point. It is edible, and of good taste, not raw, but a little toasted. If they are toasted more, they take on a smell and a taste so like those of coffee that it is not difficult to deceive anyone. An oil is made from the cacahuates which is not of bad taste; but it is believed to be harmful, because it is too heating. It gives a fine light, but is easily put out. This plant would surely thrive in Italy. It is sown in March or in April, and the harvest is gathered in October or in November.

Among the very many other fruits which, to shorten my description, I pass over, I cannot refrain from making mention

of

(B) We speak only of the imprisoned nut of the Mexican empire, since that of New Mexico is larger, and of better flavor than the common walnut of Europe, according to what a man worthy of trust has told me. It may be believed that this walnut of New Mexico is the same as that of Louisiana, called Pacana, or Pacaria. [p. 54] of the cacao, the vanilla, the chia, the chilli, or capsicum, the tomato, Bk. I.

Tabasco pepper, cotton, and the grains and pulses most in use among

the Mexicans.

Of the cacao (a name taken from the Mexican Cacahuatl) Dr.

Hernández counts four species; but the Tlalcacahuatl, the smallest of

all, was the one the Mexicans most commonly used in their chocolate

and in other daily drinks, while the other species served them more

as money for trading in the market than as food. The cacao was one

of the most cultivated plants in the hot lands of that kingdom; and

several provinces paid tribute in it to the Crown of Mexico, among

others that of Soconusco, whose cacao is excellent and better not

only than that of Caracas, but also than that of Magdalena. The de‐

scription of this celebrated plant, and of its cultivation, is found in

many authors of all the civilized nations of Europe.

Vanilla, or Vainiglia, so well known and so much used in Europe,

grows without cultivation in the hot lands. The ancient Mexicans

used it in their chocolate and in other drinks which they made of

cacao.

The chia is the small seed of a beautiful plant, whose stem is

straight and quadrangular, the branches spread toward the four quar‐

ters and symmetrically opposed, and the little flowers blue. There are

two species, the one black and small, from which an excellent oil for

painting is drawn, and the other white and larger, from which a re‐

freshing drink is made. Both the one and the other were used by the

Mexicans for these and other purposes, which we shall see later.

Of the chilli, or capsicum, (C) which was as common among the

Mexicans as salt among the Europeans, there are at least eleven

species, differing in size, in shape, and in pungency. The Quauhchilli,

which is the fruit of a

shrub

(C) In other countries of America they call the chilli Axì, in Spain Pimiento, and in

France Poivre de Guinée, and by other names. In Italian I use the name by which it is

known in the places of Italy where I have been. [p. 55] shrub, and the Chiltecpin are the smallest, but also the most pungent.

Bk. I. Of the tomato there are six species, distinct in size, in color, and in taste. The largest, which is the Xictomatl, or Xitomate, as the Spaniards of Mexico call it, is already very common in Europe: in Spain and in France under the name of Tomate (D), and in Italy under that of Pomo d’oro. The Miltomatl is smaller, green, and perfectly round. How much both were used by the Mexicans at their meals will be told later, when we come to speak of their foods.

The Xocoxochitl, commonly known by the name of Tabasco pepper because it abounds in that province, is larger than the pepper of Malabar. It grows on a large tree, whose leaves have the color and the luster of those of the orange tree; and the flowers are a fine red, and similar in shape to those of the pomegranate, and of a lively and most pleasing odor, in which the branches also share. The fruit is round and grows in clusters, which, being green at first, afterwards turn almost black. This pepper, already used by the ancient Mexicans, can take the place of that of Malabar.

Cotton was, by reason of its usefulness, one of the most considerable fruits of that country: for it took the place of flax (although this plant, too, was not lacking to them (E)), and of it the inhabitants of Anahuac for the most part clothed themselves. There is a white kind, and a tawny kind, commonly called coyote. It is a plant very common in the hot lands, but much more cultivated by the ancients than by the moderns.

The

(D) The Tomatl of the Mexicans is the generic name of all the fruits of that class. The Spaniards of Europe and the French adopted it to signify the Xictomatl, which is the species known to them, and the Spaniards of Mexico to signify the Miltomatl, which in that country is the most usual.

(E) Flax was in fact found in Michoacán, in New Mexico, and in Quivira in great abundance, and of excellent quality; but we do not know that those nations cultivated it or made use of it. The Court of Spain, aware of the lands in the kingdom of Mexico that are suited to the cultivation of flax and hemp, sent to that country last year (1778) twelve peasant families from the Vega of Granada, so that they might employ themselves in this branch of agriculture.

[p. 56]

The fruit of the Achiote, called Rocou by the French, served at that Bk. I. time for the dyes of the Mexicans, as it serves now for those of the

Europeans. Of the bark of the tree they made cords, and with the

wood they drew fire by friction, after the custom of the ancient shep‐

herds of Europe. This plant is well described in the Dictionary of M.

de Bomare.

As for the grains and pulses properly so called, that land received

from Europe wheat, barley, rice, chickpeas, peas, broad beans, lentils,

and others; all of which throve happily in lands suited to their nature,

and have multiplied there as much as we shall show in our disserta‐

tions. (F)

Among the grains the chief, the most useful, and the most usual

was maize, called by the Mexicans Tlaolli, of which there are many

species differing in size, in color, in weight, and in taste. There is a

large kind and a small, white, yellow, blue, purple, red, and black. Of

maize the Mexicans made their bread and several dishes, of which we

shall speak elsewhere. Maize was carried from America to Spain, and

thence to other countries of Europe, to the great advantage of the

poor, although there is no lack in our day of an author who claims to

make America indebted to Europe for maize. A notion truly the most

extravagant and the most improbable that can enter a man’s head. (G)

The

(F) Dr. Hernández describes in his Natural History of Mexico the species of wheat

that was found in Michoacán, and boasts of its prodigious fecundity; but the an‐

cients either did not know how, or else did not wish, to make use of it, valuing more

highly, as they still do today, their maiz, or maize. The first who sowed European

wheat in that land was a Black slave of the conqueror Cortés, who had found three

or four grains inside a sack of rice that was carried for the provisioning of the

Spanish soldiers.

(G) Here are the words of M. de Bomare in his Dictionn. d’hist. nat., entry Blé de

Turquie: “This curious and useful plant was given the name of blé d’Inde, because it

takes its origin from the Indies, whence it was brought to Turkey, and from there to

all the other parts of Europe, of Africa, and of America.” The name of Grano di

Turchia, by which it has been known in Italy, will doubtless have been M. de Bomare’s

whole reason for

1. Coatzontecoxochitl. 2. Xiloxochitl. 3. Iolloxochitl. 4. Oceloxochitl. 5.

Macpalxochitl.

6. Nopalxochquetzalli. 7. Tlalcacahuatl. 8. Tliltzapotl. 9. Chajotli.

[p. 57]

The principal pulse of the Mexicans was that of the beans, of which there are several species, and much more varied than those of Bk. I. maize. The largest species is that of the Ayacotli, which is the size of a broad bean, and springs from a handsome red flower; but the most esteemed is that of certain small, black, heavy beans. This pulse, which in Italy is little esteemed, because poor, is in Mexico so good that it serves not only for the sustenance of poor people, but also for the delicacies of the Spanish nobility.

As for the plants valued for their root, their leaves, their stem, or § 8.

Plants valued for their wood, the Mexicans had very many that served them for food, their root, for their

leaves, for their stem, such as the Xicama, the Camote, the Huacamote, the Cacomite, and oth‐ or for their wood. ers, or else that provided them with thread for their cloths and their cords, such as the Iczotl, and several species of Metl or maguei, or again that furnished them with wood for buildings and other works, such as the cedar, the pine, the cypress, the fir, the ebony, etc.

The Xicama, called Catzotl by the Mexicans, is a root of the shape and the size of an onion, all white, firm, fresh, juicy, and savory, which is always eaten raw.

The Camote is another root, most common in all that land, of which there are three species, one white, another yellow, and another purple. Cooked camotes are of good taste, especially those of Querétaro, which are deservedly prized throughout the kingdom. (*)

The Cacomite is the edible root of the plant that bears the beautiful Tiger Flower, which we have already described

History of Mexico, Vol. I.                           H                           The adopting such an error, contrary to the testimony of all the writers of America and to the universal sentiment of nations. By the Spaniards of Europe and of America maize is called by the name Maiz, taken from the Haitian language that was spoken in the island now called Hispaniola, or Santo Domingo.

(*) Many call the camotes Batate, or Patate; but I have avoided this name, because it is equivocal and is used indifferently by authors to signify both the camotes and the potatoes, which are quite different roots.

[p. 58]

The Huacamote is the sweet root of a species of Jucca (H), which is Bk. I.

also eaten cooked. The potato, a root transplanted to Europe and

much esteemed in Ireland and in Sweden, was also carried to Mexico

from South America, its own country, just as there were brought

thither from Spain and from the Canaries several other roots and sal‐

ad plants, such as turnips, radishes, carrots, garlic, lettuces, asparagus,

cabbages, and the like. Of onions, Cortés bears witness in his letters

to Charles V that they were sold in the markets of Mexico: so that

they had no need to have them brought from Europe. Moreover, the

name Xonacatl, which they give to the onion, and that of Xonacatepec,

by which a place has been known since the time of the Mexican

kings, make us know that this plant was very ancient in that land, and

not transplanted from Europe.

The Maguei, called Metl by the Mexicans, Pita by the Spaniards,

and by many authors American aloe, since it is in fact very like the true

aloe, is among the most common and most useful plants of Mexico.

Dr. Hernández describes as many as nineteen species of it, still more

different in the inner substance than in the form and the color of

their leaves. In Book VII of our History we shall have occasion to set

forth the great advantages which the Mexicans drew from these

plants, and the incredible profit which the Spaniards draw from them

today.

The Iczotl is a species of mountain palm, quite tall, which for the

most part has a doubled trunk. Its branches have the shape of a fan,

and its leaves that of a sword. Its flowers are white and fragrant, of

which the Spaniards make good preserves, and its fruit is like the

Musa at first sight, but quite useless. Of the leaves they made in an‐

cient times, and still make today, fine mats, and the Mexicans drew

thread from them for their manufactures.

This is not the only palm of that country. Besides the

royal

(H) The Jucca is the plant from whose root they make the bread called Casave in

some countries of America. [p. 59] royal palm, which surpasses the others in the beauty of its branches, the coconut palm, and those of dates, (I) there are other species wor‐ Bk. I. thy of mention.

The Quauhcojolli is a palm of middling size, whose trunk is inaccessible to quadrupeds, being entirely armed with long, strong, and very sharp spines. Its branches have the shape of a handsome plume, among which its fruits are seen hanging in large clusters, round, as large as common walnuts, and like them composed of four parts, namely a husk at first green and afterwards dark, a yellow pulp tenaciously attached to the stone, a round and very hard stone, and inside the stone an almond, or white marrow.

The palm Ixhuatl is smaller, and has no more than six or seven branches, for where a new branch sprouts, one of the old ones soon dries up. Of its leaves they made baskets and mats, and today they make hats also and other articles. Its bark, to the depth of three finger-breadths, is nothing but a mass of membranes about a foot long, thin and flexible, but otherwise strong, of which, by joining many together, some poor people make their mattresses.

The palm Teoiczotl is also small. The pith of its trunk, which is soft, is surrounded by certain leaves of a peculiar substance, round, thick, white, smooth, and shining, which look like so many shells piled one upon another, and which the Indians used anciently, and still use today, for the ornament of the arches of boughs that they make for their festivals.

There is another palm, which bears the oil nuts (called by the Spaniards cocos de aceite), so named because a good oil is drawn from them. The oil nut is a nut similar in shape and in size to the nutmeg,

H2                       inside

(I) Besides the date palm proper to that country, there is also that of Barbary. Dates are sold in the month of June in the markets of Mexico City, Puebla, and other cities, but in spite of their sweetness they are not much sought after. [p. 60] inside which is a white almond, oily and edible, covered with a thin Bk. I.

purple skin. The oil gives off a sweet odor, but is too easily con‐

densed, and then becomes a soft mass, white as snow.

As for the excellence, the variety, and the abundance of woods,

that country yields to none in the world; for since no sort of climate

is lacking there, neither are the trees proper to each lacking. Besides

the oaks, the durmast oaks, the firs, the pines, the cypresses, the

beeches, the elms, the walnuts, the poplars, and very many others

common in Europe, there are whole forests of cedars and of ebonies,

the two kinds of trees most esteemed by the ancients; there is a great

quantity of agalloch, or aloes-wood, in the Mixteca, of Tapinzeran in

Michoacán, of Caoba in Chiapas, of Palo gateado, which we might call

striped wood, in Zoncoliuhcan (today Zongolica), of Camote in the

mountains of Texcoco, of Granadillo, or red ebony, in the Mixteca and

elsewhere, of Mizquitl or true acacia, of Tepehuaxin, of Coptè, of Jabin,

of Guayacan, or lignum vitae, of Ayaquahuitl, of Oyametl, of Zopilote

wood, and of other innumerable woods valued for their incorruptibil‐

ity, for their hardness and weight, (K) for their pliancy or ease of carv‐

ing, for the beauty of their colors, or for their fragrance. The Camote

has a most beautiful purple color, and the Granadillo a dark red; but

still more beautiful are the Palo gateado, the Caoba, and the

Tzopiloquahuitl, or Zopilote wood. The hardness of the Guayacan is al‐

ready well known in Europe, and that of the Jabin is no less. The

aloes-wood of the Mixteca, although different from the true agalloch

of the Levant, judging by the description

that

(K) Pliny, in his Natural History, bk. 16, ch. 4, mentions no other woods of greater

specific gravity in relation to water than these four: ebony, boxwood, larch, and

stripped cork; but in Mexico there are many other trees whose wood does not float

in water, such as the Guayacan, the Tapinzeran, the Jabin, the Quiebrahacha, etc. The

Quiebrahacha, that is, axe-breaker, is so called because in cutting it the axe not infre‐

quently breaks, from the hardness of the wood. [p. 61] that Garcia de Orta gives of it (*), and other authors, is none the less

Bk. I. to be prized for the very sweet odor it gives off, especially when freshly cut. There is also in that country a tree whose wood is precious, but otherwise of so malignant a nature that it causes swelling of the scrotum in anyone who handles it carelessly when it has newly been cut. The name which the Michoacans give it, which I do not remember, expresses that bad effect plainly. I have not been a witness of such a phenomenon, nor have I seen the tree; but I learned of it when I was in Michoacán from persons worthy of trust.

Dr. Hernández describes in his Natural History about a hundred species of trees; but since he devoted his study principally, as we have already said, to medicinal plants, he leaves out the greater part of the trees which that most fertile land bears, and especially those most considerable for their size and for the preciousness of their wood. There are trees of a height and breadth so surprising that they are in no way inferior to those that Pliny boasts of as miracles of Nature.

Father Acosta makes mention of a cedar that was in Atlacuechahuayan, a place nine miles distant from Antequera, or Oaxaca, whose trunk had a circumference of sixteen brazas, that is, more than eighty-two Paris feet; and I have myself seen in a country house a beam a hundred and twenty Castilian feet long, or a hundred and seven Paris feet. In several houses of the capital, and of other cities of the kingdom, very large cedar planks of a single piece are to be seen. In the valley of Atlixco there is still preserved alive a very ancient fir (L), so large that in the cavity in its trunk made by

some

(*) History of the Simples, Aromatics, etc., of the East Indies.

(L) The Mexican name of this tree is Ahuehuetl, and the Spanish common people of that country call it Ahuehuete; but those who wish to speak in Castilian call it Sabino, that is, savin, in which they are mistaken; for the Ahuehuetl, though very like the savin, is not even a savin, but a fir, as Dr. Hernández shows in bk. 3, ch. 66, of [p. 62] some thunderbolts, fourteen men on horseback can stand comfortBk. I.

ably. A still greater idea of its breadth is given us by a testimony as au‐

thoritative as that of His Excellency Señor Don Francisco Lorenzana,

formerly Archbishop of Mexico, and at present of Toledo. This

prelate, in the annotations made by him on the Letters of the

Conqueror Cortés to Charles V, printed in Mexico City in 1770,

bears witness to us that, having himself gone to observe that

renowned tree, in company with the Archbishop of Guatemala and

the Bishop of Puebla, he had as many as a hundred boys enter the

aforesaid cavity of the tree.

Comparable also with this famous fir are the Ceibas which I saw in

the maritime province of Jicayán. The breadth of these trees is pro‐

portioned to their prodigious height, and their prospect is supremely

delightful in the season when they are seen adorned with new foliage,

and laden with fruit, inside which is a certain kind of cotton, white,

fine, and most delicate. Cloths could be made of it, and in fact have

been made, as soft and delicate as those of silk, and perhaps even

more so; (M) but it is hard to spin on account of the smallness of the

threads, besides which the advantage would be less than the labor,

since the cloth would have to be of little durability. Some use that

cotton in cushions and mattresses, which have the peculiarity of

swelling enormously in the heat of the sun.

Among very many trees worthy of memory for their singularity,

which I am compelled to pass over, there is one I cannot omit, a cer‐

tain kind of wild fig tree, which grows in the country of the

Cohuixcas

his Natural History. I saw the fir of Atlixco as I passed through that city in 1756, but

not so near that I could form a just idea of its size.

(M) M. de Bomare says that the Africans make of the thread of the Ceiba the veg‐

etable taffeta so rare and so esteemed in Europe. I do not wonder at the rarity of

such a cloth, given the difficulty there is in making it. The name Ceiba is taken, like

many others, from the language that was spoken in the island of Haiti, or Santo

Domingo. The Mexicans call it Pochotl, and many Spaniards Pochote. In Africa it has

the name of Benten. The Ceiba, says the aforesaid author, is taller than all the trees

hitherto known.

[p. 63]

Cohuixcas, and in other places of the kingdom. It is a tall, thick, and solid tree, similar in its leaves and in its fruit to the common fig. Bk. I. From its branches, which spread horizontally, there spring certain filaments, which, taking their direction toward the ground, go on constantly thickening and growing until, introduced into it, they make roots, and form as many other trunks, so that from a single fig tree a forest can be made.
