The fruit of this tree is quite useless, but the wood is good. (N)

As for the plants, finally, that yield us resins, gums, oils, or prof‐ § 9. itable juices, the land of Anahuac is most singularly fertile in them, as Plants useful for their

resins, gums, oils, and Acosta acknowledges in his Natural History.                                            juices.

The Huitziloxitl, from which the balsam distills, is a tree of middling height. Its leaves are somewhat like those of the almond tree, but larger; its wood is reddish and fragrant, and the bark ashen, but covered with a reddish film. Its pale flowers come at the ends of the branches. Its seed is small, whitish, and curved, and comes likewise at the end of a thin husk a finger long. At whatever point an incision is made, especially after rains, there distills from it that most noble resin so greatly esteemed in Europe, and in no way inferior to the renowned balsam of Palestine. (O) Our balsam is reddish black, or else whitish yellow; for of the one color and of the other it flows from the incision; of an acrid and bitter taste, and of an intense but most agreeable odor. The balsam tree is common in the provinces of Pánuco and of Chiapas,

and

(N) Father Andrés Pérez de Ribas, in his History of the Missions of Sinaloa, and M. de Bomare, in his Dictionary under the names of Figuier des Indes, Grand Figuier, and Figuier admirable, make mention of this singular fig tree. The historians of the East Indies describe another tree like this, which is found in those regions.

(O) The first balsam that was brought from Mexico to Rome was sold at a hundred ducats the ounce, as Dr. Monardes attests in his History of the Medicinal Simples of America; and it was declared by the Apostolic See to be fit matter for chrism, though different from that of Palestine, as Acosta and other historians of America observe. [p. 64] and in other hot lands. The Mexican kings had it transplanted to the Bk. I. celebrated garden of Oaxtepec, where it throve happily, and multi‐

plied considerably in all those mountains. Some Indians, in order to

draw more balsam from it, after making an incision in the tree, burn

its branches. The abundance of these precious plants makes them dis‐

regard the loss of a great number of them, so as not to have to wait

for the slowness of the distillation. Not only did the ancient

Mexicans gather the opobalsamum, or tear, distilled from the trunk,

but they also obtained the xylobalsamum by the decoction of the

branches. (P) From the Huaconex, and from the Maripenda (*) they ob‐

tained likewise an oil equivalent to balsam. The Huaconex is a tree of

middling height, and of aromatic and hard wood, which keeps uncor‐

rupted for years, even when buried in the earth. Its leaves are small

and yellow, the flowers likewise small and whitish, and the fruit like

that of the laurel. They drew the oil by distillation from the bark of

the tree, after breaking it up, keeping it three days in ordinary water,

and then drying it in the sun. They drew also from the leaves an oil of

agreeable odor. The Maripenda is a shrub, whose leaves have the

shape of a lance head, and the fruit is like the grape, and comes in

clusters, at first green, then red. They drew the oil by the decoction

of the branches mixed with some fruit.

The Xochiocotzotl, commonly called liquidambar, is the liquid

storax of the Mexicans. The tree is large (not merely a small shrub, as

Pluche says); its leaves are somewhat like those of the maple,

toothed, whitish on one side, dark on the other, and arranged in

threes. The fruit is spiny and roundish, but polygonal, with black sur‐

faces and yellow angles. The bark of the tree is in part green and in

part tawny. From the trunk is drawn by incision

that

(P) An oil is also drawn from the fruit of the Huitziloxitl, similar in odor and in

taste to that of bitter almonds, but of greater pungency, and of a more intense odor,

which is very useful in medicine.

(*) The names Huaconex and Maripenda are not Mexican; but are those used by the

authors who write of those plants. [p. 65] that precious resin, which the Spaniards called Liquidambar, and the oil of the same name, which is still more fragrant and valuable. Bk. I. Liquidambar is also drawn by the decoction of the branches, but it is inferior to that which distills from the tree.

The Mexican name Copalli is generic, and common to all resins, but especially signifies those that serve for incense. There are as many as ten species of trees that yield these sorts of resin, differing among themselves not so much in name as in the form of the foliage and of the fruit, and in the quality of the resin. The Copal, by antonomasia, is a white and transparent resin, which distills from a large tree, whose leaves resemble those of the oak, but are larger, and the fruit is roundish and reddish. This resin is well known in Europe under the name of gum copal, and the use made of it both in medicine and in varnishes is also well known. The ancient Mexicans used it principally in the incensings which they made, now for the religious worship of their idols, now as homage to ambassadors and other persons of the first rank. Today they consume a great quantity of it in the worship of the true God and of his saints. The Tecopalli, or Tepecopalli, is a resin like the frankincense of Arabia in color, in odor, and in taste, which distills from a tree of middling size, that grows in the mountains, whose fruit is like an acorn containing a pine nut moistened with a mucilage, or viscous saliva, and inside it a little almond, which is usefully employed in medicine. Both these trees, and all the others of this class, in whose description we cannot linger, are proper to hot lands.

The caranna and the tacamahac, resins well known in the apothecaries’ shops of Europe, distill from two very large Mexican trees. The tree of the caranna (*) has a tawny trunk, smooth,

History of Mexico, Vol. I.                          I                     shining

(*) The Mexicans gave the tree of the caranna the name of Tlahuelilocaquahuitl, that is, tree of wickedness (not Habeliloca, as M. de Bomare writes); because they superstitiously believed that it was feared by the spirits [p. 66] shining, and fragrant, and its leaves, though round, are not unlike Bk. I.

those of the olive tree. The tree of the tacamahac has broad, toothed

leaves, and the fruit red, round, and small, hanging from the ends of

the branches. Both grow in hot countries.

The Mizquitl, or Mezquite, as the Spaniards call it, is a species of

true acacia, and the gum that distills from it is true gum arabic, as Dr.

Hernández and other learned naturalists bear witness. The mesquite

is a thorny shrub, whose branches are very irregularly arranged, and

the leaves, thin, slender, and conjugate, that is, growing on one side

and the other of the branches, resemble the feathers of birds; its

flowers are similar to those of the birch. Its fruits are sweet and edi‐

ble pods, and contain the seed, of which the barbarous Chichimecs

anciently made a paste that served them for bread. Its wood is very

hard and heavy. These trees are almost as common in Mexico as oaks

in Europe, especially on the hills of the temperate countries. (Q)

The lac, or gum lac (as it is wont to be called by apothecaries),

flows in such abundance from a tree resembling the mesquite that the

branches are covered with it. (R)

This

of evil, and to be an effective preservative against fascination. The name Tecamaca is

taken from the Mexican Tecomac ihiyac.

(Q) In Michoacán there is a species of mesquite or acacia that has no thorns at all,

and has thinner leaves; but in the rest it agrees entirely with the other.

(R) Garcia de Orta, in his History of the Simples of India, establishes on the re‐

port of some men experienced in that country that lac is made by certain ants. This

opinion has been adopted by very many authors, and M. de Bomare does it the hon‐

or of believing it almost demonstrated already; but how far it is from the truth may

be seen: 1. because all these vaunted demonstrations are nothing but equivocal indi‐

cations and fallacious conjectures, as is plain to anyone who reads the aforesaid au‐

thors. 2. Among all the naturalists who write of lac, there is no other who has seen it

on the tree, except Dr. Hernández; and yet this learned and candid author affirms, as

indubitable, that lac is a true resin distilled from the tree which the Mexicans call

Tzinacancuitlaquahuitl, and rejects, as a vulgar error, the opposite opinion. 3. The

country where lac abounds is the extremely fertile province of the [p. 67] This tree, whose size is middling, and the trunk of a red color, is very

Bk. I. common in the provinces of the Cohuixcas and of the Tlahuicas.

Dragon’s blood distills from a large tree, whose leaves are broad and angular. This tree grows in the mountains of Huauchinango and in those of the Cohuixcas. (S)

The elastic resin, called Olin or Olli by the Mexicans, and Ule by the Spaniards of that kingdom, distills from the Olquahuitl, which is a tree of sufficient size, whose trunk is smooth and yellowish, the leaves quite large, the flowers white, and the fruit yellow and roundish, but angular, inside which there are almonds as large as hazelnuts, white, but covered with a yellowish skin. The almond has a bitter taste, and the fruit is always attached to the bark of the tree. The Ule, as it distills from the wounded trunk, is white, liquid, and viscous: then it becomes yellowish, and finally takes the color of lead, somewhat blacker, which it keeps from then on. Those who gather it have it take in various molds whatever form they wish, according to the use for which they intend it. This resin, condensed, is of all solid bodies the most elastic that we know.

The Mexicans made their balls of this resin, which, though heavy, bound higher than those of air. Today, besides this use, they use it, as wax is used in Europe, for making hats, boots, greatcoats, and cloaks

I2                 impervious Tlahuicas, where all the fruits grow marvelously well, and from there a very great quantity is carried to the capital. Now so great a harvest of fruits could not be made if there were in that land as many millions of ants as would be needed for the making of so excessive a quantity of lac, since the trees are very many, and almost all of them are full of it. 4. If lac is the work of ants, why do they make it only on those trees, and not on those of another species? etc. Lac was called by the Mexicans Tzinacancuitlatl, that is, bat dung, from some analogy which they found between those two things.

(S) The Mexicans call dragon’s blood Ezpatli, which means blood medicine, and the tree Ezquahuitl, that is, blood tree. There is another tree of the same name in the mountains of Cuauhnahuac, which is somewhat like it; but this one has round and rough leaves, a thick bark, and a fragrant root. [p. 68] impervious to water. From the Ule liquefied by fire a medicinal oil is

Bk. I.

drawn. This tree grows in hot lands, like those of Ihualapan and of

Mecatlán, and is common in the kingdom of Guatemala. (T)

The Quauhxiotl is a tree of middling size, whose leaves are round,

and the bark reddish. There are two subordinate species of it: one

yields a white gum, which, put in water, tinges it a milky color; the

other distills a reddish gum, both most useful for dysentery.

In this class of plants there should find a place the fir, the higuerilla

(a plant like the fig tree), and the ocote, a certain species of very aro‐

matic pine, for the oil they yield; and brazil-wood, logwood or

Campeachy wood, indigo, and many others for their juices; but sever‐

al of these plants are already known in Europe, and of others we shall

have occasion to speak elsewhere.

That small part of the vegetable kingdom of Anahuac which we

have so far set forth renews in us the sorrow we feel at seeing van‐

ished, and for the most part lost, that exact knowledge of natural his‐

tory which the ancient Mexicans had acquired. We know that those

woods, those mountains, and those valleys are full of infinite plants

most useful and precious, without finding anyone who is willing to

turn his gaze to examine them. Whom will it not grieve that of the

immense treasures drawn during the last two centuries and a half

from those richest mines, no part has been set aside to found acad‐

emies of naturalists, who, following in the footsteps of the most dis‐

tinguished Hernández, might discover for the benefit of society those

precious gifts which the Creator has so liberally bestowed upon

them?

§ 10.    The animal kingdom is no less unknown, despite the diligence Quadrupeds of the kingdom of Mexico. which Dr. Hernández applied in this part also. The difficulty there is

in distinguishing the species, and

the

(T) In Michoacán there is a tree called by the Tarascans Tarantaqua, of the same

species as the Olquahuitl, but different in the leaves. [p. 69] the impropriety of the nomenclature caused by analogy, have made

Bk. I. the history of animals difficult and arduous. The first Spanish nomenclators, more versed in the art of war than in the study of nature, instead of retaining, as would have been better, the names which the Mexicans gave to their animals, called several animals of very different species tigers, wolves, bears, dogs, squirrels, etc., either from the similarity of the color of the skin, or of other features, or from conformity in certain operations and properties. Now I do not pretend to correct their errors, nor even to illustrate the natural history of that vast kingdom; but only to give my readers some idea of the quadrupeds, the birds, the reptiles, the fish, and the insects that live on the land and in the waters of Anahuac.

Among the quadrupeds some are new, and others ancient. The new ones (so we call those which in the sixteenth century were carried to that land from the Canaries and from Europe) are the horses, the asses, the bulls, the sheep, the goats, the hogs, the dogs, and the cats, all of which have multiplied as happily and as greatly as we shall show in our Fourth Dissertation against certain philosophers of the age, who have taken it upon themselves to persuade us of the degradation of all the quadrupeds in the New World.

Of the ancient quadrupeds, that is, of those which from time immemorial have bred in that land, some were common to both continents, others peculiar to the New World, but yet common to Mexico and to other countries of North or South America, and others finally proper only to the kingdom of Mexico.

The ancient quadrupeds common to Mexico and to the old continent are the lions, the tigers, the wild cats, the bears, the wolves, the foxes, the deer, both the common and the white (U), the fallow deer, the wild goats, the badgers, the beech martens, the

weasels

(U) The white deer, whether of the same species or of a species distinct from the other deer, are undoubtedly common to both continents [p. 70] weasels, the martens, the squirrels, the polatouches, the rabbits, the Bk. I.

hares, the otters, and the mice. I know well that M. de Buffon denies

to America the lions, the tigers, and the rabbits; but since his opinion

rests chiefly on the weak foundation of the alleged impossibility of

the passage to the new continent of animals proper to the hot lands

of the old world, and is sufficiently contested by us in our disserta‐

tions, there is no need to interrupt the course of our History to re‐

fute it.

The Miztli of the Mexicans is nothing other than the maneless lion

mentioned by Pliny, (V) and quite distinct from the lion of Africa;

and the Ocelotl is not different from the African tiger, as Dr.

Hernández, who knew both, bears witness. The Tochtli of Mexico is

the same rabbit as that of the old continent, and at least as ancient in

that country as the calendar of the Mexicans, in which the figure of

the rabbit was the first character of their years. The wild cats, much

larger than the domestic ones, are very fierce and feared. The bears

are all black, and more corpulent than those that are seen in Italy,

brought from the Alps. The hares are distinguished from the

European by having longer ears, and the wolves by having a larger

head. Both species abound too much in that land. Polatouche we call,

with M. de Buffon, the Quimichpatlan, or flying mouse of the

Mexicans. They called it mouse because it is like one in the head,

though larger; and flying, because, having in its natural state the skin

of the flanks loose and wrinkled, when it wants to make a violent leap

from one tree to another, it stretches

and

They were known to the Greeks and to the Romans. The Mexicans called them

kings of the deer. M. de Buffon would persuade us that whiteness in deer is an effect

of their servitude; but since there are found in the mountains of New Spain, as in

fact there are, white deer that have never been slaves of men, such a thought no

longer has any place.

(V) Pliny, bk. 8, ch. 16, distinguishes the two species of lions, with mane and with‐

out mane, and gives the number of lions of each species that Pompey presented in

the Roman spectacles. [p. 71] and spreads out, together with its feet, that skin like wings. The Spanish common people confuse this quadruped with the squirrel be‐ Bk. I. cause of their resemblance; but they are certainly different. The rats were brought to Mexico by European ships; not so the mice, which were always known to the Mexicans under the name of Quimichin, which they also gave metaphorically to their spies. (*)

The species of quadrupeds that were in the kingdom of Mexico, common also to other countries of the New World, are the Cojametl, the Epatl, several species of monkeys, comprised by the Spaniards under the generic name of Monos, the Ajotochtli, the Aztacojotl, the Tlacuatzin, the Techichi, the Tlalmototli, the Techallotl, the Amiztli, the Mapach, and the Danta (X).

The Cojametl, to which the Spaniards give, because of its resemblance to the boar, the name of Javali, or wild hog, is called in other countries of America Pecar, Saino, and Tayassù. That gland which it has in a cavity of the back, from which a watery and stinking liquid abundantly distills, led the first historians of America, and from them many other authors, into the error of believing that there were found in America hogs which had the navel on the back; and even now there are those who believe so, although it is now more than two centuries

since

(*) Although in the Tuscan language the names Topo and Sorcio are used indiscriminately, as in Latin the names Mus and Sorex, there being nevertheless two species, as M. de Buffon says, the one larger and fiercer, the other smaller and more domestic, we give to the latter the name Sorcio (Sorex), and to the former the name Topo (Mus), in order to distinguish them. The Spaniards distinguish the species well, calling the large one Rata and the small one Raton: the latter was in America, the other was brought from Europe, and has in Peru the name of Pericote.

(X) Several authors count among the animals of Mexico the Paco, or Peruvian sheep, the Huanaco, the vicuña, the taruca, and the sloth; but all these quadrupeds are proper to South America, and none of them to North America. It is true that Dr. Hernández makes mention among the quadrupeds of New Spain of the Paco, gives its portrait, and uses the Mexican name Pelonichcatl; but he did so on account of some individuals brought there from Peru, to which the Mexicans gave that name, as he also describes several animals of the Philippine Islands; yet they have never been bred in Mexico, nor are they found in any country of North America, unless perhaps some individual taken there out of curiosity, as one has been taken to Europe. [p. 72] since the error was discovered through the anatomy of that animal. Bk. I. So hard is it to uproot popular opinions! The flesh of the Cojametl is

good to eat, provided that as soon as it is killed the gland is cut out

and all that stinking liquid is removed from the back; for otherwise

the whole of the flesh would be tainted.

The Epatl, called Zorrillo by the Spaniards, is less known in Europe

for the beauty of its skin than for the insufferable stench it gives off

when the hunters are upon it. (Y)

The Tlacuatzin, which in other countries has the names Churcha,

Sarigua, and Opossum, has been described by many authors, and is

quite famous for the doubled skin which the female has on her belly,

from the beginning of the stomach to the orifice of the womb; it cov‐

ers her teats and has in the middle an opening, where she receives her

young after bearing them, to keep them well guarded and to suckle

them. In walking, and in climbing the walls of houses, she holds the

skin stretched out and the opening shut, so that the young cannot get

out; but when she wishes to send them out, that they may begin to

seek their own food, or to bring them in again, whether to suckle

them or to snatch them from some danger, she opens the passage by

loosening the skin, counterfeiting pregnancy while she carries them

and birth every time she sets them out. This curious quadruped is the

destroyer of poultry-yards.

The Ajotochtli, called Armadillo or Encobertado by the Spaniards,

and by others Tatú, is also well known to Europeans

for

(Y) M. de Buffon counts four species of Epatl under the generic name of

Mouffettes. He then says that the first two, which he calls Coaso and Conepata, belong

to North America, and the Chincho and the Zorrillo, which are the other two, to

South America. We find no reason to believe them four different species, but only

four breeds of one and the same species. The names which the Mexicans give to the

first two breeds are Izquiepatl and Conepatl, which breeds are distinguished only in

size and color. The name Coaso or Squash, taken from the traveler Dampier, who af‐

firms that it is common in New Spain, has never been heard in that whole land. The

Indians of Yucatán, where the said traveler stayed, call that quadruped Pai. [p. 73] for those bony plates with which it keeps its back covered, in imitation of the ancient armor of horses. The Mexicans gave it that name Bk. I. for the resemblance, though imperfect, which it bears to the rabbit when it puts out its head, and to the gourd when it takes shelter beneath its plates, or shells; (Z) but it resembles no other animal more than the tortoise, although in several features it is not a little unlike it: one might give it the name of testaceous quadruped. Whenever this animal finds itself pursued on some plain, it has no way of escaping from the hands of the hunter; but since it ordinarily inhabits the mountains, where it finds some slope, it curls up, makes itself into a ball, and, rolling down the hillside, leaves the hunter mocked.

The Techichi, which elsewhere had the name of Alco, was a quadruped of Mexico and of other countries of America, which, because it was like a small dog in shape, was called Perro, that is, dog, by the Spaniards. It was of a melancholy aspect and altogether mute: whence arose the fable, spread by several authors still living, that all the dogs carried over from the old world become mute in the new. The flesh of the Techichi was eaten by the Mexicans, and, if we give credit to the Spaniards who still ate it, was of good taste and nourishing. The Spaniards after the Conquest of Mexico, having as yet neither herds nor flocks, supplied their slaughterhouses with these quadrupeds; so that in a short time they consumed the species, although it was numerous.

The Tlalmototli, or ground squirrel, called the Swiss by M. de Buffon, resembles the true squirrel in the eyes, the tail, the swiftness, and in all its movements

History of Mexico, Vol. I.                          K                          but

(Z) Ajotochtli is a word composed of Ajotli, Gourd, and Tochtli, Rabbit. M. de Buffon counts eight species of it under the name of Tatous, taking their diversity from the number of plates and movable bands that cover them. I could not say how many species there are in Mexico, since I have seen few individuals, and at that time, as I was not thinking of writing on this subject, I was not curious to count the plates and bands, nor do I know whether anyone has taken this trouble. [p. 74] but it is very unlike it in color, in size, in dwelling, and in some propBk. I.

erty. The hair of its belly is all white, and elsewhere white mixed with

gray. Its size is double that of the squirrel, and it does not live, as the

squirrel does, in trees, but in the small burrows which it digs in the

ground, or among the stones of the bulwarks that surround the fields,

in which it does considerable damage because of the great quantity of

grain that it steals from them. It bites furiously anyone who ap‐

proaches, and is incapable of being tamed; but for the rest it has ele‐

gance of form and grace in its movements. The species of these

quadrupeds is among the most numerous, especially in the kingdom

of Michoacán. The Techallotl is hardly to be distinguished from the

aforesaid animals, except in having a smaller and less hairy tail.

The Amiztli, or water lion, is an amphibious quadruped that lives

on the shores of the Pacific Ocean and in some rivers of that king‐

dom. Its body is three feet long, and its tail two. Its muzzle is long, its

legs short, and its claws curved. Its skin is valuable for the length and

softness of the hair. (aa)

The Mapach of the Mexicans is, in the opinion of M. de Buffon,

the very same quadruped as is known in Jamaica by the name of

Ratton. The Mexican one is the size of a badger, with a black head, a

long and slender muzzle like that of the greyhound, small ears, a

rounded body, hair variegated with black and white, a long and well-

furred tail, and five toes on each foot. Above each eye it has a little

white band, and, like the squirrel, it uses its paws to carry to its

mouth what it wishes to eat. It feeds indifferently on grain, fruit, in‐

sects, lizards, and the blood of hens. It is easily tamed, and is very

graceful in its play; but

it

(aa) We count the Amiztli among the quadrupeds common to other countries of

America, because it seems to us to be the very one that M. de Buffon describes for us

under the name of Saricovienne. [p. 75] it is treacherous like the squirrel itself, and is apt to bite its master.

Bk. I.

The Danta, or Anta, or Beori, or Tapir (for it has various names in various countries) is the bulkiest quadruped of all that were in the lands of the Mexican kingdom, (bb) and the one that comes nearest to the hippopotamus, not only in size but in certain features and in some properties. The Danta is as large as a middling mule. Its body is somewhat arched like that of the hog; its head is thick and long, with an appendage in the skin of the upper lip, which it widens or shortens at will; its eyes are small, its ears small and round, its legs short, its forefeet have four hooves and its hind feet three, its tail is short and pyramidal; its skin is very thick and clothed with dense hair, which in maturity is brown. Its dentition, made up of twenty molars and as many incisors, is so strong and sharp, and the bites it inflicts are so terrible, that it has been seen, as the historian Oviedo attests as an eyewitness, to tear away at one stroke of its teeth a span or two of skin from a hunting dog, and from another a whole leg with the thigh. Its flesh is edible, (cc) and its skin valuable, since it is so strong that it resists not only arrows but even musket balls. This quadruped inhabits the solitary woods of the hot lands, near some river or little lake, since it lives no less in the water than on land.

All the species of monkeys that are in that kingdom are known to the Mexicans under the general name of Ozomatli, and to the Spaniards under that of Monos. There are some of various sizes and shapes: small ones and singularly

K2                    graceful

(bb) The Danta is much smaller than the Tlacaxolotl described by Dr. Hernández; but we do not know that this great quadruped has ever been in the lands of the Mexican kingdom. The same must be said of the deer of New Mexico and of the cíbolos, or bison, which are likewise larger than the Danta. On this see our Fourth Dissertation.

(cc) Oviedo says that the legs of the Danta are very good and savory nourishment, provided that they are kept at the fire for twenty-four hours without interruption. [p. 76] graceful ones; middling ones, of the bulk of a badger; and large, Bk. I.

strong, fierce, and bearded ones, which some call Zambos. These,

when they stand erect, as they do, on two feet, sometimes equal the

stature of a man. Among the middling ones there are some which, be‐

cause they have the head of a dog, belong to the class of the cyno‐

cephali, although all of them are furnished with tails. (dd)

As for the anteaters, those quadrupeds so singular for the enor‐

mous length of their snout, the narrowness of their throat, and their

immense tongue, which they use to draw ants out of the anthills,

whence they got their name, I have never seen them in that kingdom,

nor do I know that there are any there; but I believe that the

Aztacojotl, that is, ant-eating coyote, mentioned but not described by

Dr. Hernández, is of no other species. (ee)

The quadrupeds most peculiar to the land of Anahuac, whose

species I do not know to be found in South America, nor in other

countries of North America not subject to the Crown of Spain, were

the Cojotl, the Tlalcojotl, the Xoloitzcuintli, the Tepeitzcuintli, the

Itzcuintepotzotli, the Ocotochtli, the Cojopollin, the Tuza, the Ahuitzotl,

the Huitztlacuatzin, and others perhaps unknown to us.

The Cojotl, or Coyote as the Spaniards call it, is a wild beast resem‐

bling the wolf in voracity, the fox in cunning, the dog in form, and in

some property the Adive, and

the

(dd) The Cynocephalos of the old continent has no tail, as everyone knows. Now

since monkeys have been found in the New World which have the head of a dog and

are furnished with tails, M. Brisson, in the division that he makes of the monkeys,

rightly gives those of this class the name of Cynocephali Cercopitheci, and distinguishes

two species of them. M. de Buffon, among the many species of monkeys that he de‐

scribes, omits these.

(ee) We call anteaters those quadrupeds which the Spaniards call Hormigueros and

the French Fourmiliers; but the ant-bears described by Oviedo are without doubt dif‐

ferent from the Fourmiliers of M. de Buffon; and although they agree in feeding on

ants and in the enormity of the tongue and snout, they nevertheless differ notably

with regard to the tail: for those of M. de Buffon have it immense, while those of

Oviedo are entirely without it. The description that Oviedo gives of their manner of

hunting ants is singularly curious. [p. 77] the jackal; whence several historians of Mexico have assigned it now to one species, now to another; but it is nonetheless indubitably dif‐ Bk. I. ferent from all of these, as we demonstrate in our dissertations. It is smaller than the wolf, and as large as a mastiff, but leaner. It has yellow, sparkling eyes, small, pointed, erect ears, a blackish muzzle, strong legs, feet armed with thick, curved claws, a thick and hairy tail, and a skin variegated with black, dusky brown, and white. Its voice has something of the howl of the wolf and of the barking of the dog. The Coyote is among the most common quadrupeds in Mexico, (ff) and among those most harmful to flocks. It assails a herd, and where it finds no lamb to carry off, it seizes a sheep by the neck with its teeth, and, joined to it in this way and beating its rump with its tail, leads it where it wishes. It pursues deer, and sometimes even attacks men. In flight it commonly does nothing but trot; but this trot is so lively and so swift that a horse at the gallop can hardly overtake it. The Cuetlachcojotl seems to us a quadruped of the same species as the Coyote; for it is not distinguished from it except by having a thicker neck and hair like that of the wolf.

The Tlalcojotl, or Tlalcoyote, is the size of a middling dog, but stouter, and is in our opinion the bulkiest of all the quadrupeds that live underground. In the head it somewhat resembles the cat, and the lion in color and in the length of its hair. It has a long, bushy tail, and feeds on hens and other small animals, which it hunts in the darkness of the night.

The Itzcuintepotzotli, the Tepeitzcuintli, and the Xoloitzcuintli were three species of quadrupeds resembling dogs. The Itzcuintepotzotli, or humpbacked dog, was as large as a Maltese dog, and its skin was variegated with white, tawny, and

black

(ff) Neither M. de Buffon nor M. de Bomare makes mention of the Coyote, although its species is the most common among wild beasts and the most numerous in Mexico, and is sufficiently described by Dr. Hernández, whose Natural History they often cite. [p. 78] black. Its head was small in proportion to the body, and seemed Bk. I. joined directly to it because of the smallness and thickness of the

neck; its eyes were pleasant, its ears drooping, its nose had a consider‐

able prominence in the middle, and its tail was so small that it barely

reached to mid-leg; but the most peculiar thing was a great hump

that it had from the neck to the rump. The country where this

quadruped was most abundant was the kingdom of Michoacán,

where it was called Ahora. The Tepeitzcuintli, that is, mountain dog, is

a wild beast so small that it looks like a puppy, yet so bold that it at‐

tacks deer, and sometimes kills them. Its hair is long, as is the tail,

and the body black, but the head, neck, and breast white. (*) The

Xoloitzcuintli is larger than the two preceding, for there are some

whose body is as much as four feet long. Its face is that of a dog, but

its fangs those of a wolf, its ears erect, its neck thick, and its tail long.

The most singular thing about this animal is that it is entirely with‐

out hair, except upon the muzzle, where it has a few thick and twist‐

ed bristles. Its whole body is covered with a smooth, soft skin of ash-

gray color, but partly spotted with black and tawny. These three

species of quadrupeds have been entirely consumed, or at least few

individuals of them remain. (gg)

The Ocotochtli appears, to judge from the description that Dr.

Hernández gives of it, to belong to the class of wild cats; but certain

particulars which the said author adds, have

the

(*) M. de Buffon believes that the Tepeitzcuintli is nothing other than the glutton;

but in our Dissertations we refute this opinion.

(gg) Johann Faber, Lincean Academician, published at Rome a long and erudite

dissertation, in which he strove to prove that the Xoloitzcuintli is the same as the

wolf of Mexico: deceived without doubt by the original portrait of the Xoloitzcuintli

that was sent to Rome together with the other paintings of Hernández; but if he had

read the description which that learned naturalist gives of the animal in the book on

the quadrupeds of New Spain, he would have spared himself the labor he had in writ‐

ing that dissertation, and the expense in publishing it. Faber's error was adopted by

M. de Buffon. See our dissertations, where other mistakes of this great man are

pointed out. [p. 79] the air of a fable; certainly not because he wished to deceive us, but because he sometimes trusted too much to the information of others. (hh)                                        Bk. I.

The Cojopollin is a quadruped as large as an ordinary rat; but it has a thicker tail, and uses it in place of a hand. The muzzle and the ears are like those of a little pig; the ears are transparent, the legs and feet white, and the belly yellowish white. It lives and rears its young in trees. The young, when they are afraid, cling tightly to their mother.

The Tozan, or Tuza, is a quadruped equivalent to the mole of Europe, but very different. Its body, which is well formed, is seven or eight inches long. Its muzzle is like that of the rat, its ears small and round, and its tail short. Its mouth is armed with very strong teeth, and its feet furnished with strong, curved claws, with which it digs the earth and makes for itself the burrows where it lives. The Tuza is most pernicious to the fields because of the grain it steals, and to travelers because of the many burrows or holes it makes in the roads; for where, because of its poor sight, it does not find the first burrow, it makes another, thus multiplying the inconveniences and dangers for those who travel on horseback. It digs the earth with its paws and with two canine teeth, larger than the others, which it has in the upper jaw; in digging it puts the earth into two membranes made in the manner of pouches, which it has beneath the ears, furnished with the muscles necessary for their contraction and dilation. When it has filled the membranes, it empties them by shaking the bottoms of the membranes with its paws, and then returns to dig in the same manner, using in this labor the canine teeth, and the claws in place of a hoe, and the membranes

in

(hh) Dr. Hernández says that when the Ocotochtli makes a kill, it covers it with foliage, and then, climbing a nearby tree, begins to utter howls as if inviting the other animals to eat of its prey; and that in fact the last to eat is the Ocotochtli itself; for such is the poison of its tongue that, if it ate first, the prey would be infected and the other animals would die in eating it. This fable is still heard from the mouth of the common people. [p. 80] in place of little sacks or baskets. The species of the Tuzas is most nuBk. I. merous; but I do not remember ever having seen them in the places

where the ground squirrels live.

The Ahuitzotl is an amphibious quadruped, which for the most

part lives in the rivers of the hot lands. Its body is a foot long, its

muzzle long and pointed, and its tail large. Its skin is variegated with

the colors black and brown.

The Huitztlacuatzin is the porcupine, or spiny hog, of Mexico. It is

as large as a middling dog, which it resembles in the face, although its

muzzle is flattened; its feet and legs are quite thick, and its tail is pro‐

portioned to the size of the body. Its whole body, except the belly, the

hinder part of the tail, and the inner part of the legs, is armed with

hollow, sharp quills or spines, four finger-breadths long. On the muz‐

zle and the forehead it has long, erect bristles, which rise above the

head in the manner of a plume. Its whole skin, even between the

spines, is covered with soft black hair. It feeds only on the fruits of

the earth. (ii)

The Cacomiztle is a quadruped very much like the beech marten in

its manner of living. It has the size and form of a common cat; but its

body is thicker, its hair longer, its legs shorter, and its aspect wilder

and fiercer. Its voice is a shrill cry, and its food hens and other small

animals. It lives and rears its young in the less frequented parts of

houses. By day it sees little, and does not come out of its hiding place

except at night to procure its food. Both the Cacomiztle and the

Tlacuatzin are found even in the houses of the capital. (jj)

Besides

(ii) M. de Buffon holds that the Huitztlacuatzin is the Coendou of Guiana; but the

Coendou is carnivorous, and the Huitztlacuatzin feeds on fruits; the Coendou has not

that plume of bristles which is seen on the Huitztlacuatzin, etc.

(jj) I do not know the true Mexican name of the Cacomiztle, and so I use the one

that the Spaniards give it in that kingdom. Dr. Hernández makes no mention of this

quadruped. It is true that he describes one under the name of Cacamiztli; but this is a

manifest error of the press, or else of the

[p. 81]

Besides these quadrupeds there were others in the Mexican empire of which I do not know whether they ought to be counted Bk. I. among the animals proper to that land or among those common to other American countries, such as the Itzcuincuani, that is, eater of dogs, the Tlalmiztli, a small lion, and the Tlalocelotl, a small tiger. Of the others, which, though they did not belong to the kingdom of Mexico, are found in other countries of North America subject to the Spaniards, we make mention in our dissertations.

The birds would give us more trouble than the quadrupeds if we § 11. were to undertake to enumerate their species and to describe their Birds        of the kingdom

of Mexico. form and character. Their abundance and variety, and their excellence, have led some authors to say that Mexico is the kingdom of birds, as Africa is that of wild beasts. Dr. Hernández in his Natural History describes more than two hundred species peculiar to that country, and yet he leaves out several worthy of record, such as the Cuitlacochi, the Zacua, and the Madrugador. We shall content ourselves with running through a few classes, pointing out some particular feature where occasion calls for it. Among the birds of prey there are kestrels, goshawks, and several species of eagles, falcons, and sparrowhawks. To the birds of this class the aforesaid naturalist grants superiority over those of Europe. Because of the well-known excellence of the Mexican falcons, Philip II, king of Spain, ordered that a hundred of them be brought to his court every year. Among the eagles the largest, the handsomest, and the most renowned is the one the Mexicans call Itzquauhtli, which hunts not only the largest birds and hares, but attacks even men and wild beasts. Of the kestrels there are two species: the one called Cenotzqui is very handsome.

History of Mexico, Vol. I.                          L                          The Roman academicians who saw to the edition of Hernández; for it should be written Zacamiztli. Now this quadruped belongs to Pánuco, and the Cacomiztle to Mexico: the Zacamiztli lives in the countryside, and the Cacomiztle inside the houses of the cities. The Zacamiztli is a Castilian braccio in length, and the Cacomiztle is smaller.

[p. 82]

The ravens of Mexico, called Cacalotl by the Mexicans, are for the Bk. I. most part employed not in cleaning the fields of carrion, as else‐

where, but rather in stealing the grain from the ears. The task of

cleaning the fields is here chiefly reserved to the Zopilotes, known in

South America by the name of Gallinazos, by others by that of Auras,

and by some, finally, by the most improper one of ravens. (kk) There

are two species of these birds, very different from each other: that of

the true Zopilote, and that of the Cozcaquauhtli. Both are larger than

the raven. These two species agree in having a curved beak and claws,

and on the head, instead of feathers, a wrinkled membrane with a few

curly hairs. In flight they rise to such a height that, though so large,

they pass out of sight, and especially when a hailstorm is about to

come they are seen wheeling in great numbers beneath the high

clouds until they vanish in the distance. They feed on carrion, which

they see with their most keen eyes, or else sense with their very lively

sense of smell, from the greatest height, and then descend, forming

with majestic flight a great spiral line down to the carcass on which

they wish to feed. Both are almost mute. The two species are distin‐

guished in size, color, number, and some property. The true Zopilotes

have black feathers, and the head, beak, and feet brown; they often

go in troops, and spend the night together in the trees. (ll) Their

species is very numerous, and common to all climes. The species

of

(kk) Dr. Hernández himself found no difficulty in making the Zopilote a species of

raven; but these birds are too different in size, in the shape of the head, in flight, and

in voice. M. de Bomare says that the Aura is the Cosquauth of New Spain and the

Tropilot of the Indians; but the Cozcaquauhtli as well as the Tzopilotl are Mexican

names used by the Indians not to signify a single bird, but two different ones. There

are some who give one species the name of Aura, and the other that of Zopilote or

Gallinazo.

(ll) In the Zopilotes that general rule laid down by Pliny in bk. 9, ch. 19, is seen to

fail: “Birds with hooked talons do not at all flock together, and each preys for itself.”

It can be true only if it is understood of birds properly of prey, such as the eagles, vul‐

tures, falcons, sparrowhawks, etc. [p. 83] of the Cozcaquauhtli, on the contrary, is not numerous, and is proper to the hot climes. It is, moreover, larger than the Zopilote; it has the Bk. I. head and feet red, and the beak white at the tip and blood-colored for the rest. Its feathers are brown, except those of the neck and of the parts near the breast, which are reddish black; its wings are ash-gray underneath, and above variegated with black and tawny.

The Cozcaquauhtli is called by the Mexicans King of the Zopilotes, (mm) and they say that when both species gather to eat a carcass, the Zopilote never touches it before the Cozcaquauhtli has tasted it. The Zopilotes are, besides, birds most useful to that kingdom; for not only do they clean the land, but they also pursue and destroy the eggs of the crocodiles in the very sand where the females of those formidable amphibians lay them so that they may be hatched by the sun: so that the killing of such birds ought to be forbidden under penalties.

Among the night birds there are eagle owls, owlets, scops owls, and others, to which the bats may be added, although properly they do not belong to the class of birds. Bats abound in the hot and shady lands, where there are some that with terrible bites draw much blood from horses and other animals. In several excessively hot countries very large bats are found, but not so large

L2                            as

(mm) That bird which now has in New Spain the name of King of the Zopilotes seems different from the one we describe. That modern King of the Zopilotes is as large as a common eagle, robust, and of a majestic air, with strong talons, lively and handsome eyes, and fine black, white, and tawny feathers: the most singular thing is that fleshy growth of scarlet color which surrounds the neck like a collar, and covers the head like a little crown. So it was described to me by a capable and trustworthy person, who says he has seen three individuals of that species, and in particular the one that in 1750 was sent from Mexico to the Catholic King Ferdinand VI. He says besides that the portrait of a bird published in the work entitled Il Gazzettiere americano is true to life. The Mexican name Cozcaquauhtli, which means eagle with a collar, really suits this one more than the other. The image of this bird, which is seen among our figures, is a copy of that in the Gazzettiere americano. [p. 84] as those of the Philippine Islands and of other eastern regions. Bk. I.

Among the water birds we wish to count not only the web-footed

birds (palmipedes), which swim and commonly live in the water, but

also the long-shanked waders (himantopodes), and other fishers,

which live for the most part on the shores of the sea, the lakes, and

the rivers, and find their food in the water. In this order of birds there

is a prodigious number of geese, at least twenty species of ducks, sev‐

eral kinds of herons and egrets, very many swans, gulls, moorhens,

cormorants, halcyons, kingfishers, pelicans, and others. The multitude

of the ducks is so great that at times they cover the fields, and seen

from some distance they look like flocks of grazing sheep. Among the

egrets and herons there are gray ones, wholly white ones, and others

which, having the feathers of the body white, have the neck, the ex‐

tremities, the front part of the wings, and a part of the tail adorned

with a vivid scarlet color or a beautiful blue. The pelican, or onocro‐

talus, known to the Spaniards of Mexico by the name of Alcatraz, is

well known for that enormous pouch, or belly, as Pliny calls it, which

it has under the beak. There are two species of it in Mexico: one has

the beak smooth, and the other has it toothed. I do not know

whether, as this bird is known to Europeans, so is known its rare prop‐

erty of succoring the disabled individuals of its species, which some

Americans make use of to provide themselves with fish without labor.

They take a pelican alive, break one of its wings, and, tying it to a tree,

lie in ambush in a place nearby, where they wait for the arrival of the

other pelicans with their provision, and when they have seen them

throw the fish from the pouch, they run up at once, and, leaving the

prisoner a part of it, carry off the rest.

But if the pelican is worthy of admiration for its providence toward

others of its species, no less admirable is the Toalquachilli for the arms

with which the Creator has furnished it for its defense. This is a small

water bird with

a [p. 85] a long, slender neck, a small head, a long yellow beak, long legs, feet, and claws, and a short tail. The color of its legs and feet is ash-gray, Bk. I. and that of its body black, with some yellow feathers near the belly. On its head it has a little circlet or coronet of horny substance, divided into three very sharp points, and it has two more on the front part of the wings. (nn)

In the other classes of birds there are some valued for their flesh, others for their feathers, others for their song or their voice, and others finally for their instinct, or for some notable property of theirs which may interest our curiosity.

As to the birds whose flesh is wholesome and pleasant nourishment, I have counted more than seventy species. Besides the common hens carried from the Canary Islands to the Antilles, and thence to Mexico, there were and are hens proper to that country, which, because they are in part like the common hens and in part like peafowl, were called Gallipavos by the Spaniards, and Huexolotl and Totolin by the Mexicans. These birds, carried to Europe in return for the hens, have multiplied excessively, particularly in Italy, where, in view of their character and their size, they were given the name of turkeys; (*) but the multiplication of the European hens in Mexico has been far greater. There are also in great abundance wild turkeys, everywhere like the domestic ones, but larger, and in many countries of more savory flesh. There are partridges, quails, pheasants, cranes, turtledoves, doves, and very many others esteemed in Europe. The prodigious number of the quails may be known from what we shall say when we come to speak of the ancient sacrifices. The birds known there by the name of pheasants are of three species different from the

European

(nn) There is in Brazil a bird, also aquatic, which has weapons similar to those of the Toalquachilli; but in the rest it is very different.

(*) Here in Bologna they are called Tocchi and Tocchini, and elsewhere Galli d’India. The French call them Dindes, Dindons, and Coqs-d’Inde.

[p. 86]

European pheasants. (oo) The Coxolitli and the Tepetototl, both the Bk. I.

size of a goose and with a crest on the head which they spread and

fold at will, are distinguished from each other in color and in some

property. The Coxolitli, called royal pheasant by the Spaniards, has

tawny feathers, and its flesh is more delicate. The Tepetototl grows so

tame that it takes food from its master’s hand, comes to meet him

when it sees him enter the house, making demonstrations of joy,

learns to knock at the door with its beak, and in all things shows it‐

self more docile than might have been expected of a bird native to

the woods. I saw one of these pheasants which, having been for some

time in a poultry yard, learned the manner of fighting of the cocks,

and fought with them raising the feathers of its crest, as the cocks

raise those of the neck. It has black, shining feathers, and the legs

and feet ashen. The pheasants of the third species, called Gritones by

the Spaniards, that is, Criers, are smaller than the others, and have

the tail and wings black, and the rest of the body brown. The

Chachalaca, whose flesh is also very good to eat, is as large as a hen.

The upper part of its body is brown, the lower whitish, and the beak

and feet bluish. The noise that these birds make in the woods with

their cries is incredible; though similar to those of hens, they are

louder, more continuous, and more annoying. Of turtledoves and

doves there are several species, some common to Europe and others

proper to those countries.

The birds valued for their feathers are so many, and so beautiful,

that we should give our readers great pleasure if we could represent

them to their eyes with all the colors that adorn their feathers. I have

counted as many as thirty-five species of Mexican birds exceedingly

beautiful, of which some will have to be mentioned by us.

The

(oo) M. de Bomare counts the Huatzin among the pheasants; but I do not know

why, since this Mexican bird belongs to the second class of the birds of prey, with the

ravens, the Zopilotes, and others.

[p. 87]

The Huitzitzilin is that marvelous little bird so celebrated by the historians of America for its smallness, its mobility, the singular beau‐ Bk. I. ty of its little feathers, the slightness of its food, and the length of its sleep in winter. This sleep, or rather this immobility, caused by the stiffness or numbness of its limbs, has been judicially established several times to convince the incredulity of some Europeans: an incredulity in truth caused by ignorance; for the same immobility is seen in several countries of Europe in dormice, hedgehogs, swallows, bats, and other animals whose blood is equally cold, although in no other is it perhaps so long as in the Huitzitzilin, since this little bird remains in some countries deprived of all movement from October until April. As many as nine species of Huitzitzilin are counted, differing in size and in colors. (pp)

The Tlauhquechol is a fairly large water bird, which has its feathers dyed a most beautiful scarlet color, or a reddish white, except those of the neck, which are black. It lives on the shores of the sea and of rivers, and eats nothing but live little fish, never touching dead flesh.

The Nepapantototl is a wild duck, which frequents the Mexican lake, and in whose feathers all the colors are seen gathered.

The Tlacuiloltototl, that is, painted bird, truly deserves the name; for its most beautiful variegated plumes are red, blue, purple, green, and black. It has black eyes with a yellow iris, and ash-gray feet.

The Tzinizcan is the size of a dove. It has

the

(pp) The Spaniards of Mexico call it Chupamirto, because it sucks particularly the flowers of a plant which is known there by the most improper name of Myrtle. In other countries of America they give it the names of Chupaflor, Picaflor, Tominejo, Colibre, etc. Among so many authors who describe this precious little bird, none gives a better idea of the beauty of its feathers than Father Acosta. [p. 88] the beak small, curved, and yellow; the head and neck like those of Bk. I. the dove, but adorned with green and shining feathers; the breast and

belly red, except in the part nearest the tail, which is variegated with

white and blue; the tail green above and black beneath, the wings

## partly black and partly white, and the eyes black with a reddish-yel‐

low iris. This beautiful bird lives in the maritime lands.

The Mezcanauhtli is a wild duck, the size of the moorhen, but of

singular beauty. It has a moderately long and broad beak, blue above

and black beneath; the feathers of the body white, but spotted with

many black points. Its wings are white and brown beneath, and above

variegated with black, white, blue, green, and tawny. Its feet are red‐

dish yellow, its head partly brown, partly tawny, and partly purple,

with a beautiful white spot between the beak and the eyes, which are

black. Its tail is blue above, brown beneath, and white at the tip.

The Tlauhtototl is very like the Tlacuiloltototl in its colors, but

smaller. The macaws and the cardinals, so prized by Europeans for

their most beautiful colors, are very common in those countries.

All these beautiful birds, and others native to Mexico, or else

brought to Mexico from other neighboring countries, were highly es‐

teemed by the Mexicans for their singular works of mosaic, of which

we shall make mention elsewhere. From the old continent peafowl

were brought there, but through the carelessness of those peoples

they have multiplied very little.

Several authors who grant the Mexican birds superiority in the

beauty of their feathers deny them that of song; but we who have

heard both the one and the other are quite certain that such an opin‐

ion was dictated not by fairness but by ignorance; for it is harder for

Europeans to hear the Mexican birds than to see them.

There are also in Mexico the goldfinches and the renowned

nightingales [p. 89] nightingales, and besides at least twenty-two other species of songbirds, little or in no way inferior to those; but far surpassing all those Bk. I. we know is the most celebrated Centzontli, a name given to it by the Mexicans to express the surprising variety of its voices. (qq) It is impossible for us to give a complete idea of the sweetness and softness of its song, of the harmony and variety of its tones, and of the facility with which it learns to express whatever it hears. It imitates to the life not only the song of other birds, but even the different voices of quadrupeds. It is the size of a common thrush. The color of its body is white beneath and gray above, with some white feathers, especially near the tail and the head. It eats anything; but it takes particular delight in flies, which it takes with demonstrations of pleasure from the fingers of whoever offers them. The species of the Centzontli is everywhere among the most numerous; but although these birds are so common, they are so prized that I have seen twenty-five scudi paid for one. Attempts have often been made to carry it to Europe; but I do not know whether any has ever succeeded; and I am persuaded that even if it arrived alive in Europe it could never be without great detriment to its voice and its instinct, because of the discomforts of the voyage and the change of climate.

The birds called cardinals are no less pleasing to the ear for the melody of their song than to the sight for the beauty of their scarlet feathers and of their crest. The Mexican calandra also sings most sweetly, and its song resembles that of the nightingale. Its feathers are variegated with white, yellow, and gray. It weaves marvelously

History of Mexico, Vol. I.                           M                             its

(qq) Centzontlatole (this is the true name, and that of Centzontli is used only by abbreviation) means, that which has infinite voices. The Mexicans use the word Centzontli (four hundred) as the Latins use mille and sexcenta, to express an indefinite and innumerable multitude. The Greek Polyglotta, which some modern ornithologists give it, agrees with the Mexican name. See what we say about the Centzontli in the Dissertations. [p. 90] its nest with bristles, thickened and attached with some sort of sticky Bk. I.

material, hanging it like a purse or little bag from some branch of a

tree. The Tigrillo, that is, little tiger, which is also valued for its music,

has such a name because its feathers are spotted like the skin of the

tiger. The Cuitlaccochi is like the Centzontli no less in the size of the

body and the color of the feathers than in the excellence of its song,

just as the Coztototl is in all respects very like the canaries carried to

that country from the Canary Islands. The Mexican sparrows, called

Gorriones by the Spaniards, resemble true sparrows only in size, in

hopping as they walk, and in making their nests in holes in walls. The

Mexican ones have the lower part of the body white and the upper

gray; but when they reach a certain age, some have the head red and

others yellow. (*) Their flight is laborious, perhaps because of the

smallness of their wings or the weakness of their feathers. Their song

is very sweet and quite varied. There is a great abundance of these

singers in the capital and in other cities and villages of Mexico.

No less abundant in the country of Anahuac are the talkative

birds, or imitators of human speech. Among the songbirds themselves

there are some that learn a few words, such as the renowned

Centzontli, and the Acolchichi, that is, red-shouldered bird, to which,

for such a badge, the Spaniards gave the name of Commendatore. The

Cehuan, which is larger than a common thrush, mimics the human

voice, but in a tone that seems mocking, and follows travelers for a

long stretch. The Tzanahuei resembles the magpie in size, but differs

in color; it learns to speak, steals cautiously whatever it can, and in all

things shows an instinct superior to that commonly observed in other

birds.

But

(*) I have heard it said that the sparrows with the red head are the males, and

those with the yellow head the females.

[p. 91]

But among all the talking birds the first place belongs to the par‐

Bk. I. rots, of which four principal species are counted in Mexico: the Huacamaya, the Toznenetl, the Cochotl, and the Quiltototl. (rr)

The Huacamaya is more valued for its most beautiful plumage than for its voice. It pronounces words indistinctly, and its voice is coarse and disagreeable. This is the largest of all the parrots. The Toznenetl, which is the best of all, is the size of a dove; the color of its feathers is green, but on the head and the front part of the wings it is red in some and yellow in others. It learns as many words and songs as they teach it, and pronounces them clearly; it imitates to the life the laughter and the mocking tone of men, the weeping of children, and the voices of various animals. Of the Cochotl there are three subordinate species, differing in size and in colors, which in all are beautiful, and among which green predominates. The largest of the Cochotl is almost the size of the Toznenetl; the other two species, called catarinas by the Spaniards, are smaller. All learn to speak, though not so perfectly as the Toznenetl. The Quiltototl, which is the smallest, is also the least fit for speaking. These little parrots, whose feathers are dyed a most beautiful green, always go in numerous flocks, now making a great noise in the air, now ravaging the grain. When they are in the trees they are confused, because of their color, with the foliage. All the other parrots go for the most part in pairs, male and female.

The birds called Madrugadores, (*) which we might call Awakeners, and those which have among the Mexicans the name

M2                            of

(rr) The Toznenetl and the Cochotl are called Pericos and Loros by the Spaniards of Mexico. The name Huacamaya is from the Haitian language, which was spoken on the island of Hispaniola. Loro is a word taken from the Quichua, or Inca, language, and Toznenetl, Cochotl, and Quiltototl from the Mexican language.

(*) Madrugador in Spanish means one who gets up early. Since there is no equivalent word in the Tuscan language, we use “awakener,” which also suits it; but perhaps “twilight bird” would be more proper. [p. 92] of Tzacua, though they are not so valuable for the beauty of their Bk. I.

feathers or for the excellence of their song, are nonetheless worthy of

particular mention for their habits. The Awakeners are, among the di‐

urnal birds, the last to take their rest in the evening and the first to

leave it in the morning and to announce the return of the Sun. They

do not cease their song and their games until an hour after sunset,

and long before dawn they resume them, nor do they ever show

themselves so cheerful as during both twilights. About an hour before

dawn one of these birds, from the branches of a tree where it spent

the night in company with many others of its species, begins to call

them in a high and sonorous voice, and in a cheerful tone repeats the

call many times, until it hears first one and then another answer.

Then, when all are awake, they make a most festive noise, which is

heard from very far away. On the journeys I made through the king‐

dom of Michoacán, where they are most abundant, they were useful

to me; for they woke me in good time to make my departure at the

break of day. These birds are as large as sparrows.

The Tzacua, a bird resembling the aforementioned Calandra in

size, in colors, and in the building of its nest, is still more wonderful.

These birds live in society, and every tree is for them a village com‐

posed of a great multitude of nests, which are seen hanging from the

branches. A Tzacua that acts as Chief, or Guard of the Village, sits in

the middle of the tree, whence it often flies now to one nest, now to

another, and after singing a little returns to its post, and thus visits all

the nests, all the others meanwhile remaining silent. If it ever sees

some bird of another species coming toward the tree, it goes to meet

it, and with beak and wings strives to drive it off; but if it sees a man

approaching (and perhaps any other animal of formidable size), it flies

away crying to another tree nearby, and if meanwhile some Tzacuas of

the same village arrive from the fields, it goes to meet them

and [p. 93] and, changing the tone of its voice, it forces them to withdraw; but as soon as it sees the danger cease, it returns cheerfully to its customary Bk. I. visit of the nests. These things observed of the Tzacuas by a man who is perspicacious, learned, and truthful, (*) lead us to believe that still more surprising ones might be found, if the observations were repeated; but leaving now these objects so pleasing, let us turn our gaze to others, terrible ones.

The reptiles of Mexico may be reduced to two orders or classes, §Reptiles

12.

of Mexico. namely four-footed reptiles and apodal reptiles, or those without feet. (ss) In the first class are the crocodiles, the large lizards, the lizards, the frogs, and the toads, and in the second all the species of serpents.

The Mexican crocodiles are such as the African ones are in size, in shape, in voracity, in manner of living, and in all the other properties belonging to their character. They abound in several rivers and lakes of the hot lands, and are destructive to other animals, and even to men. A description of these fierce amphibians would be superfluous, since so much is found written of them.

Among the large lizards we count the Acaltetepon and the Iguana: the Acaltetepon, known to the Spanish common people by the very improper name of Scorpions, are two large lizards similar to each other in color and in shape, but different in size and in the tail. The smaller is about fifteen inches long, and has a long tail, short legs, a broad, cleft, red tongue, a gray and rough skin with little white bumps scattered everywhere, which look like pearls, a slow gait, and a fierce look. From the muscles of the hind legs to the tip of the tail it has the skin crossed

with

(*) The Abbé Don José Rafael Campoy, whom we praise elsewhere as he deserves.

(ss) I know very well the diversity of opinions among authors on deciding which animals ought to be comprised in the class of reptiles; but as I do not undertake to make an exact division of animals, but only to present them to readers with some order, I take the name of reptiles in the common signification which it had among our forebears. [p. 94] with yellow bands in the form of rings. Its bite is painful, but not Bk. I. mortal, as some think. It is peculiar to the hot countries. Of that

same clime is the other large lizard, but it is twice as large; for it is,

according to those who have seen it, about two and a half feet long,

and more than a foot in circumference at the belly and the back. Its

tail is short, and its head and legs thick. This large lizard is the

scourge of the rabbits.

The Iguana is an innocent large lizard, well known in Europe from

the accounts of the historians of America. They abound in the hot

lands, and there are two species of them, one terrestrial and the other

amphibious. Some are so large that they reach as much as three feet

in length. They are extremely swift in their course, and extremely ag‐

ile in climbing trees. Their flesh and their eggs are edible, and praised

by several authors; but the flesh is pernicious to those who are infect‐

ed with the French disease.

Of lizards there are innumerable species, differing in size, in col‐

ors, and in qualities; for some are venomous and others innocent.

Among the innocent ones the first place is due to the chameleon,

called by the Mexicans Quatapalcatl. This is almost wholly like the

common chameleon; but it differs in being without a crest, and in

having ears, which are large, round, and too open. Among the other

innocent lizards there is none worth recording, except the Tapayaxin,

(*) both for its shape and for other respects. It is perfectly orbicular,

cartilaginous, and feels very cold to the touch. The diameter of its

body is six finger-breadths. Its head is very hard, and spotted with

various colors. It is so slow and sluggish that even when shaken it

does not move. If they strike its head, or press its eyes, it squirts from

them, to a distance of two or three paces, a few drops of blood; but

otherwise it is an innocent animal, and shows pleasure in being

handled.

It

(*) See the image of this lizard among our figures.

[p. 95]

It may be believed that, being of so cold a temperament, it receives

Bk. I. some comfort from the warmth of the hand.

Among the venomous lizards the worst appears to be the one which, for its rarity, received from the Mexicans the name of Tetzauhqui. This one is very small, and of a gray color, which on its body is yellowish, and on its tail bluish. There are still others reputed venomous, and known to the Spaniards by the name of Salamanquesas, or by that of Scorpions (since the ignorant common people have given this name to several reptiles); but I have assured myself, after many observations, that such lizards are either wholly without venom, or, if perhaps they have any, it is not as active as is believed.

What we say of the lizards may also be said of the toads; for we have never seen or heard of any misfortune caused by their venom, although in several countries too hot and humid the ground is covered with them. In such lands are found toads so large that they measure as much as eight inches in diameter.

Of frogs there are in the lake of Chalco three very numerous species, differing in size and in colors, and very common on the tables of the capital. Those of the Huasteca are excellent, and so large that they weigh a Spanish pound. But I never saw or heard of tree frogs in that kingdom, which are so common both in Italy and in other countries of Europe.

The variety of serpents is much greater than that of the aforesaid reptiles, for there are large ones and small, many-colored ones and ones of a single color, venomous ones and innocent ones.

The one the Mexicans called Canauhcoatl seems to have been the most considerable in thickness. It was as much as three Paris perches long, and as thick as a man of ordinary size. A little smaller was one of the Tlilcoa, or black serpents, seen by Dr. Hernández in the mountains of Tepoztlán; for, having such a thickness, it was also ten Spanish cubits

long, [p. 96] long, or more than sixteen Paris feet; but nowadays serpents of such Bk. I.

bulk will hardly be found, unless it be in some solitary wood, very far

from the capital.

The most notable venomous serpents are the Ahueyactli, the

Cuicuilcoatl, the Coral, or Coralline, the Teixminani, the Cencoatl, and

the Teotlacozauhqui.

The Teotlacozauhqui, of whose kind there are several species, is the

famous rattlesnake. Its size is varied, as are its colors; but ordinarily it

is three or four feet long. The rattles may be considered as an ap‐

pendage of the vertebrae, and are sonorous rings of horny substance,

movable, and dependent on one another by means of articulations or

joints, each composed of three small bones. (tt) These rattles sound

every time the serpent moves, especially when it is agitated to bite. It

is very swift in its motion, and therefore it also received from the

Mexicans the name of Ehecacoatl, or Air Serpent. Its bite causes death

infallibly, unless one runs promptly to its remedies, among which it is

esteemed effective to keep the wounded part inside the earth for

some time. It bites with two canine teeth which it has in the upper

jaw, which are, as in the viper and in other species of serpents, mov‐

able, hollow, and perforated toward the point. The venom, that is,

that yellowish liquid, crystallizable and so pernicious, is contained in

the glands that are above the root of those two teeth. These glands,

compressed in biting, shoot the fatal liquor through the channels of

the teeth, and introduce it through the holes into the wound and into

the mass of the blood. We would gladly communicate to the public

several other observations made on this subject, if the condition of

this history allowed us. (uu)

The

(tt) Dr. Hernández says that this serpent has as many years as it has rattles, be‐

cause every year it gets one; but we do not know whether he says this on the strength

of his own observations, or rather on another's word.

(uu) Father Inama, Jesuit missionary of California, made many experiments [PDF 106] 1. Mexican Tiger. 2. Tlacocelotl. 3. Itzcuintepotzotli. 4. Mexican Porcupine. 5. King of

the Zopilotes.

6. Axolotl. 7. Occhione. 8. Tepajaxin. 9. Mexican Amphisbaena. 10. 11. Temolin.

[p. 97]

The Ahueyactli is little different from the one already described, but it has no rattles. This serpent communicates, according to what Bk. I. Hernández says, that kind of venom which was called by the Ancients Hemorrhoos, by which the wounded man discharges blood through the mouth, the nose, and the eyes, although such an action can be prevented with certain antidotes.

The Cuicuilcoatl, so called for the variety of its colors, is barely eight inches long, and as thick as the little finger; but its venom is as active as that of the Teotlacozauhqui.

The Teixminani is that sort of serpent which Pliny calls jaculus. It is long and slender, and has a gray back and a purplish belly. It always moves in a straight line, and never turns about. It darts from the trees at travelers, and from this it took its name. (vv)

There are some of these serpents in the mountains of Cuauhnahuac, and in other hot lands; but although I was so many years in that kingdom, I never learned that such a misfortune befell any traveler, and I can say the same of the terrible effects which the Ahueyactli causes.

The Cencoatl, (*) also venomous, is about five feet in length, and eight inches in circumference where it is thickest. The most singular thing about this serpent is that it shines in the dark. Thus the provident author of nature awakens our attention in various ways to defend ourselves from harm, now through hearing with the noise of the rattles, now through the eyes with the impression of light.

Among the innocent serpents, of which there are several species, we cannot omit the Tzicatlinan and the Maquizcoatl. The Tzicatlinan is very beautiful, more than a foot long, and as thick as

History of Mexico, Vol. I.                       N                          the on serpents, which confirm those that Mr. Mead made on vipers.

(vv) The Mexicans also give this serpent the name of Micoatl, and the Spaniards that of Saetilla: the one and the other mean the same as the jaculus of the Latins.

(*) There are other species of serpents which, being of the same color, have the same name of Cencoatl, but all are innocent. [p. 98] the little finger. It lives always in ant-hills, and finds itself so well with Bk. I. the ants that it often accompanies them on their journeys, and re‐

turns to its dwelling. The Mexican name Tzicatlinan means Mother of

the ants, and the Spaniards also call it so; but I suspect that this little

serpent's whole inclination for ant-hills is for no other reason than to

feed on the ants themselves.

The Maquizcoatl is of the same size, but all silvered and transpar‐

ent. Its tail is thicker than its head, and it moves indifferently in ei‐

ther direction, using the head for a tail and the tail for a head. This

little serpent, called by the Greeks Amphisbaena (xx), is very rare, nor

do I know that it has been seen elsewhere, except in the Valley of

Toluca.

Among so many species of serpents as are found in the little-fre‐

quented woods of that kingdom, I know of none so far discovered to

be viviparous, except the Acoatl, or water serpent, of which this is be‐

lieved, but not known. This one is about twenty inches long, and one

inch thick. Its teeth are very small: the upper part of its head is black,

the sides blue, and the lower yellow; the back streaked with black and

blue, and the belly entirely blue.

The ancient Mexicans, who delighted in raising every kind of ani‐

mal, and who by familiarity had driven the natural horror from their

minds, used to take in the fields a green and innocent little serpent to

raise at home, where, well fed, it would usually grow as thick as a

man. They kept it inside a tub, from which it did not come out ex‐

cept to take its food from its master's hand, and to climb onto his

shoulder, or else to wind itself about his feet.

If from the land we now turn our gaze to the waters of the

rivers

(xx) Pliny, bk. 8, ch. 23, gives two heads to the Amphisbaena; but the Greek name

signifies nothing else than movement indifferently in one direction or the other. In

Europe this two-headed serpent of Pliny has been seen, and some say that it is also

found in Mexico; but I do not know that anyone has seen it; and if it has ever been

seen, it must not be considered a regular species, but a monster, like the two-headed

eagle found a few years ago in Oaxaca, and thence sent to the Catholic King. [p. 99] rivers, the lakes, and the seas of Anahuac, we shall find in them a much greater number of animals. The known species of their fish are alto‐ Bk. I. gether innumerable; for of those alone that serve for the sustenance of man I have counted more than a hundred species, not counting the turtles, the crabs, the shrimps, nor any other testaceous or crustaceous animal. Of the fish, some are common to both seas, others are proper only to the Gulf of Mexico, others to the Pacific Ocean, and others finally to the rivers and lakes.

The fish common to both seas are: the whales, the dolphins, the

§ 13. swordfish, the sawfish, the sharks, the manatees, the mantas, the sea- Fish of the seas, wolves, the hogfish, the bonitos, the cod, the robalos, the pargos of three rivers, and lakes of
