Anahuac. species, the meros, the pámpanos, the grey mullets, the little doves, the rays or skates, the chuchos, the barbels, the humpbacks, the gilt-heads, the flying fish, the guitarfish, the cabrillas, the black and white sargos, the needlefish, the sphyraenas, the shads, the spiny lobsters, the soles, and very many others, as well as several species of turtles, octopuses, crabs, shrimps, sponges, etc.

The Gulf of Mexico, besides those aforesaid, has the sturgeons, the redfish, the parrot wrasses, the pikes, the conger eels, the damselfish, the stingrays, the kingfish, the turbots, the toadfish, the sea breams, the “blondes,” the plaice, the lanternfish, the dentex, the lampreys, the morays, the cuttlefish, the anchovies, the carpioni, the eels, the pompili, or nautili, and others.

The Pacific Ocean, besides those common to both seas, has the salmon, the tunnies, the cornudas, the barber-fish, the sand soles, the “goldfinches,” the horse mackerel, the corvinas, the “old wives,” the sardines, the Occhioni (“big-eyes”), the lizardfish, the parrotfish, the scorpion fish, the cockfish, the catfish, the herrings, the botetes, and others.

The rivers and lakes have whitefish of three or four species, the carp, the grey mullets, the trout, the red mullets, the bobos, the robalos, the barbels, the gilt-heads, the corvinas, the sargos, the eels, and several others. (yy)

N2                     The

(yy) Since among the fish mentioned by me there are some known to the Italians, and others altogether unknown to them, and therefore without a Tuscan name, I have observed [p. 100] The description of these fish, besides diverting us too far from the Bk. I. course of our History, would for the most part be useless to Italian

readers; so we shall have to content ourselves with stating some par‐

ticulars that may serve for the history of fish.

The shark belongs to that class of marine beasts which were called

by the Ancients caniculae. Its voracity is very well known, as also its

size, its strength, and its speed. It has two, three, and sometimes

more rows of teeth no less sharp than strong, and swallows whatever

is presented to it, whether it be edible or not. There has been found

at times in its belly a whole sheep's skin, and even a large butcher's

knife. It often accompanies ships, and there have been sharks that,

according to what Oviedo attests, accompanied for as much as five

hundred miles a ship that was going with the wind astern and sails

swollen, circling it often for love of the refuse that was thrown into

the sea.

The manatee, or Lamentino as it is called by some, is of a disposi‐

tion very different from that of the shark, and surpasses it in size.

Oviedo himself testifies that manatees were at times caught so large

that to transport one a cart with two pairs of oxen was needed. It is

viviparous, like the shark; but the female bears at each birth no more

than one little manatee, though a very large one. (zz) Its flesh is deli‐

cate and resembling that of veal.

Some

in naming them these rules. 1. To the known fish I give their own Tuscan name, as

whale, dolphin, sand sole, ray, shad, sphyraena, pike, stingray, mullet, plaice, anchovy, etc. 2.

To those fish which, although they have no proper name in the Tuscan language, can

nevertheless be expressed by a Tuscan name equivalent to the Spanish or Mexican, I

give such a name: thus I say blonde for Vermejuela, little dove for Palometa, toad for

Sapo, goldfinch for Sirguero, old wife for Vieja, parrot for Cochomichin (a Mexican name),

etc. 3. For those, then, that have neither a proper name nor can be expressed by an

equivalent Tuscan name, I use the same Spanish names, but conforming some of

them to the Tuscan manner, as Pampano, Roballo, Pargo, Bobo, Botetto for Botete, Ciuccio

for Chucho, etc.

(zz) M. de Buffon agrees with Dr. Hernández in saying that the manatee bears

only a single little manatee at a time; but others say that it bears two. It may [p. 101] Some authors place the manatee in the class of amphibians; but wrongly, since it never comes onto land; it merely puts its head and Bk. I. part of its body out of the water to bite the herbage that is on the banks of the rivers. (Aa)

The manta is that flat fish so dangerous to the pearl fishers, of which Ulloa and other authors make mention, and I do not doubt that it is the same one recorded by Pliny, but not well understood, under the name of Cloud, or Mist. (Bb) It may be believed that it passed from the seas of the old continent to those of the new, as other fish appear also to have passed. So great is the strength

which

be believed that the same happens to the female manatee as to woman, namely, that while ordinarily she has one fetus, extraordinarily she bears two or three. Dr. Hernández describes the coupling of such animals thus: It couples in the human manner, the female lying on her back, almost entirely on the shore, and the male coming upon her with a certain swiftness. We do not count the manatee, though viviparous, among the quadrupeds, as several modern naturalists do; because all the world understands by the name of quadruped an animal that goes on four feet, and the manatee has only two, and those shapeless.

(Aa) M. de la Condamine confirms what we say with regard to the manatee's living always in the water, and the same had been said two centuries earlier by Oviedo and Hernández, eyewitnesses both. It is true that Hernández seems to say quite the opposite; but it is an evident misprint, which anyone who reads the text can perceive. It should also be noted that the manatee, though properly marine, is frequently found in the rivers.

(Bb) The divers themselves report that a kind of cloud thickens over their heads, like flat fish, pressing upon them and preventing them from coming up again, and that for this reason they carry very sharp stilettos attached to lines; for unless it is pierced it does not withdraw, this being, as I think, the effect of darkness and terror. For no one has found among the animals any one called a cloud or mist (the name by which they call this evil). Pliny, Natural History, bk. 9, ch. 46. The account which these divers, or “cormorants,” gave of their Cloud is not different from what the divers of the seas of America say of their Manta, and the name of Cloud suits it, since it really looks like a cloud to those who are in the water beneath it, and even today swimmers carry long knives, or sharp sticks, to free themselves from such a beast. This observation, which escaped all the interpreters of Pliny, was made by my compatriot and friend the Abbé José Rafael Campoy, a person highly praiseworthy no less for his morals and his honor than for his eloquence and his erudition, especially in Latinity, in history, in criticism, and in geography. His death, which touches us too deeply, occurring on December 29, 1777, did not allow him to complete several works already begun, which would have been very useful. [p. 102] which this beast has in its muscles that it not only suffocates the man whom Bk. I. it embraces, or enfolds by bending itself, but has been seen to seize the cable

of a sloop and move it from the place where it was moored. It got the name

Manta; because when it spreads its body over the surface of the sea, as it of‐

ten does, it looks like a floating woolen coverlet.

The swordfish of those seas is very different from that of the sea of

Greenland. Its sword is larger, and more like in shape to the true sword of

iron, and it is not situated, as that of the Greenland one is, in the hind part,

but in the front of the body, like that of the sawfish, which it wields at its

will with great force, and uses as an offensive weapon.

Of the two species of sawfish that are in those seas, one is the common

one known to Pliny and described by many naturalists; the other, no longer

than a foot, has on its back a row of teeth, or spines, that looks like a saw,

whence it received from the Mexicans the name of Tlateconi, and from the

Spaniards that of Sierra.

The robalo is one of the most numerous species, and its flesh is one of the

most delicate foods, especially that of the river kind. Dr. Hernández believed

it to be the Lupus of the Ancients, and Campoy the Asellus minor; but these

are mere conjectures; for the marks that the Ancients left us of those fish are

so scanty that it is impossible to establish the identity.

The humpback (among the Spaniards Corcobado) was so called on account

of a hump, or prominence, which it has from the beginning of the head to

the mouth, which is very small. The sphyraena likewise received the name of

Picuda (which we might call Long-beak) because it has the lower jaw longer

than the upper.

The toadfish is a fish horrible to look at, black, perfectly round, and with‐

out scales, whose diameter is three or four inches. Its flesh is tasty and

wholesome.

Among the needlefish there is one called by the Mexicans Huitzitzilmichin,

three [p. 103] three feet long, but very slender. Its body is covered with certain little plates instead of scales. Its snout is eight inches long, and is longer in Bk. I. the upper part, the opposite of the other needlefish, which it surpasses no less in the taste of its flesh than in the size of its body.

The bobo is a most noble fish, and much esteemed for the excellence of its flesh, about two feet long, and four or six inches wide where widest. The river barbel, known by the name of Bagre, is of the size of the bobo, and of an exquisite taste too, but harmful unless its flesh is first purged with lemon juice, or some other acid, of a certain slime or viscous liquid which it has. The bobos, as far as we know, are caught only in the rivers that empty into the Gulf of Mexico, and the barbels in those that discharge into the Pacific Ocean, or in some lake. The taste of these two species of fish, though delicate, does not quite reach that of the pámpanos and of the little doves, which are, not without reason, the most esteemed of all.

The corvina is a foot and a half long, but slender and rounded, and of a blackish purple color. In the head of this fish are found two little white stones that look like alabaster, an inch and a half long, about four lines wide, which are believed effective against retention of urine, taking three grains of them in water.

The botete is a small fish about eight inches long, but disproportionately thick. Its liver is so poisonous that in half an hour it causes death to him who eats it, with severe pains and convulsions. When it is still alive on the beach, where it feels itself touched, it swells up enormously, and boys take pleasure in making it burst with a stamp of the foot.

The Occhione (*) is a flat, round fish, which has eight,

or

(*) This fish, which is usually caught only in California, either has had no name until now, or else we do not know it; so we have given it that of Occhione (Big-eye), which seems to us to suit it. [p. 104] or ten inches in diameter. The lower part of its body is quite flat, but Bk. I. the upper is convex, and in the center where it rises most it has a sin‐

gle eye, but as large as that of an ox, and furnished with the necessary

eyelids. After death it always remains with the eye open, causing

some horror to those who look at it. (Cc)

The Iztacmichin, or white fish, has always been famous in Mexico,

and is no less common today on the tables of the Spaniards than an‐

ciently on those of the Mexicans. Three or four species of it are dis‐

tinguished. The Amilotl, which is the largest and the most prized, is

more than a foot in length, and has five little fins, two on the back,

two at the two sides of the belly, and another under the belly itself.

The Xalmichin, a little smaller than that, seems to me to be of the

same species. The Jacapitzahuac, which is the smallest, is no more

than eight inches in length, and an inch and a half in width. All these

fish are scaly, savory, and very wholesome, and the lakes of Chalco, of

Pátzcuaro, and of Chapala are full of them. The other species is that

of the Xalmichin of Cuauhnahuac, which is without scales, and is cov‐

ered with a soft, white skin.

The Axolotl, or Axolote (*), is an aquatic large lizard of the Mexican

lake. Its form is ugly, and its aspect ridiculous. Its length is ordinarily

eight inches; but there are some twice as large. Its skin is soft and

black, its head long, its mouth large, its tongue broad, small, and car‐

tilaginous, and its tail long. From the middle of the body to the end

of the tail it tapers. It swims with its four feet, which are like those of

the

frog

(Cc) Campoy convinced himself that the Occhione was the Uranoscopos, or

Callionymos, of Pliny; but Pliny has not left us the marks of this fish. The name

Uranoscopos, which has been the whole foundation of that opinion, suits equally all

those fish which, having their eyes on the top of the head, look at the sky, like the

rays and other flat fish.

(*) M. de Bomare could not hit upon the name of this fish. He calls it Azalotl,

Ascolotl, Azoloti, and Axoloti; and says that the Spaniards call it Juguete del agua; but

the Mexicans call it Axolotl, and the Spaniards give it no other name than that of

Axolote. [p. 105] frog. The most singular thing about this fish is that it has a uterus like that of woman, and is subject like her to the periodic evacuation of Bk. I. blood, as is established by several observations, to which Hernández bears witness. (Dd) Its flesh is edible and wholesome, and has almost the same taste as the eel. It is believed singularly beneficial to consumptives. In the same Mexican lake there are other species of little fishes, but not such that we need linger over their description.

As for shells, there are infinite species, and among them some of a beauty never seen, especially in the Pacific Ocean. Along all the coasts of this sea there was at various times the pearl fishery. The Mexicans fished for them on the coast of Tototepec, and on that of the Cuitlatecs, where they now fish for tortoiseshell. Among the starfish there is one furnished with five rays, and with an eye in each. Among the sponges and lithophytes there are rare and strange species. Dr. Hernández presents us with the portrait of a sponge sent to him from the Pacific Ocean, which had the shape of a human hand, but with ten or more fingers, clay-colored with black dots and red streaks, and was more callous than ordinary ones.

§ 14.

Finally, descending to the smallest animals, in which the power and Insects of Mexico. wisdom of the Creator shine forth the more, we may reduce the innumerable species of insects that are in Mexico to three orders, namely flying, terrestrial, and aquatic, although there are terrestrial and aquatic ones that afterward become flying, and must be considered now in the one state, now in the other.

Among the flying ones are the beetles, the bees, the wasps, the

History of Mexico, Vol. I.                         O                          flies

(Dd) M. de Bomare finds difficulty in believing what is said of the Axolote; but while we are assured by the testimony of those who have had these animals under their eyes for years, we ought not to mind the distrust of a Frenchman who, though learned in Natural History, has never seen the Axoloti, nor even knows their name: especially since the periodic evacuation of blood is not so peculiar to women that female monkeys do not have it also. “The females of monkeys,” says the same M. de Bomare, “for the most part have menses like women.” See Singes. [p. 106] flies, the gnats, the butterflies, and the grasshoppers. The beetles are Bk. I. of several species, but for the most part innocent. There are green

ones, to which the Mexicans gave the name of Majatl, with which

boys amuse themselves because of the great noise they make in flying.

There are others, black, stinking, and of irregular shape, called

Pinacatl.

The Cucujo, or luminous beetle, which is the most worthy of con‐

sideration, has been mentioned by several authors, but by none, so far

as I know, described. It is more than an inch long, and furnished with

double wings, like the other flying beetles. It has on its head a mov‐

able little horn, which is very useful to it; for when, having turned

over and resting on its back, it finds itself hindered in its motion, it

restores itself to its natural position by the action of this little horn,

sheathing and pressing it into a membrane made in the manner of a

pouch, which it has on its belly. Near the eyes it has two little mem‐

branes, and another larger one on the belly, thin, transparent, and

filled with a matter so luminous that its light suffices to read a letter

comfortably, and to light the road for those who walk at night; but it

never gives a greater light than in flying. When it sleeps, it does not

send it forth; for it covers it with other, opaque membranes. This lu‐

minous matter is a white, mealy, viscous substance, which preserves

its illuminating virtue for some time after it has been drawn from the

body of the Cucujo, and with it some people write luminous charac‐

ters on hats. There is a great abundance of these flying phosphors on

the sea coasts, and at night they are wont to form charming and bril‐

liant spectacles in the nearby mountains. To catch them boys do

nothing but whirl a firebrand in the evening: attracted by this light

the Cucujos come into the hands of the hunters. There has not been

lacking some author who confounded these marvelous insects with

fireflies; but these are much smaller, and much less luminous, com‐

mon in Europe, and very frequent in that country.

As agreeable as the sight of the Cucujo is, so disagreeable is

that [p. 107] that of the Temolin. This is a large beetle, of a reddish chestnut color,

Bk. I. furnished with six hairy feet, and with four toes on each. There are two species of Temolin: the one has its forehead armed with a horn, or antenna, and the other with two.

Of bees there are at least six different species. The first is that of the common bees of Europe, with which they agree no less in size, form, and color than in nature, habits, and the quality of the honey and wax which they make. The second species is of others like them, but entirely without a sting. Of this species are those of Yucatán and of Chiapas, which make the famous honey of Estabentun, which is clear, aromatic, and of a taste superior to that of all the other kinds of honey that we know. The harvests of this honey are six, one every two months; but the best is that which is gathered in November, because the bees make it from a white flower like jasmine, and very fragrant, which comes in September, and is called in that country Estabentun, whence the honey took its name. (Ee) The third species is of certain bees similar in form to winged ants, but smaller than the common bees, and without a sting. These insects, proper to hot and temperate countries, build combs similar in size and shape to the large sugar loaves, and sometimes much larger than they, hanging from the rocks or from the trees, especially from oaks. The population of these combs is much more numerous than that of the combs of the common bees. The nymphs of these bees are white and round like pearls, and are edible too. Their honey is grayish, but of a delicate taste. The bees of the fourth species are yellow, smaller than the common ones, and like them armed with a sting. Their honey is inferior to the aforesaid. Those of the fifth species are small and stingless; they build round combs in underground cavities, and their

O2                      honey

(Ee) The honey of Estabentun is very highly prized by the English and the French who come to the ports of Yucatán. I know that the French of Guarico have bought it sometimes to send as a present to their sovereign. [p. 108] honey is sour and bitterish. The Tlalpipiolli, which makes up the sixth Bk. I.

species, is black and yellow, as large as the common ones, but

stingless.

The species of wasps are at least four. The Quetzalmiahuatl is the

common one of Europe. The Tetlatoca, or vagabond, is so called be‐

cause it often changes its dwelling, and is always seen occupied in

gathering the materials for building it. It has a sting; but it makes nei‐

ther honey nor wax. The Xicotli, or Xicote, is a large wasp, black ex‐

cept for the belly, which is yellow. It makes a very sweet honey in the

holes that it opens in the walls. It is armed with a strong sting, and its

wound is very painful. The Cuicalmiahuatl likewise has its sting, but

we do not know whether it makes honey.

The Quauhxicotli is a hornet, all black save the tail, which is red. Its

sting is so large and so strong that with it it not only pierces a sugar

cane through from one side to the other, but even bores through the

trunks of trees.

Among the flies, besides the common ones, which are neither in so

great a number nor so importunate as those of Italy in summer, (Ff)

there are luminous ones, like the fireflies. The Axayacatl is a marsh fly

of the Mexican lake. From the innumerable eggs that these flies lay

on the rushes and on the flag, or iris, of the lake, thick crusts are

made, which the fishermen gather to sell in the market. This caviar,

called Ahuauhtli, was eaten by the Mexicans, and today is a very famil‐

iar dish to the Spaniards, and has almost the same taste as the caviar

of fish. But the ancient Mexicans ate not only the eggs, but even the

flies themselves, reduced to a mass and cooked with nitre.

The gnats, so common in Europe, especially

in

(Ff) The same observation about flies was already made by Oviedo: “In the

Islands,” he says, “and in Tierra Firme there are very few flies, and in comparison

with those there are in Europe it may be said that there are none there”; Sumario de la

natural historia de las Indias, ch. 81. It is true that in Mexico they are not so few as

Oviedo says; but, generally speaking, there are neither so many of them nor so trou‐

blesome as in Europe. [p. 109] in Italy, abound also in the maritime lands of Mexico, and in those places where the heat, the stagnant waters, and the groves foster their Bk. I. multiplication. In the lake of Chalco there are infinite numbers of them; but the capital, although it is near that lake, is quite free from this nuisance.

There are also in the hot lands certain midges, which make no noise in flying, but whose sting causes a violent itching, and if, to rid himself of it, the person bitten scratches the affected part, a sore is easily made.

In the same hot lands, especially in certain coastal ones, cockroaches abound, which are large, winged insects, loathsome and harmful, because they infest food, and sweet things especially, but otherwise useful to dwellings, because they purge them of bedbugs. It has been observed that the ships which on the voyage from Europe to New Spain go laden with bedbugs are on the return voyage entirely free of these stinking insects, because of the cockroaches. (*)

The species of butterflies are far more diverse, and more numerous, in Mexico than in Europe. Their variety and their beauty cannot be worthily depicted; nor are the best brushes capable of expressing the excellence of design and of colors which the author of nature employed in adorning their wings. Several notable authors have celebrated them in their writings, and Dr. Hernández had some of them drawn to give Europeans some idea of their beauty.

But butterflies are not comparable in number with the locusts, which at times fall upon the maritime lands, darkening the air with the dense clouds which they form, and utterly destroying all the vegetation that is in the countryside, as I saw in 1738 or 39 on the coast of Jicayán.

In

(*) These insects are also enemies of men of letters, since they consume the ink at night, unless care is taken to keep the inkstand well covered. The Spaniards call them Cucarachas, others Kakerlaques, others Dermestes, etc.

[p. 110]

In the peninsula of Yucatán there was not long ago a great famine for Bk. I.

this reason; but in no other country of that continent has this terrible

scourge been more frequent than in wretched California. (Gg) Among

the land insects, besides the common ones, of which there is nothing

particular to be said, there are several species of worms, centipedes,

scorpions, spiders, ants, crab lice, chigoes, and the cochineal.

Of the worms some are useful and others harmful: some served as

food for the ancient Mexicans, and others as medicine, like the Axin

and the Pollin, of which we shall speak elsewhere. The Tleocuilin, or

burning worm, has the properties of cantharides. Its head is red, its

breast green, and the rest of its body tawny. The Temahuani is a worm

entirely armed with small yellow and poisonous spines. The Temictli

resembles the silkworm in its operations, no less than in its transfor‐

mations. Silkworms were brought over from Europe and multiplied

successfully. Abundant harvests of good silk were gathered, especially

in the Mixteca, (*) where it was a considerable article of commerce;

but when the Mixtecs were afterward compelled to give it up for po‐

litical reasons, the raising of silkworms was likewise neglected, and

today few there are who engage in it. Besides this common silk there

is another, very valuable, white, soft, and strong, which is found on

the trees in several forests of the coastal countries, especially in those

years when the rains are scanty; but only some poor people make use

of it, because of the little economy of those peoples, or rather be‐

cause of the burdens that anyone would have to suffer who wished to

undertake such a trade. We know, moreover, from the letters of

Cortés to Charles V

that

(Gg) In the History of California, which will be published within a few months, the

copious observations made on locusts by Abbé Don Miguel del Barco will be set out;

he spent more than thirty years in that country, as famous as it is unworthy of the

fame it has.

(*) There are villages in the Mixteca that still preserve the name then given them

because of their trade, such as San Francisco de la Seda and Tepexi de la Seda. [p. 111] that silk was sold in the markets of Mexico, and that some paintings on silk paper made by the ancient Mexicans are still preserved.      Bk. I.

Centipedes are found sometimes in temperate countries, and more often in hot and humid ones. Dr. Hernández says that he saw some so large that they were two feet long and two fingers thick; but such insects must have been seen by that author in some country too humid and uncultivated, for we, although we have lingered in many places of every sort of climate, have never found any of so immense a size.

Scorpions are common throughout that kingdom; but in the cold and temperate countries they are for the most part few and not very harmful. In the hot lands, and in those where the air is too dry, however moderate the heat may be, they are more abundant, and their venom is such that it suffices to bring death to children and to cause terrible agonies to adults. It has been observed that the venom of the small yellowish scorpions is more active than that of the large brown ones, and that their sting is more deadly at those hours of the day when the sun is hottest.

Among the many species of spiders found there, we cannot pass over two remarkable ones, the tarantula and the Casampulga. (*) The name of Tarantola is given in that country, improperly, to a very large spider, whose back and legs are covered with a blackish down, soft and fine, like that of chicks. It belongs to the hot lands, and is found not only in the fields but also in houses. It is thought venomous, and it is commonly believed that a horse which in walking treads on one of them immediately loses its hoof; but I have never heard of any particular occurrence that might confirm this common opinion, although I spent five years in a very hot country where such spiders abounded. The Casampulga is small, and has short feet,

a

(*) I suspect that the original name of this spider was Cazapulga, that is, flea-hunter, afterward corrupted by the common people, as often happens. [p. 112] a red belly, and is as large as a chickpea. It is venomous and common Bk. I.

in the diocese of Chiapas and elsewhere. I do not know whether it is

the same spider that in other countries is called the capullino spider,

because it has the same marks.

The most common ants in that country are of three species. The

first is that of the small black ones common on both continents.

Others are large and red, and armed with a sting with which they in‐

flict painful bites; the Spaniards call them bravas, that is, fierce. Still

others are large and brown, and have from the Spaniards the name of

Harrieras, that is, carriers, because they are continually occupied in

carrying off grain for their provision, much more than the common

ants do; for which reason they are more destructive to the fields. In

some countries they have multiplied excessively through the negli‐

gence of the inhabitants. In the province of Jicayán black bands are

seen on the ground for several miles, formed by the ants as they go

and come.

Besides these species there is another, a remarkable one, in

Michoacán, and perhaps in other provinces as well. This one is larger

than the other ants, and has a gray body and a black head. In its hind

part it carries a little sack full of a very sweet liquor, of which the

boys are very fond, and they believe that it is honey made by the ants,

like the common sort by the bees; but to us it seems rather to be

eggs. M. de la Barrère, in his Natural History of Equinoctial France,

makes mention of such ants found in Cayenne; but those are winged,

and ours are wingless.

The Nigua, called Pique in other countries, is a very small insect

not altogether unlike the flea, which in some hot lands breeds in the

dust. It attaches itself to the feet, and, breaking the cuticle impercep‐

tibly, nests between it and the skin; and if it is not removed at once, it

breaks the skin also and passes on to the flesh, multiplying with in‐

credible quickness. For the most part it is not felt until, in piercing

the skin, it causes an intolerable itching. These insects, because of

their [p. 113] their surprising multiplication would be enough to depopulate those countries, if it were not so easy to avoid them, and if the inhabitants Bk. I. were not so quick in removing them before they multiply. God, for His part, that the harm done us by them might be less, not only denied them wings, but also withheld from their legs that conformation, and those vigorous muscles, which He granted to fleas for leaping; but among the poor, who by reason of their misery are condemned to live in the dust and to a habitual neglect of their persons, such insects usually multiply so greatly that they make wide cavities in their flesh and cause them dangerous sores.

What the Nigue do in dwellings, the Garrapate, or ticks, do in the country; of which there are two species, or rather two classes. The first is of those lice, no less common in the new than in the old continent, which attach themselves to the skin of sheep, horses, and other quadrupeds, and get into their ears, and even sometimes into those of men.

The other is found in abundance on the herbage of the hot lands, and from it passes easily to the clothes of travelers, and from the clothes to the body, to which it clings with such force, because of the particular shape of its feet, that it is hard to detach; and if it is not promptly detached, it makes a sore like that of the Nigua. At the beginning it seems nothing but a little black dot; but with the blood that it sucks it swells in such a way, and so quickly, that in a very short time it becomes as large as a broad bean, and then takes on the color of lead. (Hh)

The celebrated cochineal of Mexico, so well known and prized throughout the world for the excellence of the color it yields, is an insect proper to that country, the most useful of all that the land of Anahuac nourishes, where from the time of the Mexican kings particular care was taken in raising

History of Mexico, Vol. I.                      P                          it.

(Hh) Oviedo says that to detach ticks promptly and without danger it is enough to anoint oneself with a little oil, and then to scrape oneself with a knife. [p. 114] it. (Ii) The country where it succeeds best is the Mixteca, in which is Bk. I. found the most considerable branch of its commerce. (Jj) In the six‐

teenth century it was also raised in Tlaxcala, in Huexotzinco, and in

other places, and a large trade was carried on; but the burdens that

the Indians (who have always been the raisers of these insects) suf‐

fered from the tyrannical greed of certain governors compelled them

to abandon that labor, which is, moreover, very long and troublesome.

The cochineal at its greatest growth has the size and shape of a bed‐

bug. The female is misshapen and sluggish. Her eyes, her mouth, her

little horns, or antennae, and her feet are so hidden among the wrin‐

kles of her skin that they cannot be distinguished without the aid of

the microscope; and so several Europeans persisted in believing that

it was some kind of seed, not a true animal, against the testimony of

the Indians who raised it, and of Hernández, who observed it as a

naturalist. The male is rarer, and one serves for three hundred females

or more. He is also smaller and thinner than the female, but nimbler

and more active. He has on his head two jointed little horns, and in

each joint four bristles arranged with perfect symmetry.

His

(Ii) The Chronicler Herrera says, in Dec. 4, bk. 8, ch. 8, that although the Indians

had the cochineal, they made no account of it until they were instructed by the

Spaniards. But what did the Spaniards teach them? To raise cochineal? How could

they teach what they knew nothing whatever of, and what, instead of an insect, they

took for a seed? Did they teach them, perhaps, to use it for dyeing? But if the Indians

did not use it for dyeing, why did they take so much trouble in raising it? Why were

Huaxyacac, Coyolapan, and other places obliged to pay the King of Mexico twenty

sacks of cochineal every year, as appears from the tribute roll? How can it be be‐

lieved that those nations, so given to painting, were ignorant of the use of cochineal,

and did not know how to employ such insects, when they knew how to make use of

the achiote, the indigo, and very many stones and mineral earths?

(Jj) The cochineal that comes every year from the Mixteca to Spain exceeds two

thousand five hundred sacks, as some authors testify. The commerce that the city of

Oaxaca carries on in it amounts annually to two hundred thousand scudi. M. de

Bomare says that a certain cochineal is given the name of mesteca cochineal because it

is raised in Meteque, in the province of Honduras; but this is an error. It is called

Misteca because it comes from the Mixteca, a province farther from Honduras than

Rome is from Paris.

[p. 115]

His feet are six, each composed of three parts. From the hinder part of his body rise two hairs, two or three times longer than the body. Bk. I. He is furnished with two large wings, of which the female is entirely deprived. These wings are strengthened by two muscles: one external, which extends along the whole circumference of the wing, and the other internal and parallel to the first. The internal color is red, but darker in the female, and the external a whitish red. In the wild cochineal the internal color is darker still, and the external whitish or ash-colored. The cochineal is raised on a species of nopal, or opuntia, or Indian fig, which rises to a height of about eight feet, and whose fruit is similar to the tunas or figs of other opuntias, but not, like them, edible. It feeds on the leaves of this opuntia, sucking the juice with a trunk that it has in its breast between the first two pairs of feet. There it attains its full growth, and produces a numerous offspring. The manner of multiplying that these precious insects have, the economy of the Indians in raising them, and the pains they take to defend them from the rain, which is too harmful to them, and from the many enemies that persecute them, will be set forth when we speak of the agriculture of the Mexicans. (Kk)

Among the aquatic insects the Atetepitz is a marsh beetle, resembling in size and shape the flying beetles. It is furnished with four feet, and covered with a hard crust. The Atopinan is a marsh grasshopper of a dusky color, six fingers long and two wide. The Ahuihuitla is a worm of the Mexican lake, four fingers long, and as thick as the quill of a goose, tawny in the

P2                        upper

(Kk) Don Antonio de Ulloa says that the nopal on which the cochineal is raised has no spines; but this is not so, for I, who spent five years in the Mixteca, always saw it on spiny nopals. M. Raynal persuaded himself that the color of the cochineal is owed to the tuna, or red fig, on which it feeds; but this author was not well informed; for the cochineal feeds not on the fruit but on the leaf, which is entirely green, nor does that nopal bear red figs but white ones. It is true that it can also be raised on that of the red fig; but that is not the plant proper to the cochineal. [p. 116] upper part of the body, and white in the lower. It stings with its tail, Bk. I. which is hard and venomous. The Ocuiliztac is a black marsh worm,

but when roasted it takes on a white color. All these insects were edi‐

ble among the ancient Mexicans.

Finally, passing over other insects, whose names alone would fur‐

nish a very copious list, I wish to end this account with a kind of

zoophytes, or plant-animals, which I saw in 1751 in a country house

ten miles distant from Puebla toward the southeast. These were three

or four fingers long, furnished with four very slender feet, and armed

with two little horns; but their body was nothing other than the ribs

of leaves of the same shape, size, and color as those seen in the other

leaves of the trees where these insects were found. Dr. Hernández

makes mention of them under the name of Quauhmecatl, and Gemelli

describes another not altogether unlike them, found in the neighbor‐

hood of Manila. (Ll)

From the little that we have so far set forth of the natural history

of that kingdom, the difference may be recognized that exists be‐

tween the hot lands, the cold, and the temperate, of which the vast

country of Anahuac is composed. In the hot lands Nature is more

prodigal, in the cold and the temperate more benign. In the hot lands

the mountains are more abundant in minerals and springs, the plains

more pleasant, and the woods more leafy. In these lands are found the

plants most useful to life, (Mm) the largest trees, the most precious

woods, the most beautiful flowers,

the

(Ll) I know well that modern naturalists give the name of Zoophytes, for the most

part, only to certain marine bodies which, having the appearance of vegetables, nev‐

ertheless have the nature of animals. Nevertheless I give that name to those land in‐

sects, because it suits them with as much propriety, and perhaps more, than those

marine bodies. In my Physics I believe I have set out, with the greatest verisimili‐

tude, the mechanism of nature in the generation of such insects.

(Mm) It is true that in the hot lands wheat does not for the most part grow, nor

are several European fruits produced, such as apples, peaches, pears, and others; but

what is the lack of these few vegetables when compared with that unspeakable abun‐

dance and variety of plants, as fruit-bearing as medicinal, which are found in such

countries? [p. 117] the most delicious fruits, and the most aromatic resins. There the species of animals are more varied and more numerous, and their in‐ Bk. I. dividuals handsomer and larger, the birds of more beautiful plumage and of sweeter song; but all these allurements are counterbalanced by as many inconveniences; for in such countries are found the most terrible wild beasts, the most venomous reptiles, and the most harmful insects. The land does not suffer the dismal symptoms of winter, nor is the air subject to the tiresome vicissitude of the seasons. On the land a perpetual spring reigns, and in the air a continual summer, to which the inhabitants readily accustom themselves; but the incessant sweating of their bodies, and the abundant and savory fruits with which the bountiful earth regales them at all times, expose them to several maladies unknown in other countries. The cold lands are not so fertile or so beautiful; but they are healthier, and their animals less harmful to man. In the temperate countries (at least in many, such as the valley of Mexico) one enjoys the advantages of the cold countries without their inconveniences, and several delights of the hot countries without their discomforts. The most common maladies in the hot countries are intermittent fevers, the spasm, and consumption, and in the port of Veracruz, for some few years past, the black vomit. (Nn) In the other countries, catarrhs, defluxions, pleurisy, and acute fevers, and in the capital diarrhea. Besides these ordinary maladies, certain epidemic diseases are wont to be felt at times, which seem periodic, although their period is not fixed and regular, such as those that occurred there in 1545, in 1576, and in our own days in 1736 and in 1762. The smallpox, brought there by the Spanish conquerors, is not seen in that country as frequently as in Europe, but after a certain number of years it comes, and then attacks all those who have not suffered it before, making in one stroke all that slaughter that it makes successively in Europe.

The

(Nn) Ulloa and other historians of America describe the spasm and the black vomit. This disease was not known there before the year 1725.

[p. 118]

The nations that occupied these lands before the Spaniards, al‐

Bk. I.

though very different from one another in language, and in part also

§ 15. in customs, were nevertheless of almost one and the same character.

Character of the Mexicans, and of the The physical and moral qualities of the Mexicans, their disposition

other nations of

Anahuac. and their inclinations, were the same as those of the Acolhuas, the

Tepanecs, the Tlaxcalans, and the other nations, without any differ‐

ence save that which comes from a different education; wherefore

what we shall say of the one we wish to be understood likewise of the

others. Several authors, ancient and modern alike, have undertaken

the portrait of these nations; but among so many I have not found

even one who is everywhere exact and faithful. Passions and preju‐

dices in the case of some authors, and want of enlightenment, or else

of reflection, in the case of others, have made them use colors very

different from those they ought to have used. What we shall say, on

the other hand, is founded on a serious and lengthy study of the his‐

tory of those nations, on an intimate acquaintance of not a few years

with them, and on the most minute observations made on their

present condition, both by ourselves and by other impartial persons.

For the rest, I find nothing in myself that could incline me for or

against them: for neither does the claim of a fellow countryman bend

my judgment in their favor, nor is love of my nation, or zeal for the

honor of my countrymen, capable of engaging me to condemn them;

so that I shall say sincerely and frankly the good and the bad that I

have recognized in them.

The Mexicans are, then, of middling stature, from which they de‐

part more by excess than by defect, and of just proportion in their

limbs; of good complexion, narrow forehead, and black eyes, with

teeth even, firm, white, and clean, and hair thick, black, coarse, and

straight; their beard is scanty, and for the most part they have no hair

on their legs, thighs, and arms. Their skin is of an olive color.

Perhaps no nation on earth will be found among whom deformed

persons are rarer. It is harder to find a hunchback, a cripple, or a

squinter among a thousand Mexicans than among

a [p. 119] a hundred individuals of other nations. The unpleasantness of their color, the narrowness of their forehead, the scantiness of their beard, Bk. I. and the coarseness of their hair are so balanced by the regularity and proportion of their limbs that they hold the middle ground between beauty and deformity. Their aspect neither attracts nor offends; but among the young Mexican women many are found who are fair and quite beautiful, whose beauty is made the more lovable by the sweetness of their speech, the pleasantness of their manners, and the natural modesty of their countenances.

Their senses are very keen, above all that of sight, which they keep entire to extreme old age. Their constitution is sound, and their health robust. They are found entirely free from many infirmities that are frequent among the Spaniards; but in the epidemic diseases to which the country is subject from time to time, they are the principal victims: among them such diseases begin, and among them they end. One never perceives from the mouth of a Mexican that foul breath which in others is wont to be caused by the corruption of the humors or by the indigestion of food. They are of a phlegmatic constitution; but the pituitous evacuations of their heads are very scanty, and they seldom spit. They turn gray and grow bald later than the Spaniards, and those who reach the age of a hundred are not very rare among them; almost all the others die of an acute disease.

They are at present, and have always been, very sober in eating; but their passion for strong liquors is nonetheless most vehement. In ancient times they were held to their duty by the severity of the laws; today the abundance of such liquors and the impunity of drunkenness make half the nation lose its wits; and this is without doubt the principal cause of the slaughter that epidemic diseases make among them, besides the misery in which they live, more exposed to malignant influences and more deprived of the means of correcting them.

Their souls are radically in all respects similar to those

of [p. 120] of the other children of Adam, and endowed with the same faculties; Bk. I.

nor did the Europeans ever do less honor to their own reason than

when they doubted the rationality of the Americans. The state of civ‐

ilization in which the Mexicans were found by the Spaniards, far su‐

perior to that in which the Spaniards were found by the Phoenicians,

the Gauls by the Greeks, and the Germans and the Britons by the

Romans, (Oo) would have been quite sufficient to remove such a

doubt from the human mind, had not the inhuman greed of certain

scoundrels committed itself to promoting it. (Pp) Their minds are al‐

together capable of all the sciences, as experience has shown. (Qq)

Among the few Mexicans who have devoted themselves to the study

of letters, the rest of the nation being employed in public and private

labors, we have known good geometers, excellent architects, and

learned theologians.

There are many who grant the Mexicans great ability for imitation,

but deny them that of invention. A vulgar error, which is belied by the

ancient history of this nation.

Their

(Oo) Don Bernardo Aldrete, in his book On the Origin of the Spanish Language,

would have us believe that the Spaniards at the arrival of the Phoenicians were more

polished than the Mexicans at the arrival of the Spaniards; but this paradox has been

sufficiently refuted by the very learned authors of the Literary History of Spain. It is

true that the Spaniards in those remote centuries were not so barbarous as the

Chichimecs, the Californians, and other savage nations of America; but neither had

they their government so well regulated, nor their arts so advanced, nor had they

made, so far as we know, so much progress in the knowledge of Nature, as the

Mexicans at the beginning of the sixteenth century.

(Pp) Let the bitter complaints made on this subject by Bishop Garcés in his letter

to Pope Paul III, and by Bishop de las Casas in his Memorials to the Catholic kings

Charles V and Philip II, be read, and above all the most humane laws made in favor

of the Indians by those most Christian monarchs.

(Qq) In our dissertations we shall set forth the opinions of Don Julián Garcés, first

bishop of Tlaxcala, of Don Juan de Zumárraga, first bishop of Mexico, and of Don

Bartolomé de las Casas, first bishop of Chiapas, on the capacity, the intelligence, and

the other good qualities of the Mexicans. The testimony of these prelates, so re‐

spectable for their virtue, their learning, and their experience of the Indians, is worth

far more than that of any historian.

[p. 121]

Their souls are, like all others, susceptible to the passions; but

Bk. II. these do not act upon them with that impetuosity or that fury that they do in others. One does not ordinarily see among the Mexicans those transports of anger, or those frenzies of love, that are so frequent in other nations.

They are slow in their operations, and have an incredible phlegm in those works that require time and lengthy labor. They are likewise most patient of injuries and hardships, and very grateful for any benefit done them, provided they have no cause to fear some harm from the beneficent hand; but some Spaniards, not knowing how to distinguish tolerance from indolence, or distrust from ingratitude, say proverbially that the Indians neither feel injuries nor appreciate benefits. (Rr) This habitual distrust, in which they stand toward those who are not of their nation, often leads them to falsehood and to perfidy; whence good faith has not been held among them in all the esteem that it deserves.

They are also naturally serious, taciturn, and severe, and show themselves more zealous for the punishment of crimes than for the reward of virtue.

Liberality and detachment from any interest whatever are among the principal attributes of their character. Gold does not have among the Mexicans all the esteem that it enjoys among others. (Ss) They give without regret what they procure with the greatest labor. This detachment of theirs from interest, and the little love they bear toward those who govern them, make them refuse those labors to which they are compelled by them, and this

History of Mexico, Vol. I.                       Q                            is

(Rr) Experience has shown us how grateful the Mexicans are for the benefits they receive, whenever they are sure of the benevolence and sincerity of the benefactor. Their gratitude has often been made manifest by public and noisy demonstrations, which make plain the falsity of that proverb.

(Ss) We do not speak of those Mexicans who, through their continual commerce with the avaricious, have been infected with avarice; but even these are less moved by interest than other avaricious people. [p. 122] is precisely the so much exaggerated laziness of the Americans. (Tt) Bk. I.

Nevertheless there is no people in that kingdom that works more, or

whose labors are more useful or more necessary. (Vv)

The respect of children toward their parents, and that of the

young toward their elders, are innate in that nation. Fathers love their

children very much; but the love that husbands bear their wives is less

than that which wives bear their husbands. It is common, though not

general, among the men to be less attached to their own wives than

to those of others.

Courage and cowardice in different respects occupy their spirits by

turns, in such a way that it is difficult to decide which of these two

things prevails in them. They advance intrepidly against the dangers

that threaten them from natural causes; but the stern glance of a

Spaniard is enough to terrify them. Now that stupid indifference to

death and to eternity, which several authors consider universal among

all the Americans, suits only those who through their rudeness and

lack of instruction have as yet no idea of the divine judgment.

Their particular fondness for the outward practices of religion eas‐

ily degenerates into superstition, as happens to all the unlettered of

any other people in the world; but their alleged adherence to idolatry

is wholly a chimera formed in the unregulated fancy of certain igno‐

rant persons. A few examples among some mountain folk are not suf‐

ficient to defame the whole body of the nation. (Uu)

Finally,

(Tt) In what we say about laziness we do not include the savage nations that in‐

habit other countries of the New World.

(Vv) In our dissertations we shall set forth the labors in which the Mexicans en‐

gage. Whence Monsignor Palafox used to say that whenever the Indians fail, the

Indies too will necessarily fail the Spaniards.

(Uu) The few examples of idolatry that can be produced are in part excusable,

since it is no wonder that men who are rude and devoid of instruction do not know

how to distinguish the idolatrous cult of some crude little figure of stone or wood

from that which is owed to the sacred images. But how many times has prejudice

against them taken for idols those

[p. 123]

Finally, in the character of the Mexicans, as in that of any other nation, there is good and there is bad; but the bad could for the most Bk. I. part be corrected by education, as experience has shown us. (Xx) It is not so easy to find another youth more docile to instruction, nor has any greater docility ever been seen than that of their ancestors toward the light of the Gospel.

For the rest, it cannot be doubted that the modern Mexicans are not in all respects like the ancient, just as the modern Greeks are not like those who flourished in the time of Plato and Pericles. In the spirits of the ancient Mexicans there was more fire, and the ideas of honor made a greater impression. They were more intrepid, more agile, more industrious, and more active; but also more superstitious, and too inhuman.

Q2                        Book that were images, although shapeless, of the Saints. In 1754 I observed certain little images believed to be idols, found in the cave of a mountain, and I did not doubt that they were figures representing the mystery of the holy Nativity.

(Xx) To know how much education can do among the Mexicans, it would suffice to know the admirable life led by the Mexican women in the Royal College of Guadalupe in Mexico, and in the convents of Capuchin nuns of the same capital, and of Valladolid of Michoacán.

[p. 124]

BOOK II. Of the Toltecs, the Chichimecs, the Acolhuas, the Olmecs, and the other nations that inhabited the land of Anahuac before the Mexicans. Departure of the Aztecs, or Mexicans, from the country of Aztlan, their homeland: events of their pilgrimage as far as the country of Anahuac, and their settlements at

Chapultepec and at Colhuacan. Founding of Mexico and of Tlatelolco.

Inhuman sacrifice of a Colhua maiden.

he history of the primitive peopling of Anahuac is so ob‐

T         scured, and so enveloped in fables (as is that of the other

peoples of the world), that to find the truth is not merely difficult but altogether impossible. Certain it is nevertheless, as much from the venerable testimony of the holy books as from the constant and universal tradition of those peoples, that the first inhabitants of Anahuac descended from those few men whom Divine Providence snatched from the waters of the Flood to preserve the human species on earth. Nor can it be doubted that the nations that anciently peopled that land came to it from countries farther north in America, where their ancestors had settled many years and centuries earlier. On these two points I find all the Toltec, Chichimec, Acolhua, Mexican, and Tlaxcalan historians in agreement; but it is not known who the first inhabitants were, nor the time of their passage, nor the events of their journey and of their first settlements. Several historians, wishing to penetrate this chaos armed with feeble conjectures, vain combinations, and certain suspect paintings, have become lost

in [p. 125] in the thick darkness of antiquity, adopting as an expedient childish and baseless narratives.                                                Bk. II.

There were authors who, relying on the tradition of the American peoples, and on the discovery of bones, skulls, and entire skeletons of immense size, which at different times and in several places of New § 1.

The Toltecs. Spain have been dug up, (a) believed that the first inhabitants of that land were giants. I for my part do not doubt their existence, in this country no less than in other countries of the New World; (b) but we cannot guess the time in which they lived, although we have reason to believe them very ancient, nor can we persuade ourselves that there was ever an entire nation of giants, as those authors imagined, but only extraordinary individuals of the known nations, or else of other, more ancient and wholly unknown ones. (c)

The

(a) The places where gigantic skeletons have been found are Atlancatepec, a village of the province of Tlaxcala, Texcoco, Toluca, Cuauhximalpan, and in our own days in California a hill not far from Kada-Kaaman.

(b) I know well that many philosophers of Europe, who laugh at the asserters of giants, will mock me too, or at least pity my too great credulity; but I must not betray the truth to avoid censure. I know that among the civilized peoples of America the tradition ran of the existence in those countries of certain men of immense height and corpulence, and I do not recall that among any American nation there is a memory either of elephants, or of hippopotami, or of other quadrupeds of such size. I know that human skulls and entire skeletons of surprising size have been found, on the deposition of innumerable authors, and especially of two eyewitnesses above all exception, Dr. Hernández and Father Acosta, who lacked neither learning, nor criticism, nor sincerity; and I do not know that so far, among so many excavations made in New Spain, the skeleton of a hippopotamus has ever been uncovered, or, what is more, even a tooth of an elephant. I know that some of those bones are found in tombs built expressly; and I do not know that tombs are built to bury hippopotami or elephants. All this, and still more, ought to be considered, before deciding, by those authors who affirmed without hesitation that all the bones discovered in America belonged to those, or to I know not what other immense animals.

(c) Several historians of Mexico say that the giants were treacherously killed by the Tlaxcalans; but this report, besides having no other support than certain poems of those same Tlaxcalans, does not agree with the chronology of the aforesaid historians, since they make the giants too ancient, and the Tlaxcalans too modern in the country of Anahuac.

[p. 126]

The nation of the Toltecs is the first of which some notices remain to us, Bk. II. though very scanty. These men, exiled, as they said, from their homeland

Huehuetlapallan, a place, as we conjecture, in the kingdom of Tollan, (d) from

which they took their name, situated to the northwest of New Mexico, began

their migration in the year I Tecpatl, that is, 596 of the common era. They

stayed in every place where they came, for as long a time as their fancy sug‐

gested, or the need of providing for the preservation of life. Where they

thought it advisable to make a longer stay, they built houses, and cultivated

the land, sowing maize, cotton, and other plants, the seeds of which they car‐

ried with them in order to procure what was necessary. In this fashion they

went wandering, always making their way toward the south, for the space of

104 years, until they arrived at the place to which they gave the name of

Tulancingo, more than 50 miles to the northeast of the site where, some cen‐

turies later, the famous city of Mexico was founded. Throughout their whole

journey they marched under the orders of certain captains, or lords, who were

seven in number when they arrived at Tulancingo. (e) In this country, al‐

though of mild climate and fertile soil, they would not settle, but when

scarcely twenty years had passed they withdrew forty miles to the west, along

the bank of a river, where they founded the city of Tollan, or Tula, after the

name of their homeland. This city, the most ancient, so far as is known, of the

land of Anahuac, and one of the most celebrated in the history of Mexico,

was the metropolis of the Toltec nation and the court of their kings. Their

monarchy began in the year VIII Acatl, that is, in 667 of the common era of

Christianity, and lasted 384 years. Here is the series of their kings, with the

Christian year in which they began to reign. (*)

Chalchiuhtlanetzin

(d) Toltecatl in Mexican means native of Tollan, as Tlaxcaltecatl means native of Tlaxcala,

Chololtecatl native of Cholula, etc.

(e) The seven Toltec lords were called Zacatl, Chalcatzin, Ehecatzin, Cohuatzon,

Tzihuacoatl, Metzotzin, and Tlapalmetzotzin.

(*) We have indicated the year in which the Toltec monarchs began to reign, supposing the

epoch of their departure from Huehuetlapallan, which is not certain, but only probable.

[p. 127]

Chalchiuhtlanetzin   in    667                         Bk. II.

Ixtlilcuechahuac     in    719

Huetzin              in    771

Totepeuh             in    823

Nacaxoc              in    875

Mitl                 in    927

Xiutzaltzin Queen    in    979

Topiltzin            in   1031

It is no wonder that only eight monarchs reigned in little less than four centuries, since a strange law of that nation ordained that none of their kings should reign either more or less than one Toltec century, which (as we shall explain elsewhere) consisted of fifty-two years. If the king completed the century on the throne, he at once left the government, and another was put in his place; but if the king failed before the century was out, the nobility succeeded to the government, and governed the rest of the century in the name of the deceased king. This was seen to happen in the case of Queen Xiutzaltzin: she died in the fifth year of her reign, and the nobility governed for the forty-eight remaining years.

§ 2.

Most celebrated among all the nations of Anahuac were the Toltecs, Civilization of the both for their civilization and for their excellence in the arts; whence in Toltecs. later centuries the name of Toltecs was given as a mark of honor to the most distinguished artisans. They always lived in society, gathered in well-regulated cities under the dominion of sovereigns and the direction of laws. They were little warlike, and more devoted to the cultivation of the arts than to the exercise of arms. The later nations acknowledged themselves indebted to their agriculture for maize, cotton, chili pepper, and other most useful fruits. And they practiced not only the arts of primary necessity, but also those that serve luxury. They knew how to smelt gold and silver, and to give them every sort of figure in casting. They worked skillfully every species of gem, and this was precisely the art that made their name most famous; but nothing could make them more esteemed among us than their having been the inventors,

or [p. 128] or at least the reformers of the system of the ordering of time which all the Bk. II. civilized nations of Anahuac adopted; which presupposes, as we shall see later,

many observations and exact knowledge of astronomy.

The Chevalier Boturini (*), on the authority of the ancient histories of the

Toltecs, says that these people, observing in their ancient homeland

Huehuetlapallan the excess of almost six hours of the solar year over the civil

year that was in use among them, regulated it by the intercalary day that they

inserted every four years; which they did a hundred and more years before the

Christian era. He says further that in the year 660, Ixtlilcuechahuac reigning

in Tula, Huematzin, a celebrated astronomer, with the approval of the king

convoked the sages of the nation, and with them painted that famous book

which they called Teoamoxtli, that is, Divine Book, in which, with well-distin‐

guished figures, were set forth the origin of the Indians, their dispersion after

the confusion of tongues at Babel, their migration in Asia, their first settle‐

ments on the continent of America, the founding of the empire of Tula, and

their progress up to that time. The heavens, the planets, the constellations,

the Toltec calendar with its cycles, the mythological transformations (in

which they enclosed their moral philosophy), and the secrets of popular wis‐

dom wrapped up among the hieroglyphics of their gods, together with every‐

thing that belonged to religion and to customs, were described there. The

said Chevalier adds, moreover, that there was seen noted in their paintings

the solar eclipse that occurred at the death of our Redeemer, in the year VII

Tochtli, (f) and that when

some

(*) In the work composed by him and printed at Madrid in 1746 under this title: Idea of a

General History of New Spain, founded upon a great abundance of figures, symbols, characters, hieroglyph‐

ics, songs, and manuscripts of Indian authors newly discovered.

(f) All persons who have studied at the source the history of the nations of Anahuac know

very well that those peoples were accustomed to note in their paintings the eclipses, the

comets, and other celestial phenomena. Now, reading what Boturini says, I took the trouble to

compare the Toltec years with ours, and found the year 34 of Christ, that is, 30 of the com‐

mon era, to be VII Tochtli. I did this out of mere curiosity, and not because I pretend to con‐

firm, or because I believe, the anecdotes of that author. [p. 129] some learned Spaniards, versed in the history and paintings of the Toltecs, compared their chronology with ours, they found that that Bk. II. nation counted from the creation of the world to the time of the birth of Christ 5199 years, which is precisely the chronology of the Roman Calendar.

Whatever may be said of these curious anecdotes of the Chevalier Boturini, which I leave to the free judgment of prudent readers, it is certain and indubitable among all those who have studied the history of those nations that the Toltecs had a clear and distinct knowledge of the universal Flood, of the confusion of tongues, and of the dispersion of peoples; indeed, they named their first ancestors, who separated from the rest of the families in that universal dispersion. It is likewise certain, as we shall show elsewhere (though it may seem an incredible thing to the critics of Europe, accustomed to believe all Americans cut to one measure), that the Mexicans and all the other civilized nations of Anahuac had their civil year as well adjusted to the solar by means of intercalary days as the Romans had it after the ordinance of Julius Caesar, and that such exactness was owed to the enlightenment of the Toltecs. As regards religion, they were idolaters, and, so far as appears from history, were the inventors of the greater part of the Mexican mythology; but we do not know that they had in use those barbarous and bloody sacrifices which afterward became so frequent among the other nations. The Texcocan historians believed the Toltecs to be the authors of that most famous idol representing the god of water, placed on Mount Tlaloc, of which we shall speak later. It is certain indeed that they built, in honor of their beloved god Quetzalcoatl, the very lofty pyramid of Cholula, and probably also the famous ones of Teotihuacan, in honor of the Sun and the Moon, which, though disfigured, still subsist. der to imitate the tower of Babel; but the painting on which he rests

multiplied considerably, extending their population on every

to have believed them the work of the Olmecs; but since we have no other remains of

the architecture of this nation by which to judge, and since, moreover, those pyramids

are built in the style of the one at Cholula, we are therefore led to think that the

(h) The painting alleged by the Chevalier Boturini represented the pyramid of

Cholula, with this Mexican inscription, Toltecatl Chalchihuatl onazia Ehecatepetl, which

he interprets thus: Monument, or precious stone of the Toltec nation, which with its neck went

searching out the region of the air; but disregarding the incorrect manner of writing and

the barbarism Chalchihuatl, anyone at all instructed in the Mexican language will at

once perceive that a more fantastic interpretation could not be made. At the foot of

the painting, the said Chevalier says, the author set a note, in which, speaking to his

countrymen, he admonished them in this fashion: Nobles and lords, behold your writings,

the mirror of your antiquity, and the history of your forefathers, who, urged by fear of the flood,

built this refuge as a fitting shelter, in case they should again be overtaken by such a calamity. But

to tell the truth, the Toltecs would have been entirely devoid of sense if, for fear of the

flood, they had undertaken with so much expense and toil the building of that porten‐

tous pyramid, when they had in the lofty mountains not far from Cholula a safer

refuge against inundations, and much less danger of dying of hunger. On the same can‐

vas was represented, says Boturini, the baptism of Ilamatecuhtli, queen of Cholula,

conferred on her by the deacon Aguilar on August 6, 1521, together with the appari‐

tion of the Madonna to a certain Franciscan religious who was in Rome, ordering him

to set out for Mexico, where on a mountain made by hand (that is, the pyramid of

Cholula) he was to place her image. But this is nothing other than a tissue of dreams

and lies: for neither were there ever kings in Cholula, nor could that baptism, of which

no writer speaks, have been celebrated on August 6, 1521, because at that time

Aguilar was with the other Spaniards in the greatest heat of the siege of the capital,

which seven days later was to surrender to the victors. Of the alleged apparition of the

Mother of God I find no memory anywhere among the Franciscan historians, who in

their Chronicles omitted nothing of what occurred in this kind. We have demonstrat‐

ed the falsity of that account in order to make those more cautious in accepting mod‐

ern paintings who in the future might undertake the History of Mexico. [p. 131] side into many large cities; but the stupendous calamities that befell them in the first years of the reign of Topiltzin overturned their pow‐ Bk. II. er and their happiness. For some years heaven denied them the rain necessary for their fields, and the earth the fruits on which they sustained themselves. The air, infected with mortal corruption, filled the land each day with corpses, and with consternation the minds of those who survived the ruin of their fellow countrymen. Thus a great part of the nation died of hunger and of contagion. Topiltzin also died in the year II Tecpatl, the twentieth of his reign, which was probably 1052 of the common era, and with him ended the monarchy of the Toltecs. The wretched remnants of the nation, thinking to escape the common calamity, sought in other countries a fitting remedy for their ills. Some made for Onohualco, or Yucatán, others toward Guatemala; meanwhile there remained in the kingdom of Tula various families scattered here and there in the great valley, where Mexico was afterward founded, in Cholula, in Tlaximaloyan, and in other places, and among them the two princes, sons of King Topiltzin, whose descendants in the course of time became related by marriage to the royal families of Mexico, of Texcoco, and of Colhuacan.

These scanty notices concerning the Toltecs are the only ones that we judge worthy of being related here, passing over various fabulous narratives of which several historians have made use. (i) We would wish to have their Divine Book, cited by Boturini and by Don Fernando de Alva Ixtlilxochitl in his highly prized manuscripts, to bring greater light to the history of this celebrated nation.

(i) Torquemada says that at a dance held by the Toltecs the wretched Devil appeared to them in gigantic form and with enormous arms, and in the midst of the dance, embracing them, strangled them; that he then let himself be seen in the form of a child with a rotting head, and brought them the pestilence; and finally that at the persuasion of the same Devil they abandoned the country of Tula. But that good author understood literally certain symbolic paintings, in which those people represented with such figures the famine and the pestilence that overtook them just when they found themselves at the height of prosperity.

[p. 132]

With the ruin of the Toltecs the land of Anahuac remained solitary

Bk. II.

and almost wholly depopulated until the arrival of the Chichimecs,

for the space of more than a century. (j) The Chichimecs, like the

Toltecs who preceded them, and the other nations that came after

them, were natives of northern countries, it being possible to call the

§ 4. north of America, with good reason, equally with that of Europe, the The Chichimecs.

seedbed of the human race. From both of them, like swarms, numer‐

ous nations were seen to issue forth to people the southern countries.

Their native country, whose situation we do not know, was called

Amaquemecan, where, by their own account, several monarchs of their

nation ruled for many years. (k)

The character of the Chichimecs was indeed singular, as is seen

from their history; for to a certain kind of civility they added many

traits of barbarism. They lived under the command of a sovereign,

and of chiefs and governors entrusted with the supreme authority,

with as much submission as is commonly seen among the most civi‐

lized nations. There was distinction between the commoners and the

nobility, and the commoners were accustomed to revere those whom

birth, merit, or the favor of the prince raised above their condition.

They lived gathered in places composed, as one must believe, of

wretched huts; (l) but they practiced neither agriculture nor those

arts

that

(j) In our Dissertation II we oppose Torquemada, who counts no more than

eleven years of interval between the ruin of the Toltecs and the arrival of the

Chichimecs.

(k) Torquemada names three Chichimec kings of Amaquemecan, and to the first

gives 180 years of reign, to the second 156, and to the third 133. See what we say in

our Dissertation II concerning the preposterous chronology of this author. The same

author undoubtedly affirms that Amaquemecan was six hundred miles distant from

the site where the city of Guadalajara is today; but in more than twelve hundred

miles of populated country that lies beyond that city, there is found no trace, nor any

memory, of the kingdom of Amaquemecan: whence we believe it to be a country not

yet discovered, and much farther north than Torquemada imagined.

(l) Torquemada says that the Chichimecs had no houses but lived in the caves of

the mountains; yet in the same chapter where he writes this, he affirms that the capi‐

tal city of their kingdom was called Amaquemecan. [p. 133] that accompany civil life. They lived only on game, fruits, and the roots that the uncultivated earth bore. Their clothing was the rough Bk. II. skins of the beasts they had taken, nor did they know any other weapon than the bow and arrow. Their religion was reduced to the simple worship of the Sun, to whose alleged divinity they offered grass, and the flowers they found growing in the fields. As for their customs, they were certainly less unpleasing, and less coarse, than the temper of a hunting people would lead one to expect.

The motive they had for abandoning their homeland is uncertain, § 5. as is also uncertain the etymology of the name Chichimecatl. (m) The Xolotl, first king of last king they had had in Amaquemecan left the government divided the      Chichimecs in

Anahuac. between his two sons, Achcauhtli and Xolotl. The latter, either because he ill endured, as often happens, the division of his own authority, wished to try whether fortune destined other lands for him, where he might command without a rival; or because, seeing that the mountains of his kingdom were not sufficient to provide sustenance for a number of inhabitants perhaps grown too large, he determined to remedy the necessity by a timely departure. Having therefore taken such a resolution, for the one motive or the other, and having first had a good part of the southern lands reconnoitered by his scouts, he set out from his homeland with a large army of his subjects, who chose, whether from affection or from interest, to accompany him. On their journey they kept encountering

the

It is a manifest, gross contradiction; or else we shall say that Amaquemecan was a city without houses, and shall give the title of city to the caves of the mountains. This fault is frequent in this author, who is nevertheless deserving of credit for his services to the History of Mexico.

(m) Several authors have labored to divine the etymology of the name Chichimecatl. Torquemada says that this name is derived from Techichinani, which means Sucker, because the Chichimecs sucked the blood of the animals they hunted. But this etymology is forced, especially among those peoples who did not alter names in such a fashion in their derivation. Vetancurt believed that it was derived from Chichime, that is, Dogs, because by that name they were called in contempt by the other nations; but if it had been so, they would not glory, as in fact they did glory, in the name of Chichimecatl. [p. 134] the ruins of Toltec places, and in particular those of the great city of Bk. II.

Tula, where they arrived after eighteen months. From this place they

headed toward those of Cempoala and Tepepulco, about forty miles

distant to the northeast of the site of Mexico. Thence Xolotl sent his

son, Prince Nopaltzin, to observe the country. The prince traversed

the shores of the lakes and the mountains that surround the delight‐

ful valley of Mexico, and having surveyed the rest of the country from

the top of a high mountain, he shot four arrows toward the four

winds as a sign of the possession that in the name of the king his fa‐

ther he was taking of all that land. Informed of the condition of the

country, Xolotl took the resolution to settle in Tenayuca, a place six

miles distant from the site of Mexico toward the north, and dis‐

tributed all his people in the neighboring lands; but because the

greater part of the population was toward the north and toward the

northeast, that tract of land afterward took the name of

Chichimecatlalli, that is, Land of the Chichimecs. The historians say

that in Tenayuca a review of the people was held, and that for this

reason the name of Nepohualco was also given to it, which means place

of the counting; but altogether incredible is what Torquemada adds,

namely that more than a million Chichimecs were found in the re‐

view, and that twelve heaps made of the stones they kept throwing

down as they passed in review lasted down to his own times. For it is

neither likely that so great an army would set out on so long a journey,

nor does it seem possible that so small a district could have sustained
