Mexicans, as all the historians call them.

A little before or a little after this event the Mexicans divided their

wretched city into four quarters, assigning to each its protecting god,

besides that of the whole nation. This division survives at present un‐

der the names of San Pablo, San Sebastián, San Juan, and Santa María.

(K) In the center of these quarters there was the sanctuary

of

(I) The ancients represented Tlatelolco in their paintings with the figure of a heap

of sand. Had they known this who undertook the interpretation of the Mexican

paintings, which were published in Mexico in 1770 together with the letters of the

conqueror Cortés, they would not have called this place Tlatilolco, a name they inter‐

pret as Oven.

(K) The quarter of San Pablo was called by the Mexicans Teopan and Xochimilca,

that of San Sebastián Atzacualco, that of San Juan Moyotla, and that of Santa María

Cuepopan and Tlaquechiuhcan. [p. 171] of Huitzilopochtli, to whom they paid greater worship every day.

Bk. II.

In homage to this wicked deity they performed at this time a hor‐

§ 21. rible sacrifice, which cannot be heard of without horror. They sent an Inhuman sacrifice. embassy to the petty king of Colhuacan, begging him to give them one of his daughters, that she might be consecrated Mother of their protecting god, and explaining to him that this was an express command of their god, in order to exalt her to so great an honor. The petty king, either enamored of the glory he hoped for in having a deified daughter, or else frightened by the misfortunes he foresaw if he should refuse the request of a god, at once granted what they asked, especially as he could not suspect what was about to happen. The Mexicans led that noble maiden with great jubilation to their city; but scarcely had she arrived when the Devil, according to what the historians say, commanded that she be sacrificed to him, and after her death flayed, and that one of the most valiant young men of the nation be clothed in her skin. Whether this was an order of the Devil or, what is more likely, a cruel invention of the barbarous priests, everything was punctually carried out. The petty king, invited by the Mexicans to be present at the apotheosis of his daughter, went to be one of the spectators of that great ceremony and one of the worshippers of that new deity. He was brought into the sanctuary, where beside the idol stood upright the young man clothed in the bloodied skin of the victim; but the darkness of the place did not let him see what was there. They put into his hands a censer and a little copal, that he might begin his worship; but having seen, by the light of the flame the copal made, the horrible spectacle that lay before him, he was moved to the depths by grief, and, carried away by violent emotions, rushed out crying like a madman, ordering his people to avenge so barbarous an outrage; but they did not dare undertake it, since they would infallibly have been overwhelmed by the multitude: so the disconsolate father returned to his house to bewail his misfortune for the rest of his life. His

Y2                 unhappy [p. 172] unhappy daughter was created a goddess, and honorary Mother not Bk. II.

only of Huitzilopochtli but of all their gods, and this is precisely what

the name Teteoinan signifies, by which she was known and revered

from that time forward. Such were, in that new city, the specimens of

a barbarous system of religion, which we shall set out elsewhere.

[p. 173]

BOOK III. Founding of the Mexican monarchy: events of the Mexicans under their first four kings, up to the defeat of the Tepanecs and the conquest of Azcapotzalco. Feats and illustrious deeds of Moctezuma Ilhuicamina. Government and death

of Techotlalatzin, fifth Chichimec king. Revolutions of the kingdom of

Acolhuacan. Death of King Ixtlilxochitl, and of the tyrants Tezozomoc and

Maxtla.

ntil 1352 the government of the Mexicans had been aris‐

U          tocratic, the whole nation obeying a body composed of the

persons most respected for their nobility and wisdom. Those who governed it when Mexico was founded were twenty in number, (*) among whom the most authoritative was Tenoch, as appears from their paintings. The extreme humiliation in which they found themselves, the hardships they suffered from their neighbors, and the example of the Chichimecs, the Tepanecs, and the Colhuas drove them to raise their little state into a monarchy, not doubting § 1.

Acamapichtli, first that royal authority would lend some splendor to the whole body of king of Mexico. the nation, and flattering themselves that in the new head they would have a father to watch over the state and a good general to defend them from the insults of their enemies. By common consent Acamapichtli was elected, either by acclamation of the people or by the votes of some electors, to whose judgment all committed themselves, as was afterward done.

Acamapichtli was one of the most illustrious and most prudent

persons

(*) The twenty lords who then governed the nation were called Tenoch, Atzin, Acacitli, Ahuexotl, or Ahueiotl, Ocelopan, Xomimitl, Xiuhcac, [p. 174] persons that they then had. He was the son of Opochtli, a most noble

Bk. III.

Aztec, (a) and of Atotoztli, a princess of the royal house of Colhuacan.

(b) On his father’s side he took his origin from Tochpanecatl, that

lord of Zumpango who received the Mexicans so kindly when they ar‐

rived at that city. He had not yet married; so they promptly resolved

to seek for him a young woman from one of the first houses of

Anahuac, and for that purpose sent embassies in succession to the

lord of Tacuba and to the king of Azcapotzalco; but by both of them

their pretension was rejected with scorn. Then, without losing hope

on account of so ignominious a refusal, they made the same request

of Acolmiztli, lord of Coatlichan and descendant of one of the three

Acolhua princes, begging him to give them one of his daughters as

queen. Acolmiztli yielded to their entreaties and gave them his

daughter Ilancueitl, whom the Mexicans conducted in triumph, and

with the greatest joy celebrated the wedding.

§ 2.    The Tlatelolcas, who, because they were neighbors and rivals, were Cuacuauhpitzahuac,

first king of always  watching what was done in Tenochtitlan, in order to emulate

Tlatelolco.

the glory of the Mexicans and not to be oppressed at some time by

their power, also created a king of their own; but not thinking it ad‐

vantageous that he should be of their own nation, but rather of that

of the Tepanecs (to whose lord the site of Tlatelolco was no less sub‐

ject than that of Mexico), they asked the king of Azcapotzalco

for

Axolohua, Nanacatzin, Quentzin, Tlalala, Tzontliyayauh, Cozcatl, Tezcatl, Tochpan,

Mimich, Tetepan, Tezacatl, Acohuatl, and Achitomecatl.

(a) Some historians say that Acamapichtli, whom they suppose to have been born

in the slavery of Colhuacan, was a son of Huitzilihuitl the Elder; but this is not prob‐

able, since Huitzilihuitl, who was born at the time when the Mexicans were in

Tizayuca, was not less than 90 years old when the Mexicans were led off into slavery;

hence Huitzilihuitl was not the father but the grandfather of Acamapichtli.

Torquemada makes this king the son of Cohuatzontli; but we adhere to the opinion

of Dr. Sigüenza, who investigated the genealogy of the Mexican kings with greater

critical skill and diligence than Torquemada.

(b) It is a marvel that Opochtli should have married so illustrious a lady at a time

when his nation was so abased by slavery; yet such a marriage is certified by the

paintings of the Mexicans and the Colhuas, seen by the most learned Sigüenza. [p. 175] for one of his sons, that as monarch he might govern them, and that they might serve him as vassals. The king gave them his son Bk. III. Cuacuauhpitzahuac, who was at once crowned first king of Tlatelolco in 1353.

It is to be suspected that the Tlatelolcas, in making this request of that king, both to flatter him and to irritate him against the Mexicans, their rivals, exaggerated to him the insolence of those who had made a king without his permission; for a few days later the same king of Azcapotzalco convoked his councilors and spoke to them thus: “What do you think, noble Tepanecs, of the Mexicans’ attempt? They have made their way into our dominions, and are increasing considerably their city and their commerce; and, what is worse, they have had the audacity to create a king from among their own countrymen without waiting for our permission. Now if they do this in the beginnings of their settlement, what may be believed they will do when they have multiplied and their forces have grown? Is it not to be feared that in the future, instead of paying us the tribute we have imposed on them, they will claim that we pay it to them, and that the petty king of the Mexicans will want to be monarch of the Tepanecs as well? I, however, think it necessary to increase their burdens in such a way that, wearing themselves out in paying them, they may be consumed, or else, by not paying them, may be afflicted by us with other evils, and finally compelled to leave our state.” All applauded this resolution, nor could anything else be hoped for, since a prince who makes his inclination plain in seeking counsel looks more for §Burdens

3.

imposed on panegyrists who will second his wishes than for councilors who will the Mexicans. enlighten his mind. The king therefore sent word to the Mexicans that, the tribute they had paid him until that time having been so small, he wished them from then on to double it; that besides this they must bring him I do not know how many thousands of willow and fir cuttings to be planted in the streets and gardens of Azcapotzalco, and together with these convey to that court a great garden, in which all the seeds usual in Anahuac should be sown and already sprouted.

The

[p. 176]

The Mexicans, who until that time had paid no other tribute than Bk. III.

a certain quantity of fish and a certain number of aquatic birds, were

greatly distressed at these new burdens, fearing that they would go on

increasing from hour to hour; yet they did everything that was pre‐

scribed to them, bringing at the appointed time, together with the

usual fishing and fowling, the cuttings and the floating garden.

Whoever has not seen the most beautiful gardens that to this day are

cultivated in the midst of the water, and the ease with which they are

transported wherever one wishes, will not be able without difficulty

to persuade himself that such an event was true; but whoever has

seen them, as I have and all those who have navigated that lake,

where the senses find the sweetest recreation in the world, will have

no reason to doubt the truth of this History. Having received the

aforesaid tribute, the same king ordered them to bring him the next

year another garden, and in it a duck and a heron, each brooding her

eggs, in such a way that on arriving at Azcapotzalco the chicks would

begin to hatch. The Mexicans obeyed, and took their measures so

well that the foolish prince had the pleasure of seeing the chicks

come out of the eggs. He then ordered that for the following year

they bring him, besides the garden, a live deer. This new order was in

truth harder to carry out, since to hunt the deer it was necessary to

go to the mountains of the mainland, in evident danger of falling in

with their enemies; nevertheless they carried it out, to avoid graver

wrongs. This harsh oppression of the Mexicans lasted no less than

fifty years. The historians of Mexico affirm that the Mexicans in all

their afflictions implored the protection of their god, and that he

made the execution of the orders easier for them; but we are of an‐

other opinion.

The poor king Acamapichtli had, besides these vexations, that of

the barrenness of Queen Ilancueitl; and so he married

Tezcatlamiahuatl, daughter of the lord of Tetepanco, by whom he had

several sons, among them Huitzilihuitl and Chimalpopoca, his succes‐

sors on the crown. He took the second wife without leaving the first;

indeed they lived, both

of [p. 177] of them in such concord that Ilancueitl took charge of the education of Huitzilihuitl. He had besides, though not honored with the title of Bk. III. queens, other wives, and among them a slave, of whom was born to him Itzcoatl, one of the best and most renowned kings that there were in Anahuac. Acamapichtli governed his city, which was then his whole kingdom, peacefully for the space of thirty-seven years. In his time the population increased, some stone buildings were erected, and the canals were begun, which served no less for the embellishment of the city than for the utility of the citizens. The Interpreter of the Mendoza Collection ascribes to this king the conquest of Mixquic, Cuitláhuac, Cuauhnahuac, and Xochimilco. But who can persuade himself that the Mexicans were capable of undertaking the conquest of four such large cities, when they could hardly sustain themselves in their own settlement? Hence the painting of this collection, which represents those four cities as conquered by the Mexicans, must be understood of them in so far as they were auxiliary troops of other states, as a little later they served the king of Texcoco against the people of Xaltocan.

Shortly before his death Acamapichtli convoked the magnates of the city and made them a short speech, commending to them his wives and his children and zeal for the public good. He said that, having received the crown from their hands, he returned it to them, that they might give it to the one whom they judged would be more useful to the state; and he declared the grief he felt in dying and leaving his nation tributary to the Tepanecs. His death, which occurred in 1389, was deeply felt by the Mexicans, and his obsequies were celebrated with as much solemnity as the wretchedness of the nation allowed.

From the death of Acamapichtli until the election of the new king there was, according to Dr. Sigüenza, an interregnum of four months; which did not happen again afterward, since from then on hardly a few days had passed after the death of a king when another was elected. This time the election could be delayed, the nobility being occupied in the

History of Mexico, Vol. I.                 Z               regulating [p. 178] regulating the number of the electors and establishing the ceremonial

Bk. III. of the coronation, which was then first observed.

The electors chosen from the nobility having therefore assembled,

the oldest of them spoke in this manner: “My age gives me courage to

speak first. Great indeed, O noble Mexicans, is the misfortune we

have suffered in the death of our king; nor is there anyone who ought

to mourn it more than we, who were the feathers of his wings and the

eyelids of his eyes. Such a misfortune becomes greater by reason of

the calamitous state in which we find ourselves under the dominion

of the Tepanecs, to the disgrace of the Mexican name. You, therefore,

to whom the remedy of the present calamities is so pressing, think of

electing a king who shall be zealous for the honor of our powerful god

Huitzilopochtli, who shall avenge with his arm the affronts done to

our nation, and who shall take under the shadow of his clemency the

§ 4. orphans, the widows, and the aged.” This brief address ended, they Huitzilihuitl, second

king of Mexico. gave their votes, and there was elected Huitzilihuitl, son of the late

king Acamapichtli. Then they went out in order, and, betaking them‐

selves to the house of the elect, they took him in their midst, led him

to the Tlatocaicpalli, that is, the royal seat, or throne, and having seat‐

ed him, anointed him in the form that we shall set out elsewhere;

they put on his head the Copilli, or crown, and one by one paid him

obedience. Then one of the most distinguished personages raised his

voice among them all, and spoke thus to the king: “Be not discour‐

aged, O generous youth, by the new burden that has been laid upon

you, of being head of a nation shut in among the reeds and rushes of

this lake. It is truly a misfortune to have so small a kingdom estab‐

lished in another’s territory, and to govern a nation which, being free

at the beginning, became tributary to the Tepanecs. But console your‐

self, for we are under the protection of our great god Huitzilopochtli,

whose image you are, and whose place you hold. The dignity to which

you have been raised by him must not serve you as a pretext for idle‐

ness and softness, but rather as a spur to toil. Always keep before

your [p. 179] your eyes the clear examples of your great father, who spared no toil

Bk. III. for the good of his people. We should wish, O lord, to make you gifts worthy of your person; but since the fortune in which we find ourselves does not permit it, deign to receive our desires and the constant fidelity that we promise you.”

Huitzilihuitl was not yet married when he ascended the throne; so it was soon thought to give him a wife, and the nobles wished her to be a daughter of the same king of Azcapotzalco; but in order not to expose themselves to so ignominious a refusal as that which they had had to suffer in the time of Acamapichtli, they agreed to make the request this time with the greatest demonstrations of submission and respect. So some nobles went to Azcapotzalco, and, presenting themselves to the king and kneeling, set out their claim thus: “Behold, great lord, at your feet the poor Mexicans, awaiting from your kindness a favor far above their merit; but to whom shall we turn, if not to you, who are both our father and our lord? Behold us hanging upon your lips, and ready at all your nods. We beg you with the most profound respect to take pity on our master and your servant Huitzilihuitl, shut in among the thick reeds of the lake. He is without a wife, and we without a queen. Deign, lord, to let slip from your hands one of your gems, or of your precious feathers. Give us one of your daughters, that she may come to reign in your land.”

These expressions, which are singularly elegant in the Mexican language, so bent the mind of Tezozomoc (this was the king’s name) that he at once granted his daughter Ayauhcihuatl, to the unspeakable delight of the Mexicans, who conducted her in pomp to Mexico, and the longed-for marriage was celebrated with the usual ceremony of tying the edge of the bride’s garment to that of the bridegroom. The king had by her in the first year a son, to whom they gave the name of Acolnahuacatl; but eager to ennoble

Z2                      his [p. 180] his nation with new alliances, he asked for and obtained from the

Bk. III. lord of Cuauhnahuac one of his daughters, named Miahuaxochitl, by

whom he had Moctezuma Ilhuicamina, the most famous king that the

Mexicans had.

§ 5.     There reigned at that time in Acolhuacan Techotlalatzin, son of Techotlalatzin, king

of Acolhuacan. King Quinatzin. The first thirty years of his reign were quite peaceful;

but then Tzompan, lord of Xaltocan, rebelled against the crown, and,

seeing that his forces were not sufficient to oppose his sovereign,

called to his aid the states of Otompan, Metztitlán, Cuahuacan,

Tecomic, Cuauhtitlan, and Tepotzotlán. King Techotlalatzin

promised him pardon, provided that he lay down his arms and sub‐

mit. It is to be believed that he used such clemency out of regard for

the most noble blood of the offender, since he was the last descen‐

dant of Chiconcuauhtli, one of the three Acolhua princes. But he,

proud of the number of troops he had, rejected the grace with con‐

tempt. The king, indignant, sent against the rebels an army, to which

were joined the Mexicans and the Tepanecs, summoned by him. The

war was obstinate, nor could it be ended in less than two months; but

victory having at last declared for the king, Tzompan and all the

chiefs of the rebel cities were punished with the extreme penalty, the

most illustrious line of Chiconcuauhtli ending in Tzompan himself.

This war, waged by the Mexicans as auxiliaries of the king of

Acolhuacan against Xaltocan and the other confederated states, is

seen represented in the third painting of the Mendoza Collection;

but the Interpreter of these paintings was mistaken, believing those

cities to have been conquered for the crown of Mexico.

When the war was over the Mexicans returned in glory to their

city, and King Techotlalatzin, to avoid new rebellions in the future,

divided his kingdom into sixty-five states, giving each a lord to govern

it in subordination to the crown. From every state he drew off some

people to settle them in another, though they remained subject to the

lord of the state from which they came, wishing thus to keep the peo‐

ples in check by means of foreign people, dependent on others,

whom

he [p. 181] he placed in each. A policy truly useful for preventing rebellion; but injurious to innocent subjects, and troublesome for the lords who Bk. III. governed them. Besides this he honored several nobles with distinguished offices. He made Tetlato general of the army, Yolqui Quartermaster and Introducer of Ambassadors, Tlami Steward of the royal palace, Amechichi Superintendent of the cleanliness of the royal houses, and Cohuatl Director of the Goldsmiths of Ocolco. No one worked in gold and silver for the king’s service except the director’s own sons, who had, however, learned the art. The Quartermaster of Ambassadors had under him several other Colhua officials, the Steward had a certain number of Chichimecs, and the superintendent of cleanliness a similar number of Tepanecs. By such measures he increased the splendor of the court and strengthened the throne of Acolhuacan, though he could not prevent the revolutions that we shall presently set forth. These and other similar strokes of policy, which will be discovered in the course of this History, will show the wrong done to the Americans by those Europeans who thought them animals of another species, and by those too who believe them incapable of improvement.

The new alliance contracted by the king of Mexico with the king of Azcapotzalco, and the glory the Mexicans had won in the war of Xaltocan, contributed greatly, no less to the vigor of their little state than to the better treatment of their persons; for, having now greater freedom and extension in their commerce, they began at this time to clothe themselves in cotton, of which they had before been entirely deprived by their poverty, and they wore nothing else than coarse cloths of maguei thread or of wild palm. But scarcely had they begun to draw breath when from the same royal family of Azcapotzalco there arose against them a new enemy and a bloodthirsty persecutor.

Maxtla, lord of Coyoacán and son of the king of Azcapotzalco, an ambitious, untamed, and cruel man, and therefore feared even by his own father, had taken offense at the

marriage [p. 182] marriage of his sister Ayauhcihuatl to the king of Mexico. He dissem‐

Bk. III.

bled his displeasure for some time out of respect for his father; but in

§ 6. Enmity of Maxtla to‐

the tenth year of the reign of Huitzilihuitl he went to Azcapotzalco ward the Mexicans. and convoked the nobility in order to set before them his complaints

against the Mexicans and their king. He represented to them the

growth of the population of Mexico, exaggerated the pride and arro‐

gance of that nation and the fatal effects that were to be feared from

its present dispositions, and above all complained of the very grave

wrong done him by the Mexican king in having taken his wife from

him. It must be known that Maxtla and Ayauhcihuatl, though chil‐

dren of Tezozomoc, were born of different mothers, and perhaps such

marriages were then permitted among the Tepanecs. Whether, then,

Maxtla truly wished to marry his sister, or whether this was, as is

more likely, a mere pretext for carrying out his cruel designs, he took

in that assembly the resolution of summoning Huitzilihuitl to re‐

proach him with his alleged rashness. The Mexican king did indeed

go to Azcapotzalco; nor should this cause wonder, since it was not un‐

usual at that time for lords to visit one another; besides which,

Huitzilihuitl had the particular reason of being a feudatory of that

crown; for although, from the birth of Acolnahuacatl, the queen of

Mexico had obtained from her father Tezozomoc the relief of the

Mexicans from the burdens to which they had been subjected for so

many years, Mexico nevertheless remained in the condition of a fief

of Azcapotzalco, and the Mexicans had to present to the Tepanec

king two ducks every year in acknowledgment of his overlordship.

Maxtla received Huitzilihuitl in a hall of his palace, and after din‐

ing with him in the presence of the courtiers, who flattered him on

his projects, he gave him a most severe reprimand for the injury he

claimed to have been done him in the marriage with Ayauhcihuatl.

The Mexican king protested his innocence with the greatest humility,

saying that he would never have asked for the princess, nor would the

king her father have granted her to him, if she had been

promised [p. 183] promised to another. But in spite of the sincerity of his excuses and the efficacy of his reasons, Maxtla replied to him indignantly: “I could Bk. III. well, without hearing you further, put you to death here on the spot: thus your rashness would be punished and my honor avenged; but I do not wish it to be said that a Tepanec prince treacherously killed his enemy. Go now in peace: time will furnish me with some occasion to take a more honorable vengeance.”

The Mexican went away full of grief and rage, and it was not long before he felt the effects of the enmity of his cruel brother-in-law. The true cause of this enmity was the fear that Maxtla conceived that the lordship of the Tepanecs might perhaps at some time fall to his nephew Acolnahuacatl, since he was born of a daughter of King Tezozomoc, whereby his nation would be made subject to the Mexican. To free himself, then, from such a fear, he took the barbarous resolution of putting his nephew to death, as in fact happened, at the hands of certain men who wished by so cruel a deed to win the favor of their master; for there never lack among the powerful venal men who are ministers of their passions. (*) Tezozomoc did not consent to this crime; but neither did he show, so far as we know, any displeasure. In the course of this History it will be seen that the pride, ambition, and cruelty of Maxtla, tolerated, indeed favored by his indulgent father, were the cause of his ruin and of the destruction of his nation. Huitzilihuitl bore so painful a blow very unwillingly; but he did not find himself with forces sufficient to avenge himself.

In

(*) There is no author who sets forth the circumstances of the tragic death of the little Prince Acolnahuacatl, nor can it be understood how the Tepanecs could carry out such an attempt in Mexico; yet we cannot doubt the fact, since it is attested to us by the native historians, although among the Spaniards there is someone, such as Father Acosta, who is mistaken, confusing this death with that of Chimalpopoca, third king of Mexico.

[p. 184]

In the same year (1399) in which this tragedy occurred in Mexico, the

Bk. III. first king, Cuacuauhpitzahuac, died in Tlatelolco, leaving that city consider‐

ably enlarged with good buildings, beautiful gardens, and greater civiliza‐

§ 7. Tlacateotl, second tion. In his place was elected Tlacateotl, of whose origin the historians speak king of Tlatelolco.

variously, since some believe him a Tepanec, like his predecessor, and others

an Acolhua, appointed by the king of Acolhuacan. The rivalry that existed

between the Mexicans and the Tlatelolcas contributed greatly to the growth

of both cities, each side seeking to surpass the other in everything. The

Mexicans for their part had formed family ties with the neighboring na‐

tions, had increased their agriculture by multiplying the floating gardens in

the lake, and had also a greater number of boats, with which their fishing

and their commerce had grown: so that they were able to celebrate their

secular year I Tochtli, corresponding to 1402 of the common era, with

greater display than all the other four that had passed since their departure

from the country of Aztlan.

Techotlalatzin was still reigning at this time in Acolhuacan, already de‐

crepit; so, foreseeing the approach of death, he called his son and successor

Ixtlilxochitl, and among the other instructions he gave him advised him to

win the minds of the lords who were his feudatories; for it might happen

that Tezozomoc, an old, crafty, and ambitious man, who until that time had

been restrained by fear, would wish to conspire against the empire. The

fears of Techotlalatzin were not vain, as we shall shortly see. Finally this

king died in 1406, after a long reign, though not so long as some authors

have said. (c)

§ 8.   After the royal obsequies had been celebrated with the usual ceremonies, Ixtlilxochitl, king of and with the attendance of the petty kings and feudatory lords of that

Acolhuacan. crown, the exaltation of Ixtlilxochitl was celebrated. Among the petty kings

there

(c) Torquemada and Vetancurt give Techotlalatzin 104 years of reign; but although it is

not impossible to reign so many years, it is wholly improbable, nor can we believe it without

grave documents, especially as their chronology is everywhere absurd. See our dissertations. [p. 185] there was the king of Azcapotzalco, who soon made plain how well he had been known to the late King Techotlalatzin; for without paying Bk. III. obedience to the new king, he went off to his own state to incite the minds of other feudatories to rebellion. He convoked the kings of Mexico and of Tlatelolco and told them that, Techotlalatzin, who for so many years had tyrannized that country, being dead, he wished to set at liberty all the individual lords in such a manner that each should rule his state with absolute independence of the king of Acolhuacan; that to attain so glorious an end he had need of their help, and trusted in their courage, already known to all nations, that they would share in the glory to which he aspired: and so that the blow might be surer, he would bring into the confederation other lords whom he knew to be animated by the same thoughts. Both kings, whether from fear of Tezozomoc’s overbearing power or to increase the glory of their arms, offered to serve him with their troops, and the same answer was given by other lords whom he solicited.

Meanwhile Ixtlilxochitl was trying to put the affairs of his court in order and to win the minds of his subjects; but he recognized, not without grave sorrow, that many had already withdrawn from his obedience to submit to the perfidious Tezozomoc; so, to check the progress of his enemies, he ordered the lords of Coatlichan, of Huexotla, and of other states near the court to arm without delay as many troops as they could. The king himself wished to command the army in person; but he was dissuaded by his courtiers, who thought his presence more necessary at court; since in that turbulence some hidden enemies, or men of equivocal loyalty, might take advantage of his absence to seize the capital and hurl him from the throne. Tochintecuhtli, son of the lord of Coatlichan, was therefore appointed general of the army, with Cuauhxilotl, lord of Iztapalocan, as his substitute in case of death or of some other accident. They chose as the theater of the war the plain of Cuauhtitlan, fifteen miles north of Azcapotzalco. The rebel troops were more

History of Mexico, Vol. I.              Aa              numerous [p. 186] numerous, but those of the royal army better disciplined. This army,

Bk. III. before going to Cuauhtitlan, laid waste six states of rebel lords, both

to weaken the enemy and so as not to leave behind anyone who might

do them harm. The war was one of the most obstinate, the discipline

of the Texcocans being balanced against the numbers of the

Tepanecs, who would soon have been wholly vanquished had they not

constantly been reinforced with fresh troops. The confederates of the

rebels frequently detached large bodies and sent them to make raids

in the loyal states, sure of finding little resistance there, because near‐

ly all the forces of the Texcocans were gathered at Cuauhtitlan.

Among the many evils they caused, they killed Cuauhxilotl, lord of

Iztapalocan, who, having returned from the camp of Cuauhtitlan,

died in glory defending his city courageously. The king of Acolhuacan

saw himself therefore compelled to divide his forces, assigning as a

garrison for the cities a good part of the people who came to his aid

from many distant places. Tezozomoc, seeing that instead of the ad‐

vantages he expected his forces were diminishing every day, and that

his people were weary of the fatigues and dangers of the war, after

three years of continual fighting asked for peace, with the intention

of ending by secret treachery what he had begun by open force. The

king of Acolhuacan, although he could not trust in the good faith of

the Tepanec, nevertheless consented without demanding of him any

condition that would make him safe for the future, because his troops

were as weary as those of his enemy.

§ 9.     Scarcely was this war finished, or shortly before it ended, when Chimalpopoca, third Huitzilihuitl died in 1409, after twenty years of reign, having pub‐

king of Mexico.

lished some laws useful to the state and leaving the nobility in posses‐

sion of the liberty it had of electing the successor. His brother

Chimalpopoca was therefore elected, and from then on the law seems

to have been established of making the election from among the

brothers of the deceased king, and, if there were no brothers, from

among his nephews. This law

was [p. 187] was constantly observed, as we shall see, until the ruin of the

Bk. III. Mexican empire.

While Chimalpopoca was trying to establish himself on the throne of Mexico, Ixtlilxochitl was wavering on that of Acolhuacan. The peace that Tezozomoc had asked of him was a mere pretext to let him fall asleep, and meanwhile to promote his negotiations more effectively. Every day he saw his own party grow larger, while that of the Texcocan was diminishing. This unfortunate king found himself reduced to such an extremity that, no longer thinking himself safe in his court, he wandered about the neighboring mountains, escorted by a small army and accompanied by the lords of Huexotla and Coatlichan, who remained constantly faithful to him. The Tepanecs, in order to press him harder, seized the provisions that were being carried to his camp: so that he came to such need that he was forced to ask for provisions from his own enemies. So easy is it to be hurled from the summit of human happiness into the abyss of misery!

He sent therefore a nephew of his named Cihuacuecuenotzin to § 10. Otompan, one of the rebel cities, to beg those citizens to succor their Memorable deed of

Cihuacuecuenotzin. king with provisions, of which he was in want, and to admonish them to abandon the party of the rebels, remembering the fidelity they had sworn to him. Cihuacuecuenotzin well knew the danger of the enterprise; but the nobility of his sentiments, the strength of his spirit, and his fidelity to his sovereign prevailing over his fear, he was ready to obey: “I go, lord,” he said to him, “to carry out your commands and to sacrifice my life to the obedience I owe you. You are not unaware how far the people of Otompan have been alienated from you in order to adhere to your enemy. All the land is occupied by the Tepanecs and full of dangers: my return is too uncertain. But if I perish in your service, and if the sacrifice I make to you of my life is worthy of some recompense, I beg you to protect my two tender little sons whom I leave.” These words, accompanied by tears, softened the heart of the king, who, on dismissing him,

Aa2                      said [p. 188] said to him: “May our god accompany you and bring you back safe. Bk. III.

Ah! perhaps on your return you will find done to me what you fear for

yourself; for there are so many enemies who seek my death.”

Cihuacuecuenotzin went without delay to Otompan, and before en‐

tering it learned that there were then in that city some Tepanecs sent

by Tezozomoc to publish a proclamation; yet he was not discouraged,

but with an intrepid spirit went on to the square, where the Tepanecs

had assembled the people to publish the proclamation, and after

courteously saluting them all, set forth his embassy freely.

The people of Otompan made sport of him and mocked his re‐

quest; but none of them dared go further, until a base fellow threw a

stone at him, urging the others to put him to death. The Tepanecs,

who had been quiet and silent to observe what the people of

Otompan would do, now seeing them openly declared against the

king of Acolhuacan and against his ambassador, cried out, saying, Let

him die, let him die, the traitor, accompanying their cries with stones.

Cihuacuecuenotzin at first faced his enemies, but seeing himself over‐

whelmed by the crowd, and trying to save his life by flight, was killed

by a shower of stones. A man truly worthy of a better fortune! A

memorable example of fidelity, which would be celebrated by histori‐

ans and poets, if the hero, instead of being an American, had been a

Greek or a Roman.

The Tepanecs became boastful over a deed so inhuman and so con‐

trary to the law of nations, and declared to the multitude the great

pleasure they had in being able to inform their master, as eyewitness‐

es, of the inviolable fidelity of the people of Otompan. They said fur‐

ther that they had been sent precisely to give them the order not to

give aid to the king of Texcoco under pain of proscription, and to ex‐

hort them to take up arms against that king, and in defense of their

own liberty. The lord of Otompan and the first men of the nobility

answered that they willingly obeyed the order of the king of

Azcapotzalco, and offered to do all they could to second his

intentions.

They

[p. 189]

They promptly gave notice of this event to the lord of Acolman, and he, who was a son of Tezozomoc, made it known to his father; Bk. III. who, believing that it was now time to put his plan into execution, summoned the lords of Otompan and of Chalco, in whose fidelity he § 11. had more confidence, and whose states were in a situation very suited Tragic death of King

Ixtlilxochitl, and to his purpose, and charged them to raise with the greatest secrecy tyranny of

Tezozomoc. possible a good army and to station it in ambush on a mountain near the camp of the king of Texcoco: that they should then send to the royal camp two captains, of the shrewdest and the bravest, who, under the pretext of communicating to the king some most important secret, should try to draw him as far as possible from his people, and then without delay kill him. Everything happened as the malicious prince had planned. The king was then in the neighborhood of Tlaxcala; he had no suspicion of the two captains who came to him, and fell incautiously into the snare. The deed was carried out in sight of the royal army, though at some small distance. They ran at once to punish the rashness of those two wicked captains; but the army of the conspirators coming up, which was more numerous, they were quickly routed. The royal corpse could scarcely be saved to have its obsequies performed, and the heir prince, who was a witness of the tragic end of his father, had need to hide among some bushes to escape the fury of the enemies. Thus ended the unfortunate king Ixtlilxochitl, after seven years of reign, in 1410.

He left several sons, and among them Nezahualcoyotl, heir to the crown, whom he had by Matlalcihuatzin, daughter of Acamapichtli, king of Mexico. (d) This prince was endowed with a

great

(d) Torquemada makes Matlalcihuatzin a daughter of Huitzilihuitl; but how? He says that this king, when he ascended the throne, was not more than 17 years old, nor was he yet married, and that he reigned 22, or at most 26 years. On the other hand he represents Nezahualcoyotl, at the death of his alleged grandfather, at an age to go to war and to conduct negotiations to secure the crown: whence it must be said that Huitzilihuitl, before counting 26 years of marriage, already had grandchildren of at least 20 years. [p. 190] great intelligence and of an incomparable magnanimity, and worthier Bk. III. than any other to occupy the throne of Acolhuacan; but he could not,

because of the overbearing power of Tezozomoc, take possession of

the throne due to him on so many titles, except after some years and

endless dangers and conflicts.

The perfidious Tezozomoc had prepared large bodies of troops, so

that, once the premeditated blow against the person of the king had

been struck by the people of Otompan and the Chalcas, they might

fall upon the cities of Texcoco, Huexotla, Coatlichan, Coatepec, and

Iztapalocan, which had been the most faithful to their lord, and put

them to fire and flame. The inhabitants of those cities who were able

to save themselves by flight went from there over the mountains to

take refuge among the Huexotzincas and the Tlaxcalans; all the oth‐

ers died defending their country; but they sold their lives dear, for in‐

finite was the blood shed on both sides. If one wishes to seek the

cause of so many evils, no other will be found than the ambition of a

prince. Would to God that the ravages of the passions were rarer in

the world and less violent! The ill-governed passion of one prince, or

of one minister, is enough to flood the fields with human blood, to

ruin cities, to overturn kingdoms, and to turn the whole earth upside

down.

The cruelty of the Tyrant being at last sated with the oppression of

his enemies, he had himself sworn king of Acolhuacan in the city of

Texcoco, granting to all those who had taken up arms against him a

general pardon and freedom to return to their homes. He gave the

city of Texcoco in fief to Chimalpopoca, king of Mexico, and that of

Huexotla to Tlacateotl, king of Tlatelolco, as a reward for the great

services they had rendered him in the war. He set governors loyal to

himself in other places, and declared Azcapotzalco the court and cap‐

ital of the whole kingdom of Acolhuacan.

There were present at this ceremony, though in disguise, several

persons of those opposed to the Tyrant, and among them Prince

Nezahualcoyotl. The grief and rage that the latter

felt [p. 191] felt at it aroused in him youthful ardor, and were on the point of hurling him into a rash action against his enemies, had not a confidant of Bk. III. his who accompanied him deterred him, representing to him the fatal consequences of his rashness, and showing him how much better it would be to wait for time to bring some more opportune occasion of recovering the crown and of avenging himself on his enemies: that the Tyrant was already decrepit, and that his death, which could not be long in coming, would change the state of affairs altogether: that the very peoples would come to submit to their lawful lord, compelled by the injustice and cruelty of the usurper. On this same occasion a Mexican officer of considerable standing (probably Itzcoatl, brother of the king and general of the Mexican armies), either on his own authority or by order of King Chimalpopoca, climbed up on the temple which the Toltec nation had in that court, and spoke thus to the immense crowd that was there: “Hear, Chichimecs, hear, Acolhuas, and all of you who are gathered here: let no one dare do any harm to our son Nezahualcoyotl, nor allow another to do it to him, unless he wishes to submit to a rigorous punishment.” This proclamation served greatly for the safety of the heir prince, no one wishing to bring upon himself the anger of a nation that was beginning to make itself respected.

Not long afterward many of the nobles who, to escape the fury of the Tepanec troops, had taken refuge in Huexotzinco and in Tlaxcala, assembled in Papalotla, a place near Texcoco, to deliberate on the course they should take in the present circumstances, and all agreed to submit to the new lords whom the Usurper had set over them in their cities, both to free themselves from so many hostilities and to be able to attend quietly to their homes and families.

The Tyrant, having satisfied his ambition by the usurpation of the §Burdens

12.

imposed by kingdom of Acolhuacan, and his cruelty by the slaughters committed the Tyrant. there, wished also to gratify his greed with burdens upon his subjects. He prescribed to them that, besides the tribute,

which [p. 192] which they had formerly paid to their king in provisions and in cloth Bk. III.

for clothing, they should pay him another in gold and precious stones,

without perceiving how much he would embitter by such burdens the

minds of his subjects, whom he ought rather to have conciliated by

moderation and gentleness, in order to make more secure the posses‐

sion of a throne established upon injustice and cruelty. The Toltec

and Chichimec nobles replied to the criers that they wished to

present themselves in person to the king to speak to him about this

matter. The Tyrant’s haughtiness seemed to them excessive, and his

conduct too different from the moderation of the ancient kings from

whom he descended. They therefore agreed to send him two orators,

the ablest there were, a Toltec and a Chichimec (*), so that each of

them, in the name of his nation, might make strong and effective re‐

monstrances to him. Both went to Azcapotzalco, and, being intro‐

duced to an audience with the Tyrant, after making a very deep bow,

the Toltec spoke first, out of regard for the greater antiquity of his

nation in that country, and set before him the humble beginnings of

the Toltecs, and the hardships they endured before arriving at the

splendor and glory which they enjoyed for some time, and the misery

to which they were reduced after their overthrow; he described the

lamentable dispersion in which they were found by Xolotl when he

came to that land, and, running through the two centuries that fol‐

lowed, he made a pathetic enumeration of the hardships they suf‐

fered, to move the Tyrant to compassion and to spare his nation the

new burden.

Scarcely had the Toltec finished his address when the Chichimec

began his. “I, lord,” he said, “can speak with greater confidence and

freedom, since I am a Chichimec, and I speak to a prince of my own

nation, as one descended from those great kings Xolotl, Nopaltzin,

and Tlotzin. You are not unaware, great lord, that those divine

Chichimecs

(*) The name of the Toltec orator was Cuatlihuac, and that of the Chichimec

Tequiquixnahuacatl.

[p. 193]

Chichimecs, your forefathers, took no account of gold and gems. They put no other crown on their heads than a garland of herbs and Bk. III. wild flowers, nor did they adorn themselves with any other bracelets than the rough leather against which the bowstring struck in shooting. Their food at first was reduced to raw flesh and insipid herbs, and their clothing to the skins of deer and of wild beasts which they themselves hunted. Once they had learned agriculture from the Toltecs, the kings themselves tilled the earth, to encourage their subjects to labor by their example. The opulence and glory to which fortune afterward raised them did not make them any prouder. They made use of their subjects, it is true, as kings; but as fathers they loved them, and were content to be acknowledged by them with the humble gifts of the earth. I present to you, lord, these illustrious examples of your forefathers for no other reason than to beg you most humbly not to wish to exact from us more than those men exacted from our ancestors.” The Tyrant listened to the one address and the other, and although the comparison made with the ancient kings vexed him, he nevertheless concealed his displeasure, and contented himself, in dismissing the orators, with confirming the order published concerning the new burden.

Meanwhile Nezahualcoyotl kept going about, ever anxious, through several cities, seeking to win over minds in order to restore himself to the throne. But although his subjects loved him, and wished to see him in possession of the kingdom, they did not dare to favor his party openly, for fear of the Tyrant. Among the subjects most closely related to him who abandoned him were the lord of Chimalpan, his uncle, and Tecpanecatl, brother of his second wife Nezahualxochitl, of the royal line of Mexico. Persevering in such negotiations, he arrived one evening at a country estate in the province of Chalco, belonging to a widow lady named Tziltomiauh. He observed that there was a plantation of maguei there, from which the widow drew wine not only for the use of her household but also to sell, which was strictly forbidden by the Chichimec laws. He was inflamed

History of Mexico, Vol. I.              Bb                   with [p. 194] with such zeal for the laws of his fathers that, without either the ad‐

Bk. III.

versity of his fortune or any other consideration availing to stop him,

he forthwith killed the guilty woman with his own hand. An altogeth‐

er inconsiderate and reprehensible act, in which the ardor of youth

had more part than prudence. This deed made a great stir in that

province, and the lord of Chalco, who was his enemy and had been an

accomplice in the death of his father, diligently sought to get him

into his hands; but the prince, foreseeing the consequences of his

deed, had already put himself in safety.

§ 13.    Eight years had already passed since Tezozomoc had been in Death of the Tyrant

Tezozomoc. peaceful possession of the kingdom of Acolhuacan, claimed in vain by

Nezahualcoyotl, when baleful dreams threw him into great consterna‐

tion. He dreamed that Nezahualcoyotl, transformed into an eagle,

opened his breast and ate his heart; and another time, that the same,

transformed into a lion, licked his body and sucked his blood. He was

so frightened by such tragic imaginings, formed in him by the very

consciousness of his injustice and tyranny, that, calling his three sons

Tayatzin, Teuctzintli, and Maxtla, after relating the dreams to them he

charged them to put Nezahualcoyotl to death as soon as possible,

provided they did it so secretly that no one could suspect the author

of such a death. He survived these dreams by scarcely a year. He was

already so old that, being unable to keep warm or to sit up any longer

in a chair, it was necessary to keep him wholly covered with cotton

inside a large osier basket made in the fashion of a cradle; but from

this cradle, or rather sepulchre, he tyrannized over the kingdom of

Acolhuacan, and gave out oracles of injustice. Shortly before dying he

declared his son Tayatzin his successor in the kingdom, and again

commanded the death of Nezahualcoyotl, keeping his perverse de‐

signs up to the last breath. Thus ended his long life this monster of

ambition, of perfidy, and of injustice, in 1422, after having tyrannized

for nine

years [p. 195] years the kingdom of Acolhuacan, and having possessed for very

Bk. III. many the state of Azcapotzalco. (e)

Although it belonged to Tayatzin, as successor to the crown, to give the appropriate orders for the funeral of his father, nevertheless his brother Maxtla, being bolder and more active, assumed the right to himself, and then began to command with as much authority as if he were already in possession of the kingdom to which he aspired, thinking it quite easy to oppress his brother, who was a man of little account and not at all versed in government. Maxtla had the kings of Mexico and of Tlatelolco and the other lords notified, so that they might honor with their presence and their tears the obsequies of their common lord. Nezahualcoyotl, though not summoned, chose nevertheless to be present, in order to observe, as may be believed, with his own eyes the disposition of the court. He went accompanied by an intimate confidant of his and by some attendants, and, having entered the hall of the royal palace where the body lay exposed, he found there the kings of Mexico and of Tlatelolco, the three princes, sons of the Tyrant, and other lords. He saluted them all one by one, in the order in which they were seated, beginning with the king of Mexico, and presented them with bunches of flowers according to the custom of that country. The compliments being over, he sat down beside King Chimalpopoca, his brother-in-law, to keep him company in his mourning. Teuctzintli, one of the sons of Tezozomoc and heir of his cruelty, thinking this a good occasion for carrying out the wicked commission of his

Bb2                       father

(e) Torquemada makes Tezozomoc the immediate son of the first Acolhua prince, and so makes him reign 160 or 180 years; but from the very address made by the Chichimec orator it is clear to us that Tezozomoc was a descendant of Xolotl, of Nopaltzin, and of Tlotzin. Now the sister of Nopaltzin married Prince Acolhuatzin, so that their children were cousins of Tlotzin, son of Nopaltzin. In all this Torquemada agrees with us. Who, then, was ever called a descendant of his cousin? Whoever will read the genealogy of the Chichimec kings in Torquemada’s work will at once perceive the blunders made by this author. It may be that there were two or three lords of Azcapotzalco named Tezozomoc; but the one who tyrannized over Acolhuacan was at most a great-grandson of Prince Acolhuatzin. [p. 196] father against Nezahualcoyotl, proposed it to his brother Maxtla. But Bk. III.

the latter, though he had a heart no less inhuman, had nevertheless a

better head. “Banish,” Maxtla answered him, “banish from your mind

such a thought. What would men say of us, seeing us contrive the

death of another when we ought to be mourning that of our father?

They would say that the grief is not heavy that leaves room for ambi‐

tion and for vengeance. Time will bring us some more opportune oc‐

casion to carry out our father’s resolve without incurring the hatred

of our subjects. Nezahualcoyotl is not invisible. If he does not hide in

fire, in water, or in the bowels of the earth, he will infallibly fall into

our hands.” This happened on the fourth day after the death of the

Tyrant, on which the body was burned, and his ashes were buried

with extraordinary pomp and solemnity.

The next day the kings of Mexico and of Tlatelolco returned to

their cities, and Maxtla began at once, with less dissimulation, to re‐

veal his ambitious design of making himself master of the kingdom,

showing by his arrogance and boldness that where his arts did not

suffice he would use force. Tayatzin had not the courage to oppose it,

knowing the bold and violent temper of his brother, and his advan‐

tage in having subjects accustomed to obey him. He therefore took

the course of going to Mexico to confer with King Chimalpopoca, to

whom he had been chiefly commended by his father, on so arduous a

matter. He was received by the king with singular demonstrations of

esteem, and after the usual compliments Chimalpopoca said to him:

What are you doing, O prince? Is not the kingdom yours? Did not your father

leave it to you? Why then, seeing yourself unjustly despoiled of it, do you not

strive to recover it? Because my rights matter little, answered Tayatzin, if

my subjects do not help me. My brother has made himself master of the king‐

dom, and there is no one to gainsay him. It would be rashness to oppose him

with no other power, nor other forces, than my desires and the justice of my

cause.

What

[p. 197]

What cannot be done by force, replied Chimalpopoca, is made up by indus‐

Bk. III. try. I shall suggest to you a means of freeing yourself from your brother, and of putting you without danger in possession of the throne. Excuse yourself from living in the palace of your late father on the pretext that your grief is revived there by the remembrance of his deeds and of the love he bore you, and that you therefore wish to build yourself another palace for your residence. When it is finished, give a sumptuous dinner, and invite your brother; and there, in the midst of the merriment, it will be easy for you, with people secretly prepared, to free your kingdom from a Tyrant, and yourself from a rival so pernicious and so unjust: and in order that you may the better succeed, I shall be at your aid with my own person and with all the forces of my nation. To such counsel Tayatzin answered nothing, except with the melancholy of his look, caused by love of his own blood, or by the baseness of the action proposed to him.

Of all this conversation there was a witness, a retainer of Tayatzin, who had stayed in a place from which he could listen at his ease, and who, hoping to make his fortune by a betrayal, left that evening secretly for Azcapotzalco, went straight to the palace, and, having obtained an audience of Maxtla, revealed to him what he had heard. His mind was in an instant torn by the anger, the fear, and the grief that this report aroused in him; but being a politician, and practiced in concealing his feelings, he pretended to despise it, and severely reproached the informer for his boldness and rashness in slandering such distinguished persons; he called him drunk, and sent him home to sleep off the wine. He spent the rest of the night deliberating on the course to be taken, and determined at last to forestall his brother and to make him fall into his own nets.

The next morning he summoned the people of Azcapotzalco, and § 14.

Maxtla, Tyrant of told them that, since he could not remain in the palace of his father, Acolhuacan. because it belonged to Prince Tayatzin, and since he moreover needed a house in that court, where he could lodge whenever, for some

business, [p. 198] business, he had to come from his state of Coyoacán, he wished them Bk. III.

to show him the love they bore him by the very speedy construction

of such a building. So great was the diligence of the people of

Azcapotzalco, and so great the multitude of workmen who flocked to

it, that Tayatzin, who had stayed in Mexico no more than three days,

found on his return to Azcapotzalco the building already begun.

Astonished at such a novelty, and asking Maxtla the reason, he was

told that, since he had to leave him the royal house so as not to preju‐

dice his rights, he was building himself another, in which he might

lodge whenever he came to court. The good Tayatzin was satisfied

with such an answer, and easily persuaded himself that Maxtla no

longer thought of the usurpation of the crown. The building being

finished after a short time, Maxtla invited to a banquet his brothers,

the kings of Mexico and of Tlatelolco, and other lords. Tayatzin, be‐

ing wholly ignorant of the treachery of his retainer, did not perceive

the snare that was being laid for him; but Chimalpopoca, who was

shrewder and more cautious, must have suspected the treachery, and

courteously excused himself from attending. When the day appointed

for so great a function arrived, the invited guests gathered at the new

house, and when they were most absorbed in the merriment of the

banquet, and perhaps still more heated by the wine, which is the

most opportune occasion for such crimes, armed men entered sud‐

denly and fell with such violence upon the unfortunate Tayatzin that

he scarcely opened his eyes to look at his murderers before they were

closed by death. The whole company was thrown into confusion by so

unexpected a tragedy; but Maxtla quieted it, laying bare the treachery

contrived against him, and protesting that in this he had done noth‐

ing else than forestall the blow that hung over him. With these and

like speeches he so changed their minds that, instead of avenging the

death of their lawful lord, they acclaimed the perfidious Tyrant king;

but if injustice raised him to the throne, it was only to cast him down

from a greater height.

Much greater was Maxtla’s indignation against the king of Mexico;

but it did not seem to him fitting to make an attempt

against [p. 199] against his life until he should see himself well secured on the throne. Meanwhile he vented his rage in insults against his person and in out‐ Bk. III. rages offered to his dignity. Soon after his intrusion into the kingdom,

§ 15. there was sent to him by the king of Mexico the present that it was Insults offered by the the custom to make every year in token of acknowledgment of the Tyrant           to the king of

Mexico. overlordship of the king of Azcapotzalco. This present, consisting of three baskets of fish, of shrimps, and of frogs, and of some vegetables, was carried by persons of rank of the court of Chimalpopoca with a fair speech and with singular expressions of submission and respect. Maxtla showed himself pleased with it; but, since according to the custom of those nations he had to reciprocate with some gift, and wishing besides to take revenge, after consulting his confidants he had delivered to the Mexican ambassadors for their king a Cueitl, that is, a kind of skirt, and a Huepilli, which was a woman’s shift, signifying by this that he held him effeminate and cowardly: an insult the most vexing to those peoples, since there was nothing they prized so much as the reputation of being courageous. Chimalpopoca’s displeasure was also very great, and he would have wished to avenge the outrage; but he could not.

Such a scorn was soon followed by a most grievous offense against honor. The Tyrant learned that among the wives of the Mexican king there was one singularly beautiful, and, inflamed by this mere report with wicked desires, he determined to sacrifice honesty and justice to his passion. To attain his end he made use of certain Tepanec ladies, charging them that when they visited, as they were wont to do, that Mexican lady, they should invite her to come to them at Azcapotzalco to divert herself for some days. Such visits being at that time quite frequent even among persons of the first rank and of different nations, it was not difficult for the villainous prince to seize the occasion he so much desired to satisfy his guilty passion, neither the tears nor the efforts used by that honest lady in defense of her honor sufficing to restrain him: whereupon she returned to Mexico full of ignominy,

and [p. 200] and pierced by the keenest grief, to complain to her husband. This

Bk. III. unfortunate king, either so as not to survive his dishonor, or so as not

to have to die in the hands of the Tyrant, resolved to put an end to his

bitter life, dying sacrificed in honor of his god Huitzilopochtli, as cer‐

tain so-called heroes of his nation had done; believing that such a

death must wipe out the infamy received, and free him from the igno‐

minious end which he feared from his enemy. He communicated this

resolution to his courtiers, and they praised it, for the extravagant

ideas they had in matters of religion, and some of them even wished

to be made sharers in the glory of so barbarous a sacrifice.

§ 16.     When the day assigned for the religious tragedy came, the king apImprisonment and peared dressed as they represented their god Huitzilopochtli, and all

death of King

Chimalpopoca. the others who were to accompany him likewise put on the best gar‐

ments they had. The ceremony began with a solemn dance; and while

it lasted, the priests went on sacrificing one by one those unfortunate

victims, reserving the king for the last. It was not possible that so

strange a novelty should be unknown to the Tyrant: he learned of it in

advance, and in order that his enemy might not by a voluntary death

escape his vengeance, he sent a body of troops to surprise him before

the sacrifice. They arrived, in fact, when barely two victims remained,

after whom the king himself was to be sacrificed. This unhappy

prince was seized by the Tepanecs and taken at once to Azcapotzalco,

where he was put in a strong wooden cage, which was, as we shall say

elsewhere, the prison used by those nations, under a strong guard. In

this event there are certain circumstances that make assent a little

difficult; but I give the account just as I find it among the historians

of Mexico. And it is indeed to be wondered at that the Tepanecs had

the boldness to enter that city and to make so perilous an attempt,

and that the Mexicans did not arm in defense of their king; but the

power of the Tyrant could without doubt embolden the Tepanecs and

frighten the Mexicans.

With

[p. 201]

With the imprisonment of Chimalpopoca Maxtla’s desire revived to seize also Prince Nezahualcoyotl, and in order to obtain him more Bk. III. easily he had him summoned under the pretext of wishing to make an agreement with him about the crown of Acolhuacan. The shrewd prince at once perceived the malign intention of the Tyrant; but the ardor of his age, and the courage, or rather the rashness, of his spirit made him present himself intrepidly to the gravest perils. In passing through Tlatelolco he visited a confidant of his called Chichincatl, who let him know that the Tyrant was not only plotting against his life and that of the king of Tlatelolco, but would even, if he could, annihilate the whole Acolhua nation. Nevertheless, without being frightened, he betook himself to Azcapotzalco in the evening, and went straight to the house of a friend of his. Early in the morning he went to find Chachaton, a great favorite of the Tyrant, by whom the prince himself was loved, and commended himself to him, that he might dissuade Maxtla from doing anything against his person. The two went together to the palace, and Chachaton went in first to inform his lord of the prince’s coming and to speak to him in his favor. Then the prince entered, and after his compliments said to him thus: “I know, lord, that you have imprisoned the king of Mexico, and I do not know whether you have already put him to death, or whether he still lives in prison. I have also heard it said that you wish to put me to death too. If it is truly so, here I am before you: kill me with your own hands, so that your anger may be vented against a prince no less innocent than unfortunate.” As he said these words, the remembrance of his misfortunes drew some tears from his eyes. “What do you think?” said Maxtla then to his favorite. “Is it any wonder that a young man who has scarcely begun to enjoy life should seek death so intrepidly?” And turning to the prince, he assured him that he was plotting nothing against his life: that the king of Mexico was not dead, nor would he ever have him put to death, and he sought at the same time to justify himself in regard to the imprisonment

History of Mexico, Vol. I.               Cc                      of [p. 202] of that unfortunate king. He then ordered that the prince be suitably Bk. III. lodged.

Chimalpopoca, informed of the arrival at the court of the prince,

his brother-in-law, sent to beg him to come and see him in the prison.

The prince went to him, having first obtained Maxtla’s consent, and

on entering the prison embraced him, both showing great tenderness

in their looks and in their expressions. Chimalpopoca set before him

the series of his misfortunes, made clear to him the malign intentions

of the Tyrant against them both, and begged him never to return to

the court; for his cruel enemy would infallibly put him to death, and

the Acolhua nation would be left wholly abandoned. “Finally,” he said

to him, “since my death is inevitable, I beg you earnestly to take care

of my poor Mexicans. Be to them a true friend and father. And in to‐

ken of the love I bear you, accept this pendant, which was once my

brother Huitzilihuitl’s.” And taking from his lip a gold pendant that

he had, he gave it to him, together with earrings and other gems that

he kept in the prison, and to a retainer who accompanied the prince

he gave other things. Then, with great sorrow, they took leave of each

other, so that a longer stay in the visit might not cause some suspi‐

cion. Nezahualcoyotl, following the advice given him, left the court at

once, and never returned to present himself to the Tyrant. He went

to Tlatelolco, and, having taken there a boat and good rowers, has‐

tened to Texcoco.

Chimalpopoca remained in his bitter solitude, turning over

thoughts of affliction. Each day the imprisonment became more in‐

tolerable to him: he had no hope of recovering his liberty, nor of be‐

ing useful to his nation in the little time of life that remained to him.

“If I must at last die,” he said, “how much better, and more glorious,

will it be for me to die by my own hands than by those of a perfidious

and cruel Tyrant! Since I cannot take any other vengeance on him, at

least I will not leave him the pleasure that

he [p. 203] he would then have in the choice of the time and of the kind of death with which I must end my unhappy days. I wish to be master of my Bk. III. life, to choose the time and the manner of dying, and to be also the executor of my own death, so that there may be so much the less ignominy in it, the less influence the will of my enemy has upon it.” (*) With such a resolution, so conformable to the ideas of those nations, he hanged himself from a beam of the cage, making use, as is to be believed, of his own girdle.

With an end so tragic the third king of Mexico finished his calamitous life. We have no more precise accounts either of his character or of the progress which the nation made in the time of his reign, which lasted about thirteen years, having ended in 1423, about a year after the death of Tezozomoc. All that is known of him is that in the eleventh year of his reign he had a great stone brought to Mexico, to serve as an altar for the ordinary sacrifice of prisoners, and another, round and larger, for the gladiatorial sacrifice, of which we shall speak elsewhere. In the fourth painting of the Mendoza Collection are represented the victories gained by the Mexicans in the reign of Chimalpopoca over the cities of Chalco and of Tequixquiac, and the naval engagement which they had with the Chalcas, with the loss of some men and of some boats overturned by the enemy. The Interpreter of that collection adds that Chimalpopoca left many sons born of his concubines.

As soon as Maxtla learned of the death of his illustrious prisoner, §Imprisonment

16.

and falling into a rage because his designs had been upset, and fearing death of King that Nezahualcoyotl too might escape his vengeance, he resolved to Chimalpopoca. anticipate in any way he could the death which he had not yet given him, either because he had not been able to carry it out in the manner ordered him by his father, or because, as other historians affirm, he had been frightened by

Cc2                      certain

(*) These last words of Chimalpopoca, reported by the historians of Mexico, could be learned from the testimony of the guards who were around the cage, or prison. [p. 204] certain omens of the priests; but his anger was already in a state not Bk. III.

to be restrained by motives of religion; so he gave four of the bravest

captains the order to seek that prince everywhere and, without fail,

to take his life wherever they should find him. The Tepanec captains

set out with few men, so that the noise might not make the prey es‐

cape, and went straight to Texcoco, where on their arrival

Nezahualcoyotl was playing ball with a retainer of his called Ocelotl.

This prince, in every place where he went to draw minds to his party,

occupied himself in dances, games, and other amusements, so that

the governors of those places, who by order of the Tyrant watched

over his conduct and observed his steps, seeing him employed in pas‐

times, might persuade themselves that he no longer thought of the

crown, and neglect to observe him. Thus he carried on his negotia‐

tions without causing the slightest suspicion. On this occasion, be‐

fore the captains entered his house, the prince learned that they were

Tepanecs and that they came armed; whence, suspecting what it

might be, he left the game and withdrew to the innermost rooms.

Then, informed by the porter that the Tepanecs were asking for him,

he ordered Ocelotl to receive them and to tell them that he would be

with them after they had dined and rested. It did not seem to the

Tepanecs that they ought to lose the opportunity by putting off the

blow, and perhaps they did not yet dare to carry out the commission

until they had made sure that there was inside the house no one capa‐

ble of offering resistance; so after resting they sat down to table, and

while they were dining the prince escaped by a secret door, and, going

out of the city, walked more than a mile to Coatitlan, a small place of

weavers, all of them people faithful and attached to him, where he re‐

mained hidden for the time. (f) The Tepanecs,

having

(f) Torquemada says that the prince went out of his house by a certain kind of

labyrinth, which he had had made with so many turns and intricacies that the way

out was impossible to anyone who did not know its arrangement, whose secret [p. 205] having waited a good while after dinner, and seeing that neither the prince let himself be seen nor his retainer Ocelotl, they searched for Bk. III. him through the whole house without finding in it any person who could give them word of him. Assured, therefore, of his flight, they went out at once to look for him everywhere, and having learned from a peasant on the road to Coatitlan that he had taken refuge in that place, they entered it with an armed force, threatening the inhabitants with death if they did not reveal the fugitive prince; but, by a rare example of fidelity, there was not in the whole place anyone who would betray him, although some were killed for this reason. Among those who sacrificed their lives to fidelity were Tochmantzin, overseer of all the looms of Coatitlan, and Matlalintzin, a noble lady. The Tepanecs, unable to find the prince there in spite of their diligence and of the cruelty they had used against the inhabitants, went out to look for him through the countryside; and Nezahualcoyotl also went out by another way, and took a road opposite to that of his enemies; but as they were searching for him everywhere, he found himself in great danger of falling into their hands, had he not been hidden by certain peasants inside a heap of the herb chian that was on the threshing floor.

Safe from such a danger, the prince went to spend the night at Tetzcotzinco, a pleasant country house built by his ancestors for their recreation. There six lords were waiting for him, who, outside their states, were wandering through the cities of the kingdom. There they §Negotiations

18.

of held that night a secret council, and resolved to solicit the aid of the Nezahualcoyotl to

obtain the crown. Chalcas, although they had been accomplices in the death of King Ixtlilxochitl. The next morning, early, the prince went to Matlallan and to other places, admonishing those of his party to be prepared with their arms for the time of his return. Two days he spent in these negotiations, and on the evening of the second

day was known to none but the prince himself and some of his intimate confidants. It is not indeed incredible that he himself designed such a building, since his intelligence was marvelous, and in every respect he showed lights superior to those of all his countrymen. [p. 206] day he was in Apan, where he was met by the ambassadors of the

Bk. III.

Cholulans, who offered to help him in the war against the Tyrant. There

also two lords of his party joined him with the sad news of the death of

Huitzilihuitl, one of his favorites, who had been put to the torture by the

Tyrant that he might reveal certain secrets, and who, being faithful to his

master, would not disclose them to him, and ended his life in the tor‐

ments. With this grief he passed from Apan to Huexotzinco, whose lord

was a relative of his, who received him with singular affection and com‐

passion, and promised also to aid him with all his forces. From there he

went to Tlaxcala, where he was magnificently received, and in this city

they agreed upon the time and the place at which the troops of Cholula,

of Huexotzinco, and of Tlaxcala should assemble. When he left this last

city to go to Calpulalpan, a place situated midway on the road from

Tlaxcala to Texcoco, he was accompanied by so great a nobility that he

seemed more a king going out on a pleasure trip with his court than a

fugitive prince seeking the means of getting possession of the crown

usurped from him. In Calpulalpan he received the reply of the Chalcas, in

which they declared themselves ready to serve their lawful lord against

the wicked usurper. It is to be believed that the cruelty and insolence of

the Tyrant alienated many peoples from him, besides which the Chalcas

were too inconstant and too ready to adhere now to one party, now to an‐

other, as will be seen in the course of this history.

While Prince Nezahualcoyotl was rousing the peoples to war, the

§ 19. Itzcoatl, fourth king Mexicans, finding themselves without a king and afflicted by the

of Mexico. Tepanecs, deliberated on putting at the head of the nation a man capable

of repressing the insolence of the Tyrant and of avenging the very grave

injuries received from him. Having assembled, therefore, for the election

of the new king, an authoritative old man spoke thus to the other elec‐

tors: “You have lost, O noble Mexicans, in the death of your king the light

of your eyes; but you still have that of reason with which to elect a worthy

successor. The Mexican nobility has not ended in Chimalpopoca; there

still remain several excellent princes,

brothers [p. 207] brothers of his, from among whom you can choose a lord who will govern you and a father who will welcome you. Imagine that for a Bk. III. short time the sun has been eclipsed and the earth darkened, and that now the light returns to us in the new king. What matters is that, without lingering in long conferences, we elect a prince who will restore the honor of our nation, avenge the affronts done to it, and set it again in its primitive liberty.” They proceeded at once to the election, and Prince Itzcoatl was elected by common consent, half-brother on the father’s side of the two preceding kings, and natural son of Acamapichtli, born of a slave woman. As much as he might lose in esteem by the unfortunate condition of his mother, so much was he worthy by the nobility and renown of his father, and far more by his own virtues, of which he gave many examples in the post of general of the Mexican armies, which he had held for more than thirty years. He was esteemed the most prudent, upright, and valiant man of the whole nation. Placed upon the Tlatocaicpalli, or royal seat, he was saluted as king by all the nobility with singular acclamations. Then one of the orators made him an address on the obligations of a sovereign: “All of us, O great king and lord,” he said to him among other things, “all of us now hang upon you. On your shoulders are borne the old, the orphans, and the widows. Will you have the heart to lay down this burden?
