legumes there was no number, nor even of the deer, the rabbits, the

ducks, the quail, and other birds. Everyone can easily understand how

great the hardship of the subjects must have been in amassing

so

(*) The historian Torquemada had the aforesaid paintings in his hands, as he himself

bears witness.

(f) The fanega is a Spanish measure of dry goods, containing about a hundred Spanish

pounds of common wheat, or more than 130 Roman pounds. [p. 245] so great a quantity of maize and cacao; especially since the cacao had

Bk. IV. to be procured through commerce with the hot countries, there being in the whole kingdom of Acolhuacan no land suited to the cultivation of this plant. For half the year, that is, for nine Mexican months, fourteen cities supplied this provision, and another fifteen the provision of the other half of the year. (g) To the young men was assigned the provision of the firewood, which was consumed in the royal palace in surprising quantity.

The progress made by this celebrated king in the arts and in the sciences was as great as can be made by a great mind that has neither books in which to study nor masters from whom to learn. He was skilled in the poetry of those nations, and made various compositions that were universally applauded. In the sixteenth century the sixty hymns composed by him in praise of the Creator of Heaven were famous even among the Spaniards. Two of his odes or songs, rendered into Spanish verse by his descendant Don Fernando de Alva Ixtlilxochitl, have been preserved down to our own times. (h) One of them was composed by him some time after the ruin of Azcapotzalco. Its subject, not unlike that of the other of which we have already made mention, is to lament the instability of human greatness in the person of the Tyrant Tezozomoc, who, like a great and thick tree, had spread his roots through so many countries and extended his green branches so far as to shade all the lands of the empire; but at last, worm-eaten and spoiled, fell to the ground without any hope of recovering his former greenness.

But

(g) The fourteen cities charged with the provision of the first half of the year were Texcoco, Huexotla, Coatlichan, Atenco, Chiauhtla, Tezoyocan, Papalotla, Tepetlaoztoc, Acolman, Tepechpan, Xaltocan, Chimalhuacan, Iztapalocan, and Coatepec. The other fifteen were Otompan, Aztaquemecan, Teotihuacan, Cempoallan, Axapochco, Tlalanapan, Tepepolco, Tizayocan, Ahuatepec, Oztoticpac, Cuauhtlatzinco, Cojoac, Oztotlatlauhcan, Achichillacachocan, and Tetliztacac.

(h) The Chevalier Boturini had these two odes of Nezahualcoyotl among his precious antiquities. I wish I had them too, so that I might publish them in this History.

[p. 246]

But in nothing did Nezahualcoyotl delight so much as in the study of Bk. IV.

nature. He also acquired several astronomical notions by the frequent

observation that he made of the course of the stars. He applied him‐

self likewise to knowing the plants and the animals, and because he

could not keep in his court those that were proper to a different

clime, he had all the vegetables and animals of the land of Anahuac

painted from life in his palaces; of which paintings the celebrated Dr.

Hernández bears witness, who saw them and made use of them in

part. Paintings truly far more useful and more worthy of a royal

palace than those that represent the wicked mythology of the

Greeks. He curiously investigated the causes of the effects that he ad‐

mired in nature, and this continual observation made him recognize

the folly of idolatry. To his sons he said privately that, in outwardly

adoring the idols to conform with the people, they should detest in

their minds that worship, worthy of scorn, because it was directed to

insensible creatures; that he acknowledged no other God than the

Creator of Heaven, and that he did not forbid idolatry in his kingdom,

as he would wish, so as not to be blamed for wanting to contradict

the doctrine of his ancestors. He prohibited sacrifices of human vic‐

tims; but, perceiving afterward how difficult it is to turn a nation

away from its ancient ideas in matters of religion, he returned to per‐

mitting them, but commanding under heavy penalties that no others

should ever be sacrificed than prisoners of war. He built in honor of

the Creator of Heaven a lofty tower of nine stories. The last story was

dark, with a small vault painted on the inside with blue and adorned

with gold moldings. There always resided in this tower men charged

with sounding, at certain hours of the day, plates of very fine metal, at

the sound of which the king knelt to make his prayer to the Creator

of Heaven, and in honor of that God he made at a certain time of the

year a fast. (i)

The

(i) All the aforesaid anecdotes have been taken from the precious manuscripts of

Don Fernando de Alva. As a great-great-grandson of that king, he was able to receive

by word of mouth many accounts from his fathers and grandfathers.

[p. 247]

The lofty genius of this king, impelled by the great love that he bore

Bk. IV. his people, so illustrated his court that in the future it was considered the homeland of the arts and the center of civilization. Texcoco was the city where the Mexican language was spoken with the greatest polish and perfection, where the best artisans were found, and where poets, orators, and historians were most abundant. (k) Hence the Mexicans and other peoples took many laws: wherefore it might be said that Texcoco had been the Athens, and Nezahualcoyotl the Solon, of Anahuac.

In his last illness, having had all his sons come into his presence, he declared as his heir and successor in the kingdom of Acolhuacan Nezahualpilli, who, although he was the youngest of all, was nevertheless preferred to the others, both for having been born of Queen Matlalcihuatzin and for his well-known rectitude and his singular talent. He charged his eldest son Acapipioltzin to aid the new king with his counsel until he should learn the difficult art of governing. To Nezahualpilli he warmly recommended love of his brothers, care of his subjects, and zeal for justice. Finally, to prevent any disorder that the news of his death might cause, he commanded that it be concealed from the people, as far as possible, until Nezahualpilli should be secure in the peaceful possession of the crown. The princes received with tears the last counsels of their father, and, coming out into the audience hall, where the nobility awaited them, Nezahualpilli was acclaimed king of Acolhuacan, Acapipioltzin declaring that this was the will of their father, who, having a long journey to make, wished first to name his successor. All gave obedience to the new king, and the next morning Nezahualcoyotl died, in the forty-fourth year of his reign and about the eightieth of his age. His sons kept his death secret, and hid

his

(k) In the list that we have given of the historians of that kingdom, it can be seen that some of them were of the royal family of Texcoco. [p. 248] his body, burning it secretly, as is to be believed, and instead of hold‐

Bk. IV. ing funeral rites for him, celebrated with extraordinary festivities and

rejoicings the coronation of the new king. But in spite of their efforts

the news of his death spread at once through the whole land, and

many lords came to the court to condole with the princes.

Nevertheless the common people remained persuaded that that great

king had been transported to the company of the gods as a reward for

his virtues.

§ 16.    Not long after the exaltation of Nezahualpilli there occurred the

Conquest of Tlatelolco, and death memorable war of the Mexicans with their neighbors and rivals the of King Moquihuix.

Tlatelolcas. The king of Tlatelolco, Moquihuix, unable to bear the

glory of the Mexican, used every means to obscure it. He was mar‐

ried, as we have already said, to a sister of King Axayacatl, given to

him by Moctezuma as a reward for the famous victory won over the

people of Cotaxtla. On this unfortunate lady he often vented his rage

against his brother-in-law, and, not content with this, he secretly

sought to ally himself with other states that bore the yoke of the

Mexicans unwillingly. These were the people of Chalco, of Xilotepec,

of Toltitlan, of Tenayuca, of Mexicaltzinco, of Huitzilopochco, of

Xochimilco, of Cuitlahuac, and of Mixquic, who agreed to attack the

Mexicans in the rear after the Tlatelolcas had begun the battle. The

people of Cuauhpanco, moreover, the Huexotzincas, and the

Matlatzincas, whose help he had also implored, were to incorporate

their troops with those of the Tlatelolcas for the defense of the city.

The queen learned of these negotiations, and, now from the hatred

that she bore her husband, now from love of her brother and her

country, she warned Axayacatl of everything, that he might avoid so

serious a blow, which would have made his throne totter.

Moquihuix, assured of the help of the confederates, convoked the

nobles of his court to encourage them to the undertaking. An old and

authoritative priest, called Poyahuitl, raised his voice in the assembly,

and in the name of all offered to fight courageously against the ene‐

mies of the country: then

to [p. 249] to encourage them still further, he washed the altar of the sacrifices

Bk. IV. and gave that water, tinged with human blood, to the king and to all the captains to drink; with it, they said, they felt their courage grow, and I do not doubt that it had grown the better to practice cruelty. Meanwhile the queen, impatient of the ill treatment she suffered and frightened by the perils of the war, left her husband and betook herself to Mexico with four children, to put herself under the shelter of her brother. This she could do easily because of the very short distance between the two cities. Such a novelty so increased the mutual ill feeling of the Mexicans and the Tlatelolcas that wherever they met they insulted one another with words, came to blows, and killed one another.

As the time for making war was now drawing near, Moquihuix, together with his captains and many of the confederates, held a solemn sacrifice on the mountain least distant from the city, in order to obtain the protection of their gods; and there the day was fixed on which war was to be declared on the Mexicans. A few days later he gave notice to the confederates, so that they might be well prepared to come to his aid as soon as the attack began. Xiloman, lord of Colhuacan, wished first to assail the Mexicans and then, feigning flight, to provoke them to pursue him, so that the Tlatelolcas might attack them from behind. The day after these embassies Moquihuix performed the ceremony of arming his troops, and then betook himself to the temple of Huitzilopochtli to implore his help; there they returned to drink that abominable draught which Poyahuitl had given them at the first assembly, and all the soldiers passed one by one before the idol, making it a deep reverence. No sooner was this ceremony over than a company of bold Mexicans entered the market square, killing everyone they met; but the Tlatelolca troops, coming up at once, drove them out and took some prisoners, who were sacrificed without delay in a temple called Tlillan. That same day, at sunset, some Tlatelolca women had the boldness to advance into the streets of

History of Mexico, Vol. I.                  Ii                 Mexico [p. 250] Mexico and to burn brooms at the doors of the houses, shamelessly Bk. IV.

hurling insults at the Mexicans and threatening them with their swift

ruin; but the Mexicans treated them with the contempt they

deserved.

That same night the Tlatelolcas took up arms, and in the morning,

at first light, they began the attack on Mexico. They were in the

hottest of the fray when Xiloman arrived with his Colhuas; but seeing

that the king of Tlatelolco had begun to fight without waiting for

him, and without heeding his counsel, he withdrew in indignation,

and, wishing to do the Mexicans some harm, had certain canals

closed to prevent any relief that might reach them by water; but they

were soon reopened by order of Axayacatl. All that day they fought

with indescribable ardor on both sides, until night forced the

Tlatelolcas to withdraw. The Mexicans burned the houses of the city

that were nearest to Tlatelolco, perhaps because they hampered them

in the fighting; but in setting the fire twenty of them were taken pris‐

oner by the enemy and immediately sacrificed.

Axayacatl that night distributed his army in all the streets that led

to Tlatelolco, and at daybreak they began to march from every side

toward the market square, which was to be the point of their union.

The Tlatelolcas, seeing themselves attacked on every side, were fall‐

ing back toward that great square, to unite all their forces there and

be able to resist still better; but on arriving at that place they found

themselves more hampered by their own multitude. The shouts with

which King Moquihuix tried from the top of the great temple to en‐

courage his men were of no avail. The Tlatelolcas were wounded and

killed, and those who fell vented their rage against the king with in‐

sults: “Come down from there,” they said to him, “and take up arms,

you coward; for it is not the part of brave men to stand calmly watch‐

ing those who fight and lose their lives in defense of their homeland.”

But these laments, caused by the pain of their wounds and the an‐

guish of death, were

altogether [p. 251] altogether unjust: for Moquihuix did not fail in the duties of a general

Bk. IV. and of a king, since he ought not to expose his own life as much as the soldiers theirs, in order to be able to be more useful to them by counsel and by voice. Meanwhile the Mexicans advanced as far as the stairway of the temple and, climbing it, reached the upper court, from which Moquihuix encouraged his people and defended himself like a desperate man; but a Mexican captain named Quetzalhua threw him down the stairs with a shove, (l) and some soldiers, taking the corpse on their arms, presented it to Axayacatl, who, opening its chest, tore out the heart. A horrible act, but performed without horror, being too common in their sacrifices.

Thus ended the brave Moquihuix, and with him the little monarchy of the Tlatelolcas, governed by four kings in the space of about a hundred and eighteen years. The Tlatelolcas, seeing their king dead, at once fell into disorder and tried to save their lives by flight, passing through the midst of their enemies; but four hundred and sixty were left dead in that square, and among them some officers of note. After this conquest the city of Tlatelolco was perfectly united to that of Mexico, and was no longer considered a distinct city, but as a part, or rather a suburb, of that court, as it is at present. The king of Mexico always kept a governor there, and the Tlatelolcas, besides the tribute of maize, clothing, arms, and armor that they paid annually to the crown, were obliged to rebuild the temple of Huitznahuac whenever it should be needed.

We do not know whether the people of Cuauhpanco, the Huexotzincas, and the Matlatzincas, who had confederated with the Tlatelolcas, were in fact present in this war. As for the other allies,

Ii2                          the

(l) The Interpreter of the Mendoza Collection says that Moquihuix, having lost the battle, fled to the top of the temple and then threw himself down, because he could not bear the insults of a priest; but the account of the other historians seems to us more in keeping with the character of that king. [p. 252] the historians say that, having arrived to the relief of the Tlatelolcas

Bk. IV. when Moquihuix was already dead and the war ended, they went back

without doing anything. As soon as Axayacatl saw himself victorious,

he condemned to the extreme penalty Poyahuitl and Ehecatzitzimitl,

both Tlatelolcas, for having been the ones who had most warmly

stirred up their fellow citizens against the Mexicans, and shortly af‐

terward he put to death the lords of Xochimilco, Cuitlahuac,

Colhuacan, Huitzilopochco, and others, for having confederated with

his enemies.

§ 17.     To avenge himself next on the Matlatzincas, a numerous and powNew conquests, and erful nation settled in the Valley of Toluca and not yet subject to the death of Axayacatl.

Mexicans, he declared war on them, and setting out from Mexico to‐

gether with the two allied kings, he took in passing the places of

Atlapolco and Xalatlauhco, and then in the same valley conquered

Toluca, Tetenanco, Metepec, Tzinacantepec, Calimaya, and other

places in the southern part of the valley, the nation remaining from

then on tributary to the crown of Mexico. After some time he re‐

turned to the same province to conquer also the northern part of the

valley, today called the Valley of Ixtlahuaca, and chiefly Xiquipilco, a

city and considerable state of the Otomis, whose lord, named

Tlilcuezpalin, was famous for his bravery. Axayacatl, who still boasted

of his courage, wished to fight a duel with him in the battle that he

offered to the people of Xiquipilco; but the outcome was disastrous

to Axayacatl himself; for he received a severe wound in the thigh, and

two Otomi captains, coming upon him, threw him to the ground with

several blows and would have taken him prisoner, had not certain

young Mexicans, seeing their king in such grave danger, courageously

saved his liberty and his life. In spite of such a misfortune, the

Mexicans gained a complete victory, and took, according to their his‐

torians, eleven thousand and sixty prisoners, among them

Tlilcuezpalin himself and those two captains who had assailed the

king. By this glorious victory Axayacatl added to the crown

Xiquipilco, Xocotitlan, Atlacomulco, and all the other places of that

pleasant valley that he still lacked.

As

[p. 253]

As soon as Axayacatl recovered from the wound, which, however, crippled

Bk. IV. one of his legs for the rest of his life, he gave a great banquet to the allied kings and to the Mexican grandees, at which he put to death Tlilcuezpalin and the two aforesaid Otomi captains. The carrying out of a punishment amid the delights of a banquet did not seem inopportune to those men; for, accustomed to shed human blood, they had changed their natural horror into recreation. So great is the force of custom, and so easy is it for our minds to make the most horrible objects familiar to themselves!

In the last years of his reign, finding the limits of the empire too narrow on the west side, he went out again on campaign through the Valley of Toluca and, passing beyond the mountains, conquered Tochpan and Tlaximaloyan, this place being from then on the frontier of the kingdom of Michoacán. Then, turning toward the east, he took possession of Ocuillan and Malacatepec. Death, which came upon him in the thirteenth year of his reign, which was 1477 of the common era, interrupted the course of his victories. He was very warlike, and severe in punishing those who transgressed the laws published by his predecessor. He left by several wives a great number of sons, among whom was the famous Moctezuma II, of whom we shall shortly speak.

In place of Axayacatl, Tizoc, his elder brother, who had exercised the office § 18.

Tizoc, seventh king of general of the army, was elected. (m) We do not know where his first expe‐ of Mexico. dition was, undertaken to procure the victims necessary for his coronation. His reign was short and obscure. Nevertheless, in the tenth painting of the Mendoza Collection fourteen cities are represented as subdued by him, among them Toluca and Tecaxic, which, having rebelled against the crown, had to be reconquered

Chillan

(m) Father Acosta makes Tizoc the son of Moctezuma I, and the Interpreter of the Mendoza Collection makes him the son of Axayacatl; but both are mistakes, as the other historians have shown. Father Acosta also erred in the order of the kings, since he makes Tizoc reign before Axayacatl. On this see our Dissertations.

[p. 254]

Chillan, and Yancuitlan in the country of the Mixtecs, Mazatlan,

Bk. IV.

Tlapan, and Tamapachco. Torquemada makes mention of a victory

won by him over Tlacotepec.

§ 19.

In the time of this king there occurred the war between the War between the Texcocans and the Huexotzincas. That war had its origin in the ambiTexcocans and the

Huexotzincas. tion of the princes, brothers of King Nezahualpilli, who, though they

showed themselves content at the beginning with the exaltation of

their younger brother, the memory of their dead father having after‐

ward cooled, and no longer able to bear seeing themselves subject to

one whom they believed they ought to command by right of age,

plotted a secret conspiracy against him. For the execution of their

perverse designs they first invited the Chalcas, who were the readiest

for such misdeeds; but having failed in all the means they had em‐

ployed, they solicited for the same end the Huexotzincas.

Nezahualpilli, informed of such a conspiracy, equipped without delay

a good army and marched to go against the Huexotzincas. The gener‐

al of that state had ascertained all the marks of King Nezahualpilli, in

order to direct all his blows against him, and had even promised re‐

wards to anyone who should deliver him over to him alive or dead.

There was someone who made it known to the king; whereupon he,

before entering the battle, exchanged his garments and insignia for

those of one of his captains. This unfortunate officer, having been

taken for the king himself, was at once overwhelmed by the multi‐

tude and killed. While they were venting their rage upon him,

Nezahualpilli fell upon the Huexotzinca general and killed him, not

without grave risk of being killed himself by the soldiers who came to

the aid of their general. The Texcocans, who had made the same mis‐

take as the Huexotzincas, not being aware of the change of garments,

had begun to lose heart; but now, recognizing him, they rushed to his

defense, and after having routed his enemies, they sacked the city of

Huexotzinco and, laden with spoils, returned to Texcoco. The histori‐

ans say nothing of the end that the princes, authors of the conspiracy,

met with. It may be believed,

that [p. 255] that they were killed in the battle, or else saved themselves by flight from the punishment they deserved. Nezahualpilli, who a little earlier Bk. IV. had built himself a new palace, also had a wall constructed, to leave an eternal monument of his victory, that enclosed as much land as that which the Huexotzincas had occupied when they came to the defense of their general, and gave that place the same name as the day on which the victory was won. Thus did those seek to make their name and the glory of their deeds immortal who are believed by many to care nothing for the future.

§ 20.

Already at that time the king of Texcoco had several wives of very Marriage of King

Nezahualpilli, with noble houses; but none had been declared queen by him, since he was two Mexican ladies. reserving that honor for one whom he meant to take from the royal family of Mexico. He asked her of King Tizoc, and the latter gave him a niece of his, daughter of Tzotzocatzin. This marriage was celebrated in Texcoco with a great concourse of the nobility of the two courts. This lady had a sister endowed with singular beauty, who was called Xocotzin. They loved each other so much that, being unable to part, the new queen obtained from her father permission to take her sister with her to Texcoco. By frequent sight of her, and by conversing often with her, the king became so enamored of his fair sister-in-law that he resolved to marry her and to raise her also to the dignity of queen. This second marriage was, according to what the historians say, the most solemn and the most magnificent ever seen in that country. Not long afterward the king had by the first queen a son named Cacamatzin, who was his successor in the crown and, taken prisoner afterward by the Spaniards, died wretchedly. By the other he had Huexotzincatzin, (*) of whom we shall speak shortly, Coanacochtzin, who was also king of Acolhuacan and some time after the conquest by the Spaniards was hanged by the Conqueror Cortés, and Ixtlilxochitl, who allied himself with the Spaniards

against

(*) The name of Huexotzincatl was given to that prince simply on account of the victory over the Huexotzincas. [p. 256] against the Mexicans, and, converted to Christianity, took in baptism

Bk. IV.

the name and surname of that Conqueror.

While Nezahualpilli was seeking to multiply his descendants, en‐

joying great peace and tranquillity in his kingdom, some of his feuda‐

§ 21. tories were plotting the death of the king of Mexico. Techotlalla, lord Tragic death of King

Tizoc. of Iztapalapan, either resentful over some displeasure received or im‐

patient of Tizoc’s domination, conceived the wicked design of mak‐

ing an attempt on his life, and would reveal it to no one except to

such as seemed to him capable of carrying it out. He and Maxtlaton,

lord of Tlachco, agreed on the manner of executing so dangerous a

crime. The historians do not agree on this point. Some say that they

made use of certain witches, and that these by sorcery took his life;

but this seems to me a popular fable. Others affirm that they found a

way of giving him poison. However that may be, it is certain that

their plot succeeded. Tizoc was killed in the fifth year of his reign, in

1482 of the common era. He was a circumspect man, grave and se‐

vere, like his predecessors and successors, in the punishment of of‐

fenders. Since in his time the power and opulence of that crown were

already so great, he undertook to build for the tutelary god of the na‐

tion a temple that in size and magnificence should surpass all the

temples of that country, and to this end he had prepared infinite ma‐

terials and had even begun the building, when death came to upset

his designs.

The Mexicans, well aware that the death of their king had not

been natural, wished to avenge it before proceeding to a new election.

Their inquiries were so thorough that in a short time they discovered

the authors of the attempt and put them to death in the great square

§ 22. of Mexico in the presence of the two allied kings and of the Mexican

Ahuitzotl, eighth and Texcocan nobility. The electors then having assembled to create a

king of Mexico.

new king, they chose Ahuitzotl, brother of the two preceding kings,

who was already general of the army; for from the time of King

Chimalpopoca

the [p. 257] the custom had been introduced of not raising to the throne anyone who had not first exercised that office, it being thought very fitting Bk. IV. that he who was to become head of so warlike a nation should give proof of his bravery, and that in commanding the troops he should learn the manner of governing the kingdom.

The first care of the new king, after he was crowned, was the §Dedication

23.

of the building of the magnificent temple that his predecessor had designed great temple of

Mexico. and begun. It was resumed with the greatest activity, an incredible number of workmen taking part in it, and it was finished in four years. While work was going on at this undertaking, the king often went out to war, and all the enemies who were taken prisoner were reserved for the festival of the Dedication. The wars of these four years were against the Mazahuas, a few miles to the west, who had rebelled against the crown of Tacuba, against the Zapotecs, three hundred miles to the southeast, and against several other peoples. When the building was finished, the king invited to the festival of the Dedication the two allied kings and all the nobility of both kingdoms. The concourse was the largest ever seen in Mexico (n); for people came to be present at so celebrated a ceremony even from the most distant places. The festival lasted four days, during which all the prisoners taken in the four preceding years were sacrificed on the upper court of the temple. The historians do not agree concerning the number of the victims. Torquemada says that there were seventy-two thousand three hundred and forty-four. Others affirm that there were sixty-four thousand and sixty. To carry out such horrible sacrifices with greater display, they arranged the victims in two rows, each about a mile and a half long, which began in the streets of

History of Mexico, Vol. I.                        Kk                     Tacuba

(n) Some authors affirm that the number of persons who were present at this festival reached six million. This number may have been exaggerated, but it does not seem to me at all improbable, considering the great population of that country, the grandeur and novelty of the festival, and the readiness of those people in going from one place to another, being accustomed to travel on foot without the encumbrance of baggage.

[p. 258]

Tacuba and Iztapalapan and came to end at the same temple, (o) and

Bk. IV.

as soon as they arrived there they were sacrificed. When the festival

was over, the king made presents to all the invited guests, in which he

must have incurred a surprising expense. This happened in 1486.

In the same year Mozauhqui, lord of Xalatlauhco, in imitation of

his king, to whom he was much attached, dedicated another temple

too, built a little earlier, and likewise sacrificed a great number of

prisoners. So great was the slaughter made by the cruel and barbarous

superstition of those peoples!

The year 1487 was not memorable except for a great earthquake

and for the death of Chimalpopoca, king of Tacuba, who was suc‐

ceeded by Totoquihuatzin II.

§ 24.    Ahuitzotl, whose warlike genius did not allow him to enjoy peace, Conquests of King went out again to war against the people of Cozcacuauhtenanco, and

Ahuitzotl.

gained a complete victory; but because they had made a great resis‐

tance, he was too severe and cruel with them. Then he subdued the

people of Cuapilollan, and next passed on to make war against

Quetzalcuitlapillan, a great province populated by a warlike people,

(p) and finally against Cuauhtla, a place situated on the coast of the

Gulf of Mexico, in which war Moctezuma, son of Axayacatl and suc‐

cessor of Ahuitzotl in the kingdom, distinguished himself. Not long

afterward the Mexicans together with the Texcocans marched against

the Huexotzincas, in which war there distinguished themselves by

their courage Tezcatzin, brother of the aforesaid Moctezuma, and

Tliltototl, a noble Mexican officer, who later became general of the

army. We do not find

in

(o) Vetancurt says that the line of prisoners drawn up along the road to

Iztapalapan began at the spot that is today called La Candelaria Malcuitlapilco, and

that for such a reason it had this name, since Malcuitlapilco signifies the tail, or point,

or extremity of the prisoners. This conjecture is quite probable, nor is it easy to find

another origin for that name.

(p) Torquemada says that Ahuitzotl, having often undertaken the conquest of

Quetzalcuitlapillan, could never achieve it; but among the conquests of this king,

represented in painting XI of the Mendoza Collection, that province is also found. [p. 259] in the historians either the cause or the circumstances of such wars. When the expedition against Huexotzinco was over, Ahuitzotl cele‐ Bk. IV. brated the dedication of a new temple, called Tlacatecco, in which the prisoners taken in the earlier wars were sacrificed; but the joy of that festival was disturbed by the burning of the temple of Tlillan.

Thus this king passed his reign in continual wars until 1496, in which year the war against Atlixco took place. The entry of the Mexican army into this valley was so sudden that the first notice the people of Atlixco had of it was that which they received through their eyes on seeing them enter. They took up arms at once for their defense; but not finding themselves with forces sufficient to resist for long, they asked help of the Huexotzincas, their neighbors. When the ambassadors of Atlixco arrived at Huexotzinco, a famous captain named Toltecatl, whose great courage was in no way inferior to the extraordinary strength of his arm, was playing ball. As soon as he learned the news of the Mexican army, he left the game to go to Atlixco with the auxiliary troops, and, entering the battle unarmed, to display his bravery and to show the contempt he felt for his enemies, he struck down with his fists the first Mexican who presented himself, and took his arms, with which he made great slaughter. The Mexicans, unable to overcome the resistance of their enemies, abandoned the field and returned to Mexico covered with ignominy. The Huexotzincas, to reward the singular prowess of Toltecatl, made him head of their republic. This had been subjected to the dominion of the Mexicans, whose arms they had provoked with their insults; but since the conquered do not bear the yoke of the conqueror except when they cannot shake it off, every time the Huexotzincas found themselves with forces sufficient to resist, they rebelled, and the same happened with the greater part of the provinces subjected by force of arms to the crown of Mexico; whence it was necessary that the Mexican army should be in continual motion to reconquer what the king was losing. Toltecatl accepted the office conferred on him; but

Kk2                scarcely [p. 260] scarcely had a year passed when he was obliged to give up his office

Bk. IV. and his country. The priests and other ministers of the temples, abus‐

ing their authority, entered the houses of private persons and carried

off the maize and the turkeys that were there, and committed other

excesses unbecoming to their dignity. Toltecatl wished to put a stop

to it; but the priests took up arms. The people partly sided with

them, and partly opposed their violence, and between these two fac‐

tions a war broke out, which, like all other civil wars, caused very

grave evils. Toltecatl, weary of ruling so unruly a people, or fearing to

perish in the tempest, withdrew from the city with other nobles and,

crossing the mountains, betook himself to Tlalmanalco. The governor

of this city gave prompt notice of it to the king of Mexico, and he at

once put to death all those fugitives in punishment of their rebellion,

and had their bodies carried to Huexotzinco to frighten the rebels.

§ 25.     In 1498 it seemed to the king of Mexico that for lack of water the New inundation of

Mexico.  navigation  of the lake had become difficult, and he wished to increase

that water with the other from the spring of Huitzilopochco, which

the people of Coyoacán used, and he summoned Tzotzomatzin, lord

of Coyoacán, to give him his orders. Tzotzomatzin represented to

him that that spring was not perennial: that at times the water failed,

and at times came in such abundance that it might cause some dam‐

age to the court. Ahuitzotl, thinking that Tzotzomatzin’s reasons

were mere pretexts to excuse himself from doing what had been com‐

manded him, insisted on his first order; and seeing that man persist in

the difficulty he had put forward, he dismissed him in anger, and af‐

terward had him put to death. This is usually the reward of good

counsels, when princes obstinate in some caprice will not listen to the

sincere remonstrances of their faithful subjects. Ahuitzotl, unwilling

by any means to abandon his project, had a great and ample aqueduct

(*) made from Coyoacán to Mexico, and through it was conducted

the

(*) This aqueduct was entirely demolished either by Ahuitzotl himself, or by [p. 261] the water with many superstitious ceremonies; for some priests went

Bk. IV. about incensing it, others sacrificed quails and anointed with their blood the lip of the aqueduct, others played musical instruments, and all celebrated the arrival of the water. The high priest wore the same dress in which they represented Chalchiuhtlicue, goddess of water. (q)

With such solemnity the water arrived at Mexico; but it was not long before the general joy was changed into weeping; for the rains of that year having been extraordinarily abundant, the water of the lake rose so much that it flooded the city, all the streets were full of boats, and some houses collapsed. One day, the king being in a lower room of his palace, the water entered it so suddenly and in such quantity that, hastening in his fear to go out by the door, which was low, he gave himself such a violent blow on the head that after some time it caused his death. Afflicted by the evils of the inundation and by the clamors of the people, he called to his aid the king of Acolhuacan, who without delay had the dike repaired that, on the counsel of his father Nezahualcoyotl, had been built in the reign of Moctezuma.

Scarcely were the Mexicans freed from the evil of the inundation when in the following year they had to suffer that of the scarcity of grain, the maize having been spoiled by the excessive abundance of water; but in this same year they had the good fortune to discover in the valley of Mexico a quarry of tetzontli, which has been so useful for the buildings of that great city. The king at once began to use this kind of stone in the temples, and in imitation of him private persons in their houses. Besides this the king had all

the

his successor; for nothing was left of it when the Spaniards arrived in that country.

(q) Father Acosta testifies that the conveying of the water of Huitzilopochco to Mexico, and the ceremonies performed by the priests, were represented in a Mexican painting that was in the Vatican library in his time (and perhaps is there still). [p. 262] the ruinous buildings pulled down and rebuilt in better form, notably

Bk. IV.

increasing the beauty and magnificence of his court.

He spent the last two years of his life in frequent wars, such as

§ 26. those      of Izquixochitlan, Amatlan, Tlacuilollan, Xaltepec, New conquests, and

death of King  Tehuantepec,     and Huexotla in the Huaxteca. Tliltototl, a Mexican

Ahuitzotl. general, the war of Izquixochitlan being over, carried his victorious

arms as far as Cuauhtemallan, or Guatemala, more than nine hundred

miles southeast of the court, in which expedition he performed, ac‐

cording to what the historians say, prodigies of courage; but no one

relates the particular deeds of so renowned a general, nor do we know

whether all that great tract of land remained subject to the crown of

Mexico.

Finally, in the year 1502, after about twenty years of reign,

Ahuitzotl died of an illness caused by the blow to the head already

mentioned. He was a very warlike man, and one of the kings who

most enlarged the dominions of the crown. When he died, the

Mexicans possessed almost all that they had at the arrival of the

Spaniards. Besides courage he had two other royal virtues that made

him famous among his countrymen, namely magnificence and liberal‐

ity. He so embellished Mexico with new and magnificent buildings

that it had already become the finest city of the New World. When

he received the tributes of the provinces, he would assemble the peo‐

ple in a certain place in the city and distribute provisions and cloth‐

ing personally to the needy. He rewarded his captains and soldiers

who distinguished themselves in war, and the ministers and officials

of the crown who served him faithfully, with gold, silver, gems, and

beautiful feathers. These virtues were obscured by some vices; for he

was capricious, vindictive, and sometimes cruel, and so given to war

that he seemed to hate peace; whence the name of Ahuitzotl is used

proverbially even among the Spaniards of that kingdom to signify a

man who with his molestations and vexations does not let another

live. (r) But he was, on the other hand, of

good

(r) The Spaniards say, N. is my Ahuizote; This is N.’s Ahuizote; no one lacks his Ahuizote,

etc. [p. 263] good humor, and so delighted in music that neither by day nor by

Bk. IV. night was this entertainment lacking in the palace; which must have been prejudicial to the public good, since it robbed him of a great part of the time and care that he ought to have given to the affairs of the kingdom. Nor did women occupy his mind any less. His predecessors had had many wives, thinking that their authority and greatness appeared the greater the greater the number of persons devoted to their pleasures. Ahuitzotl, having so greatly enlarged the dominions and increased the power of the crown, wished to show the superiority of his greatness over that of his predecessors in the excessive number of his wives. Such was the state of the court of Mexico at the beginning of the sixteenth century, of that century so fertile in great events, in which that kingdom was to change its face and the whole New World was to be turned upside down.

[p. 264]

B O O K V.

Events of the reign of Moctezuma II, ninth king of Mexico, up to the year

1519. Account of his life, of his government, and of the magnificence of his

palaces, gardens, and woods. War of Tlaxcala, and the fortunes of Tlahuicole, a

Tlaxcalan captain. Death and eulogy of Nezahualpilli, king of Acolhuacan,

and new revolutions in that kingdom. Presages of the arrival and of the con‐

quest of the Spaniards.

hen Ahuitzotl was dead, and his funeral rites had been

W             celebrated with extraordinary magnificence, they pro‐

ceeded to the election of the new sovereign. No brother

of the preceding kings was still living; so that, according to the law of

the kingdom, one of his nephews, sons of his predecessors, had to

succeed the dead king. These were many; for of the sons of Axayacatl

there were living Moctezuma, (a) Cuitláhuac, Matlatzincatl,

§ 1. Pinahuitzin, Cecepacticatzin, and of those of King Tizoc, Moctezuma II, ninth Imactlacuiyatzin, Tepehuatzin, and others, whose names we do not

king of Mexico.

know. Moctezuma was elected from among them all, and, to distin‐

guish him from the other king of the same name, he was given the

surname of Xocoyotzin.

(*)

(a) The author of the annotations on the letters of the Conqueror Cortés, printed

in Mexico in the year 1770, says that Moctezuma II was the son of Moctezuma I.

This is a gross mistake; for we know from all the historians, Spanish as well as

Mexican, that he was the son of Axayacatl. See Torquemada, Bernal Díaz, the

Interpreter of the Mendoza Collection, etc.

[p. 265]

(*) Besides the bravery that he had shown in several battles, in the exercise of the office of general, he was also a priest, and for his gravity Bk. V. and circumspection, and for his religion, he was much revered. He was a taciturn man and very considerate no less in his actions than in his words, and every time he spoke in the royal council, of which he was a member, he made himself heard with respect. Notice of the election was given to the allied kings, and they betook themselves at once to that court to pay their compliments. Moctezuma, aware of this, withdrew to the temple, as if to protest himself unworthy of so great an honor. The nobility went there to inform him of his election, and found him, according to what the historians say, sweeping the pavement of the temple. He was conducted with a great retinue to the palace, where the electors solemnly announced to him the election that had been made of his person to occupy the throne of Mexico. He then returned to the temple to perform the customary ceremonies; and when they were over, he received on the throne the obedience of the nobility and listened to the congratulatory speeches of the orators. The first was that of Nezahualpilli, king of Acolhuacan, which we present here to the readers just as the Mexicans have preserved it for us.

“The great good fortune of the Mexican monarchy,” he said, “is made manifest in the concord of your election, and in the singular applause with which it is celebrated by all. They have indeed reason to celebrate it; for the kingdom of Mexico has attained such an extent that to bear so great a weight neither a lesser fortitude than that of your invincible heart would suffice, nor a lesser wisdom than that which we admire in you. I see clearly how great is the love that Almighty God bears this nation; for He has enlightened it, so that it might know how to choose what

History of Mexico, Vol. I.                      Ll                        was

(*) The first Moctezuma was called by the Mexicans Huehue Moteuczoma, and the second Moteuczoma Xocoyotzin, names equivalent to the Senior and the Junior of the Latins. [p. 266] was more to its advantage. Who will be able to persuade himself that Bk. V. he who as a private man had already explored the folds of the heav‐

ens, (b) will now as king not know the things of the earth, for the

happiness of his subjects? Will he who has made the greatness of his

spirit shine on so many occasions not have it now, when he needs it

more than ever? Who can believe that where there is so much

courage and so much wisdom, relief will be lacking to the widow and

to the orphan? Without doubt the Mexican empire has reached the

summit of authority; for the Creator of Heaven has communicated so

much of it to you that you inspire respect in all who look upon you.

Rejoice, then, O blessed land, that a prince has fallen to your lot who

will be your support, and by his mercy will be a father and a brother

to his subjects. You have in fact a king who will not take occasion of

his superiority to give himself to softness and to lie stretched on his

bed, abandoned to pastimes and delights; rather, in the sweetest re‐

pose the care he will have of you will disquiet his heart and rouse

him, nor will he find relish in the most delicate food, for his anxiety

for your welfare. And you, most noble prince and powerful lord, take

courage, and trust that the Creator of Heaven, who has raised you to

so eminent a dignity, will give you strength to fulfill the obligations

attached to it. He who has been so liberal toward you until now will

not deny you his precious gifts, having himself raised you to the

throne, in which I wish you many and very happy years.”

Moctezuma listened attentively to this speech, and was so moved

that, though three times he wished to reply, he could not, being pre‐

vented by the tears produced by a sweet pleasure that had the appear‐

ance of humility; but at last, having somewhat restrained his weeping,

he replied in a few words, protesting himself unworthy of the honor

to which he was raised, and thanking that

king

(b) This saying of King Nezahualpilli seems to signify that Moctezuma was en‐

gaged in the study of astronomy. [p. 267] king for the praises with which he favored him; and having listened to the other speeches, he remained in the temple to keep the fast of Bk. V. four days, and was then conducted with great pomp back to the royal palace.

He thought next of making war to procure the victims that were to be sacrificed at his coronation. This misfortune fell upon the people of Atlixco, who a little before had rebelled against the crown. The king therefore went out from the court with the flower of the nobility, among whom went also his brothers and cousins. In this war the Mexicans lost some brave officers; but nevertheless they put the rebels back under their former yoke, and Moctezuma returned victorious, bringing with him the unfortunate prisoners he needed for his coronation. This ceremony was celebrated with such a display of games, dances, theatrical performances, and illuminations, and with such abundance and richness of tributes sent from the provinces of the kingdom, that even foreigners never before seen in Mexico came to see it, and the very enemies of the Mexicans, such as the Tlaxcalans and the Michoacans, disguised themselves in order to be present as spectators; but Moctezuma, having learned of it, with royal generosity had them lodged and entertained, and also had some terraces fitted up from which they could observe that great ceremony at their ease.

The first thing he did was to reward with the state of Tlachauhco the great services rendered to his predecessors in several wars by a renowned captain named Tlilxochitl. A truly happy beginning of his reign, if the progress had matched it. But scarcely had he begun to § 2.

Conduct and ceremouse his authority when he made manifest the pride that until then he nial of King had kept hidden under a fair appearance of humility. All his predeces‐ Moctezuma. sors had been accustomed to confer the offices on the most deserving, and on those who seemed to them the fittest to exercise them, honoring with them indifferently nobles and commoners, notwithstanding the solemn agreement made between the nobility and the commoners under the reign of Itzcoatl. Moctezuma, as soon as he took the reins of government, showed himself

Ll2                      of [p. 268] of a different mind, and disapproved the conduct of his predecessors, Bk. V. on the pretext that the commoners served according to their station,

and that in all their actions they displayed the baseness of their birth

and of their upbringing. Prompted by this maxim, he stripped the

commoners of all the offices they held, both in his royal household

and at court, declaring them incapable of obtaining any in the future.

A prudent old man who had been his tutor represented to him that

such a resolution might estrange the minds of the common people

from his person; but nothing sufficed to turn him from the course he

had resolved upon.

The whole service of his royal palace was performed by persons of

rank. Besides those who always lived there, who were very many, six

hundred feudatory lords and nobles entered every morning to pay

him court. These spent the whole day in the antechambers, where

servants were not allowed to enter, talking in low voices and awaiting

the orders of their sovereign. The servants who accompanied these

lords were so many that they filled the three courtyards of the palace,

and many remained in the street. No smaller was the number of the

women who lived there, ladies, maidservants, and slaves. All this nu‐

merous flock lived shut up within a kind of seraglio, under the care of

some noble matrons, who watched over their conduct; for those

kings were exceedingly jealous, and punished with rigor any disorder

in the palace, however slight. From these women the king took for

himself those who pleased him, (c) and the others served to reward

the services of his vassals. All the feudatories of the crown had to re‐

side some months of the year at court, and on returning to their

states they left there their sons or their brothers, as hostages required

by the king to assure himself of their fidelity; hence they had to keep

a house in Mexico.

Another

(c) Some historians affirm that Moctezuma had a hundred and fifty of his wives

pregnant at the same time; but this is utterly incredible.

[p. 269]

Another effect of Moctezuma’s despotism was the ceremonial that he introduced at court. No one could enter the palace, whether to Bk. V. serve the king or to transact some business with him, without first taking off his shoes at the door. No one was permitted to appear before the king in splendid dress, because it was thought a want of respect toward Majesty; so that the greatest lords (except the king’s close kinsmen) stripped off the rich garment they wore, or at least covered it with another, plain one, to show their humility. All, on entering the audience hall, and before speaking to the king, made three bows, saying at the first, Lord, at the second, My Lord, and at the third, Great Lord. (d) They spoke low and with bowed heads, and received the answer that the king gave them through his secretaries as attentively and as humbly as if it had been an oracle. In taking leave, no one turned his back to the throne.

The same audience hall served him for his dinner: the table was a large cushion, and the chair a low stool. The tablecloths, napkins, and towels were of cotton, but very fine, white, and spotless. The dishes were of the fine majolica of Cholula; but none of these things served him more than once, for he immediately gave it to one of the nobles. The cups in which chocolate and the other drinks of cacao were prepared for him were of gold, or of handsome sea shell, or certain natural vessels curiously varnished, of which we shall speak elsewhere. He also had gold plates, but he did not use them except in the temple at certain festivals. The dishes were so many and so varied that the Spaniards who saw them were amazed. The conqueror Cortés says that they covered the floor of a great hall, and that there were presented to Moctezuma plates of every kind of game, fish, fruit, and herbs of that country. Three or four hundred young nobles in good order carried this dinner;

they

(d) The Mexican words are Tlatoani, Lord; Notlatocatzin, my Lord; and Hueitlatoani, Great Lord. [p. 270] they presented it before the king sat down to table, and at once withBk. V. drew; and so that it should not grow cold, every dish was accompa‐

nied by its own chafing dish. The king indicated with a little rod that

he held in his hand the dishes he wanted, and all the rest he dis‐

tributed among the nobles who were in the antechambers. Before sit‐

ting down, four of his wives, the most beautiful of his seraglio, of‐

fered him water to wash his hands; they remained standing there the

whole time the dinner lasted, together with six of his principal minis‐

ters and the steward.

As soon as the king sat down to table, the steward closed the door

of the hall, so that none of the other nobles should see him eat. The

ministers kept at a distance and observed a deep silence, except when

they had to reply to what the king said to them. The steward and the

four women served him the dishes, besides two others who brought

him the maize bread kneaded with eggs. Often he listened to music

while dining, and amused himself with the jests of certain deformed

men whom he kept for grandeur. He showed great pleasure in hearing

them, and said that among their jests they were wont to tell him

some important truth. When the dinner was over he took tobacco

mixed with liquidambar in a pipe, or reed, handsomely varnished, and

with that smoke he brought on sleep.

After sleeping a little, leaning on the same stool, he gave audience,

in which he listened attentively to all that was proposed to him, en‐

couraged those who from agitation could not speak, and answered all

through his ministers or secretaries. After the audience there was mu‐

sic, for he took great delight in hearing sung the glorious deeds of his

ancestors. At other times he amused himself by watching various

games, of which we shall speak elsewhere. When he went out of the

house he was carried on the shoulders of the nobles in an open litter

under a rich canopy, with a numerous train of courtiers, and wherever

he passed all stopped with eyes closed, as if they feared to be dazzled

by the splendor

of [p. 271] of Majesty. When he dismounted from the litter to go on foot, carpets were spread so that he should not touch the ground with his Bk. V. feet.

To so great a majesty the grandeur and magnificence of his palaces, of his pleasure houses, of his woods and gardens corresponded. The §Magnificence

3.

of the palace of his ordinary residence was a vast building of stone and lime, palaces

houses.

and royal

which had twenty doors opening on the square and the streets, three great courtyards, and in one of them a handsome fountain, several halls, and more than a hundred rooms. Some of the chambers had their walls faced with marble and other precious stones. The beams were of cedar, cypress, and other excellent woods, well worked and carved. Among the halls there was one so large that, according to an eyewitness and a careful one (*), three thousand men could stand in it. Besides this palace he had others, within and outside the capital. In Mexico, besides the seraglio of his wives, there were dwellings for his councilors and ministers, and for all the officials of his household and of his court, and also for lodging foreign lords who happened to come there, and especially the two allied kings.

He had two houses in Mexico for animals: one for the birds other than birds of prey, and the other for the birds of prey, for the quadrupeds, and for the reptiles. In the first there were many rooms, and corridors supported on columns of marble, each of a single piece. These corridors looked upon a garden, where amid the foliage of a grove of trees ten ponds were laid out, some of fresh water for the aquatic river birds, and the others of brackish water for those of the sea. In the rest of the house there were all the other species of birds, which were so many and so different that the Spaniards who saw them were amazed, and could not persuade

themselves

(*) The Anonymous Conqueror, in his valuable account. He tells us further that, having entered that great palace four times, and having gone round it until he was worn out, he could not see all of it. [p. 272] themselves that any species was lacking of all that exist in the world. Bk. V.

To each was supplied the same food on which it fed in its time of lib‐

erty, now seeds, now fruits, now insects. For the birds alone that live

on fish, ten Castilian weights of fish were consumed every day (as the

conqueror Cortés bears witness in his letters to Charles V), that is,

more than three hundred Roman pounds. Three hundred men, ac‐

cording to the same Conqueror, were employed solely in the care of

these birds, besides their physicians, who observed their diseases and

applied the suitable remedies. Of those three hundred some procured

their food, others distributed it, others had charge of the eggs and of

their hatching, and others, finally, plucked the birds at a certain sea‐

son; for besides the pleasure that the king took in seeing so great a

multitude of animals gathered together, he took chief care of the

feathers, no less for the famous mosaic images, of which we shall

speak in their place, than for the other works that were made of

them. The halls and rooms of these houses were so many that, as the

aforesaid Conqueror testifies, two great princes could have lodged in

them with all their retinue. This celebrated house stood on the site

where the great convent of San Francisco is today.

The other house, intended for the wild beasts, had a large and

handsome courtyard paved in checkerboard pattern, and was divided

into many apartments. In one of them were all the birds of prey, from

the royal eagle down to the kestrel, and of every species many indi‐

viduals. These birds were distributed according to their species in

many underground chambers, which were more than seven feet deep

and more than seventeen long and wide. Half of each chamber was

covered with good slabs, and there were perches fixed to the wall, so

that they could sleep there and shelter from the rain. The other half

was covered only by a lattice with other perches, where they might

enjoy the sun. For the sustenance of these birds as many as five hun‐

dred turkeys were killed every day. In the same house there were

many

halls [p. 273] low halls with a great number of strong wooden cages, in which were shut up the lions, the tigers, the wolves, the coyotes, the wild cats, Bk. V. and all the other species of wild beasts, which were fed on deer, rabbits, hares, techichi, and other animals, and on the entrails of the men who were sacrificed.

Nor did the king of Mexico keep only those species of animals that other princes keep for grandeur, but even those which by their nature seem exempt from servitude, such as crocodiles and serpents. The serpents of several species were kept in certain casks or large vessels, the crocodiles in ponds enclosed by a wall. There were likewise very many ponds for fish, of which two handsome ones still survive, seen by us in the palace of Chapultepec, two miles from Mexico.

Not content with keeping in his palaces every sort of animal, Moctezuma had also gathered there all the abnormal men who, whether by the color of their hair or of their skin or by some other deformity of the limbs, had become singular in their kind. A truly profitable vanity, since it secured the maintenance of so many wretched persons and freed them from the inhuman insults of other men.

In all his palaces he had most beautiful gardens, in which there was every kind of precious flower, fragrant herb, and medicinal plant. He had also woods enclosed by walls and supplied with abundant game, where he was accustomed to amuse himself. One of these woods was on a little island of the lake, known at present to the Spaniards by the name of Peñón.

Of all the aforesaid palaces, gardens, and woods nothing remains but the wood of Chapultepec, which the Spanish viceroys have preserved for their recreation. All the others were wrecked by the conquerors. They demolished the most magnificent edifices of Mexican antiquity, now from an indiscreet zeal for religion, now from vengeance, now to make use of the materials. They abandoned the cultivation of the royal gardens, cut down the woods, and reduced that land to such a state that nowadays the magnificence of those kings could not be believed, if

History of Mexico, Vol. I.                Mm                        it [p. 274] it were not evident to us from the testimony of those very men who an‐

Bk. V.

nihilated it.

Both the palaces and all the aforesaid pleasure places were kept ex‐

tremely clean, even those where he never went; for there was nothing of

which he boasted more than cleanliness in his person and in all his

things. Every day he bathed, and therefore there were baths in all his

palaces. Every day he changed four garments, and the one that he had

§ 4. The good and the once laid aside he did not use again, but destined it to be given as a gift bad in Moctezuma. to the nobles who served him and to the soldiers who bore themselves

well in war. Every morning he employed, according to some historians,

more than a thousand men in sweeping and sprinkling the streets of the

city.

In one of the royal houses there was a great armory, where he had

every sort of arms, offensive and defensive, in use among those nations,

and insignia and military ornaments. In the making of these implements

he kept employed a surprising number of artisans; as also for other

works he had very many goldsmiths, mosaic workers, marble workers,

painters, and others. A whole street was devoted solely to dancers, in‐

tended for his amusement.

His zeal for religion was not inferior to his magnificence. He built

several temples to his gods, and offered them frequent sacrifices, observ‐

ing exactly the established rites and ceremonies. He took great care that

all the temples, and especially the greatest one of Mexico, should be well

served and exceedingly clean; but the vain fear of the auguries and of the

supposed oracles of those wicked deities utterly cowed his spirit.

He was extremely zealous for the observance of his orders and of the

laws of the kingdom, and was inexorable in punishing transgressors. He

often tested, through a third person and with gifts, the rectitude of his

magistrates, and if he ever found anyone guilty, he punished him without

remission, even though he belonged to the most conspicuous nobility.

He was an implacable enemy of idleness, and to banish it

as [p. 275] as far as possible from his dominions, he sought to keep his subjects

Bk. V. constantly occupied: the soldiers in continual exercises of war, and the others either in the cultivation of the fields, or in the construction of new buildings, or in other public works; and even the beggars, that they should not be altogether idle, he required to contribute a certain quantity of those filthy insects that are the nurslings of squalor and the companions of misery.

This oppression in which he held his vassals, the excessive burdens that he had laid upon them, his haughtiness and his pride, and his too great severity in punishing alienated their minds; but otherwise he won their love by liberality, both in providing for the needs of his peoples and in rewarding the services of his captains and ministers. Among the other things worthy of being celebrated with the highest praises, and of being imitated by all princes, he designated the city of Colhuacan as a hospital for all those invalids who, after having faithfully served the crown in military or political employments, needed, whether on account of their age or of their infirmities, to be cared for. There, at the expense of the royal treasury, their support and their cure were attended to. Such were the qualities, partly good and partly bad, of the celebrated Moctezuma, which it seemed to us fitting to set before the readers here, before relating the series of events that befell him.

At the beginning of his reign he put to death Malinalli, lord of Tlachquiauhco, for having rebelled against the crown of Mexico; he brought that state back under his obedience, and conquered that of Achiotlan. Soon afterward another war broke out, more serious and dangerous, in which he was not so fortunate.

Among so many provinces conquered by the Mexicans by force of § 5. arms, or voluntarily submitted to them for fear of their power, the re‐ War of Tlaxcala. public of Tlaxcala had always maintained itself unconquered, never bending its neck to the yoke, although it was so little distant from the court of the Mexican empire. The Huexotzincas, the Cholulans, and other neighboring states, which had formerly been allies of the republic, jealous then of their prosperity

Mm2                       had [p. 276] had embittered the Mexicans against them, on the pretext that the Bk. V.

Tlaxcalans wished to take possession of the maritime provinces of the

Gulf of Mexico, and that by their commerce with those provinces

they increased their power and wealth every day, and were stirring up

the minds of the peoples. The commerce of the Tlaxcalans, of which

the Huexotzincas complained, was quite justified and necessary; for

besides the fact that the people of those coasts were originally in

great part from Tlaxcala, and the one and the other considered them‐

selves kinsmen, the Tlaxcalans needed to provide themselves with the

cacao, the cotton, and the salt that they lacked. Nevertheless the rep‐

resentations of the Huexotzincas and of the other rivals of Tlaxcala

so moved the minds of the Mexicans that, beginning with

Moctezuma I, all the kings of Mexico treated the Tlaxcalans as the

greatest enemies of their empire, and always kept strong garrisons on

the frontiers of Tlaxcala, to prevent those people from trading with

the maritime provinces.

The Tlaxcalans, finding themselves deprived of freedom in trade,

and consequently of the acquisition of things necessary to life, re‐

solved to send an embassy to the Mexican nobility (probably in the

time of King Axayacatl), complaining of the wrong done them

through the sinister reports of their rivals. The Mexicans, insolent

from their prosperity, replied that the king of Mexico was universal

lord of the world, and that all mortals were his vassals, and as such

the Tlaxcalans ought to give him obedience and acknowledge him

with tribute, after the example of the other nations; but if they re‐

fused to submit, they would perish without fail, their cities would be

utterly ruined, and their country would be inhabited by other peo‐

ples. To so arrogant and so foolish a reply the ambassadors answered

with these courageous words: “Most powerful lords, Tlaxcala owes

you no homage; nor, since their ancestors came out of the northern

countries to dwell in this land, have the Tlaxcalans ever acknowl‐

edged any prince with tribute. They have always preserved

their [p. 277] their liberty, and, not being accustomed to the servitude to which you claim to reduce them, rather than surrender to your power they will Bk. V. shed more blood than their ancestors shed in the famous battle of Poyauhtlan.”

The Tlaxcalans, distressed by the arrogant and ambitious pretension of the Mexicans, and despairing of being able to induce them to a reasonable agreement, thought seriously of fortifying their frontiers still further to prevent any invasion. They had before surrounded the lands of the republic with great ditches, and had placed good garrisons on the frontiers; now, in view of the threats of the Mexicans, they increased their fortifications, augmenting the troops of the garrisons, and building that famous wall of six miles which barred the entry to enemies on the east side, from which quarter greater danger threatened them. Often they were attacked by the Huexotzincas, the Cholulans, the people of Itzocan, the people of Tecamachalco, and other states neighboring or little distant from Tlaxcala; but they could never take from the republic a palm of land, thanks to the supreme vigilance of the Tlaxcalans and the courage with which they opposed the invaders.

Very many vassals of the crown of Mexico had taken refuge in the land of Tlaxcala, especially Chalcas and Otomis of Xaltocan, who had saved themselves from the ruin of their homelands in the wars already mentioned. These bore a deadly hatred to the Mexicans on account of the evils they had received from them, and therefore seemed to the Tlaxcalans the men best fitted to oppose vigorously the attempts of their enemies; nor were they mistaken; for in fact the Mexicans found greater resistance in no others than in those exiles, especially in the Otomis, of whom the garrison of the frontiers was composed, who served the republic faithfully, and were rewarded by it with distinguished posts.

During all the time that Axayacatl and his successors reigned, the Tlaxcalans remained deprived of commerce with the maritime provinces; hence salt failed the people to such a degree that they became accustomed to eating their food without such a seasoning

and [p. 278] and did not return to the use of it until many years after the conquest Bk. V. by the Spaniards; but the nobles (at least some of them), as they had

secret correspondence with certain Mexican lords, provided them‐

selves with everything necessary, without the commoners of either

side knowing it. No one is unaware that in general calamities it is the

poor who bear the whole weight of the tribulation, while the well-to-

do find in their wealth the means of escaping it, or at least of soften‐

ing it.

Now Moctezuma, unable to endure that the little republic of

Tlaxcala should refuse to render him the obedience and the adoration

that so many peoples paid him, even those most distant from the

court, ordered, at the beginning of his reign, that the states neighbor‐

ing Tlaxcala should make ready their troops and attack that republic

on every side. The Huexotzincas, confederated with the Cholulans,

soon raised troops under the command of Tecayahuatzin, head of the

state of Huexotzinco, but trusting more to their cunning than to

their forces, they tried first, with gifts and promises, to draw to their

side the people of Hueyotlipan, a city of the republic situated on the

frontier of the kingdom of Acolhuacan, and the Otomis, who guarded

the other frontiers; but neither the one nor the other would bind

themselves; indeed they declared that they were prepared to die in

defense of their republic. Whereupon the Huexotzincas, being com‐

pelled to resort to force, entered the lands of Tlaxcala with such fury

that, the garrison of the frontier not sufficing to hold them back, they

advanced, making a great slaughter, as far as Xiloxochitla, a place only

three miles distant from the capital. There Tizatlacatzin, a celebrated

Tlaxcalan captain, put up a great resistance against them; but in the

end he died, overcome by the multitude of the enemy; who, finding

themselves so near the capital, were so afraid of the vengeance of the

Tlaxcalans that they returned from there precipitately to their own

lands. This was the beginning of the continual battles and hostilities

that there were between those two states until the arrival of the

Spaniards. We do not know from history whether on this occasion

there also engaged

in [p. 279] in the war the other states neighboring Tlaxcala; it may be that the Huexotzincas and the Cholulans did not permit the others to have a Bk. V. share in their glory.

The Tlaxcalans remained so enraged against the Huexotzincas that, no longer willing to confine themselves within their own state to defend it, as they had done before, they often went out to attack their enemies. Once they assaulted them along the foot of the mountains that lie to the west of Huexotzinco (*), and pressed them in such a way that, the Huexotzincas not being able to resist, they asked help of Moctezuma, who immediately sent an army under the command of his eldest son. This army marched along the southern slope of the volcano Popocatepetl, where it was swelled by the troops of Chietlan and Itzocan, and thence by way of Cuauhquechollan entered the valley of Atlixco. The Tlaxcalans, aware of the route the Mexicans were taking, determined to forestall them and fall upon them before they could unite with the Huexotzincas. So sudden was their assault that the Mexicans were defeated, and the Tlaxcalans, taking advantage of their disorder, made a great slaughter of them. Among the dead fell the prince-general himself, to whom perhaps so important an office had been given rather to add this honor to the splendor of his birth than out of regard for his skill in the art of war. The rest of the army took to flight, and the victors, laden with spoils, returned to Tlaxcala. It is surprising that they did not fall immediately upon the city of Huexotzinco, when they might have hoped that it would at once surrender; but perhaps their victory was not so complete as to leave them without the loss of many of their own in the battle, and they thought it better to enjoy then the fruits of the victory, to return afterward to the war with greater forces. In fact they returned; but they were repulsed by the Huexotzincas, who had meanwhile fortified themselves, and went back to Tlaxcala without

any

(*) The city of Huexotzinco was not then where it is at present, but farther up, toward the west. [p. 280] any other advantage than that of laying waste the fields of Bk. V. Huexotzinco and of Cholula; whereby those peoples were brought to

such a necessity that they were compelled to seek provisions in

Mexico and in other places.

As for King Moctezuma, he felt an inexpressible grief at the death

of his eldest son and at the defeat of his army; therefore, to avenge

himself, he had another army immediately made ready in the prov‐

inces surrounding Tlaxcala, to blockade the whole republic; but the

Tlaxcalans, well foreseeing the hostility of the Mexicans, had fortified

themselves extraordinarily and had increased the garrisons every‐

where. There was vigorous fighting on the one side and on the other;

but at last the royal troops were repulsed, leaving no little wealth in

the hands of their enemies. The republic celebrated this victory with

great rejoicings, and rewarded the Otomis, to whom it was chiefly

due, raising the most distinguished of them to the dignity of Texctli,

which was held among them in the highest esteem, and giving to the

chiefs of that nation as wives some daughters of the noblest

Tlaxcalans.

There is no doubt that if the kings of Mexico had engaged serious‐

ly against the Tlaxcalans, they would at length have subjected them to

the crown; for however great the forces of the republic, however sea‐

soned its troops, and however strong its places, it was nevertheless by

far inferior in power and in strength to the Mexicans. Hence what

the historians affirm seems to me quite probable, that the kings of

Mexico purposely left the republic of Tlaxcala in existence, scarcely

sixty miles distant from that capital, having conquered the more dis‐

tant provinces, both so that the Mexican troops might have some‐

where to exercise their courage, and also, and chiefly, to have a place

where they could easily procure victims for their sacrifices. Both of

these they obtained in the frequent assaults that they made on the

places of Tlaxcala.

Among the Tlaxcalan victims, one very memorable in the Mexican

histories

was [p. 281] was a most famous general called Tlahuicole, (e) in whom it was not known which was greater, the courage or the surprising strength of Bk. V. his body. The Maquahuitl, or Mexican sword, with which he fought was so heavy that a man of ordinary strength could hardly lift it from § 6. the ground. His name was the terror of the enemies of the republic, Tlahuicole, celebrat‐

ed general of the and wherever he appeared with his weapon, all fled. This man, then, Tlaxcalans. in an assault that the Huexotzincas made on a garrison of Otomis, rashly placed himself, in the greatest heat of the fray, in a marshy place, where, not being able to move as nimbly as he wished, he was taken prisoner, shut up in a strong cage, and then carried to Mexico and presented to Moctezuma. This king, who knew how to esteem merit even in his enemies, instead of putting him to death generously granted him the liberty of returning to his native land; but the arrogant Tlaxcalan would not accept the favor, on the pretext that, having been taken prisoner, he had not the heart to present himself with such ignominy to his countrymen. He said that he wished to die, like the other prisoners, in honor of their gods. Moctezuma, seeing him so reluctant to return to his native land, and not wishing besides to deprive the world of so celebrated a man, kept him at court in the hope of making him a friend of the Mexicans and of making use of him for the benefit of the crown. Meanwhile war broke out with the Michoacans, the cause and circumstances of which we do not know at all, and Moctezuma committed to Tlahuicole himself the command of the army that he sent to Tlaximaloyan, the frontier, as we have already said, of the kingdom of Michoacán. Tlahuicole responded advantageously to the confidence placed in him; for although he could not dislodge the Michoacans from the place where they had fortified themselves, he nevertheless took many of them prisoner, and took from them a good quantity of gold and silver. Moctezuma held in esteem

History of Mexico, Vol. I.                          Nn                            his

(e) The event of Tlahuicole occurred probably in the last years of Moctezuma’s reign; but because of its relation to the war of Tlaxcala, it seemed to us fitting to anticipate it. [p. 282] his service, and again granted him liberty; but he refusing it as before,

Bk. V. he offered him the illustrious office of Tlacatecatl, or general of the

Mexican forces. To this the Tlaxcalan replied courageously that he

would not be a traitor to his country, and that he wished absolutely to

die, provided it were in the gladiatorial sacrifice, which, being des‐

tined for the most distinguished prisoners, would be more honorable

for him. This celebrated general remained three years in Mexico with

one of his wives, who had gone there from Tlaxcala to live with him.
